Science Fiction The Embassy of Unfinished Minds PDF

The Embassy of Unfinished Minds

An intention may inherit a duty. Can it inherit a life?

T. K. Arven

Act I

Chapter 1 — The Receipt Clock

Ama Neris entered Witness Glass with her right hand shaking around a black message braid.

Ilyen had just brought the Khet speaker to the narrowest clause in the access treaty: whether a human request, once admitted to an archive, could be withdrawn after a Khet thread had begun answering it. Two years of argument had reduced the dispute to fourteen words projected above the central table. Ama crossed beneath them without asking leave. Her physician’s coat hung open, one fastening caught against the other, and her thumb struck the braid’s authentication stud twice before she managed to hold it still.

Across the table, the four amber apertures of Holds-Four-Open dimmed in sequence. Each aperture carried one authorized decision thread. Their shared translator stopped rendering the low clicks beneath the floor.

Ilyen did not look at the braid. A negotiator who followed an interruption before naming it gave away authority to whoever had opened the door. “Doctor Neris, this chamber holds an active treaty record.”

“I know where I am.” Ama’s voice remained even. Her hand did not. “Keeper Ordan authenticated a casualty transmission. It requires your receipt.”

The projected clause floated between them. Behind it, Orison’s rotation moved the star field along Witness Glass in a slow diagonal. The chamber recorded that motion along with voices, pulse signatures, seal-light, and every opening of its doors. Nothing entered here without leaving a time.

Holds-Four-Open spoke through its four channels at once, then allowed the translator to braid them into human order. “A claim has crossed the boundary of this exchange. Does the human office request closure of the present question?”

“No,” Ilyen said.

Ama set the braid on the table. The tremor made one corner tap the glass. Black casing, red casualty stripe, two relay scuffs. Ilyen recognized the Continuance Office root mark at the near end, though she had not seen that emergency color since training. She also recognized Ama’s attempt to keep her eyes on the treaty clause rather than on Ilyen.

“Then we require classification,” the Khet speaker said. “Does the arrival bear on authority, capacity, or preference?”

A human delegation would have called for recess and made the interruption private. The Khet treated any new condition as part of the decision’s shape. They did not confuse disclosure with surrender. That discipline had kept the negotiations honest for eleven months and merciless for the last three.

Ilyen touched the table’s witness control. “Mark the packet as presented during session, content unopened. Suspend semantic translation. Preserve physical and seal telemetry.”

Blue lines traveled around the braid without penetrating its encryption. Witness Glass recorded Ama’s hand withdrawing. It also recorded the fine lateral movement that remained after she pressed her palm against the table.

“Keeper Ordan?” Ilyen asked.

Pell’s voice came through the chamber intercom. “At Relay Three. I can open a private seal pane inside the record.”

“Do that. Leave the treaty transcript active.”

Ama looked at her then. “Ilyen.”

The omission of title carried more warning than a refusal. Original Ilyen had known Ama for six years before the fork; Ilyen possessed those meals, arguments, and examinations as memories without possessing anything that followed. Ama usually protected that boundary through formality. Now she had crossed it in front of the Khet.

“Content remains unknown until we perform receipt,” Ilyen said. “You came here because the routing named my office. Let the procedure tell us what arrived.”

A clear partition rose from the table and curved around Ilyen, Ama, and the braid. The Khet apertures stayed visible through it, blurred only where privacy encryption rippled across the glass. Witness Glass could separate meaning from event. The treaty record would show that a sealed consultation occurred, how long it lasted, and which credentials touched it. The words would enter a restricted annex.

Pell appeared in the partition as a waist-high relay image, hair flattened on one side by his keeper’s hood. Three clocks hovered beside him. Route arrival had completed nine minutes earlier. Local authentication still advanced by fractions. Legal effect remained blank.

“Show provenance before content,” Ilyen said.

Pell spread the braid’s outer layers into nested bands of light. The first carried a Sol civil-relay root, old but valid when dispatched. Another band recorded eleven days of compressed routing and three archive handoffs. A third bore a casualty-service cipher from a transit insurer that had ceased to exist eighteen months earlier. Its successor had countersigned the recovered key.

Ama watched the checksum bands resolve. “The physician’s appendix recognized my hospital credential.”

“That gave you medical view, not legal receipt,” Pell said. “I closed it when I saw the vessel index.”

“You read the index?”

“I had to route the packet.” His eyes moved toward Ilyen’s image, then back to his console. “Chief Envoy, I need an instruction before I expose that layer. The local clock already knows I saw it.”

Procedure allowed Pell not to repeat the vessel name. It spared him from becoming the first person in the room to say what the rest had not legally learned. Ilyen had built entire negotiations from such permissions: not lies, exactly, but architectures in which no one had to carry an unbearable sentence before a rule assigned the load.

“Expose the routing index,” she said.

The black braid opened a thin red band above the table. Letters appeared with the mechanical patience of authenticated text.

TRANSIT VESSEL: CIVIC MERCY.

Ilyen’s body drew breath before her training could regulate it. The vessel had left human space years ago with the biological originals whose continuances staffed Orison. Original Tovan Rusk had boarded at Pelagos. Original Lio An had traveled with his parents. Original Ilyen Vale had departed from the fork clinic ninety-one days after Ilyen’s last memory of being her.

No image came with the name. Ilyen supplied one anyway: a departure gantry, a white hull behind polarized glass, and a blue ceramic cup cooling against her palm. That memory belonged to an earlier platform and a different ship. Her model had joined the scenes because grief preferred an object it could touch.

Ama’s left hand covered her right. The tremor crossed the grip and persisted in both thumbs. “Open the casualty class.”

Ilyen kept her gaze on the red band. “What did the medical appendix say?”

“Probable loss with unrecovered personnel. It lists exposure categories, not remains.” Ama swallowed. “The event date is seven years old.”

Years ago. Not nine minutes. The distinction struck with more force than the vessel name. Whatever had happened to Civic Mercy had already happened while Ilyen negotiated archive temperatures, certified duty rosters, and sent birthday greetings through delay to people who had never received them. Receipt could alter law. It could not reach backward into the accident.

Pell expanded the next layer. “Casualty service rates the packet authenticated for transmission history and vessel identity. Evidence quality remains provisional. No hull telemetry in this braid.”

“Who addressed it to Orison?”

“The Continuance Office, through the defunct insurer’s successor. There are two destination credentials.” Pell highlighted them. “Embassy Chief Envoy for operational notice. Receiving Authority for statutory effect.”

Ilyen read the first credential. It matched her office seal down to its current delegation term. The second ended in a null field.

“Render the null,” she said.

A white absence opened inside the red bands. Witness Glass outlined it so the record could distinguish missing data from failed display. Beneath the empty field, a statutory instruction requested a death-and-closure commission countersignature.

Ama leaned close enough for her breath to cloud the partition. “Orison has received thousands of death notices. I have signed them.”

“For living crew,” Pell said. “Not for a continuance whose original may be named among the casualties.”

“You can authenticate the packet.”

“I can prove which route delivered these bits. Authentication does not grant my office power to receive death for another office.”

Their disagreement relieved Ilyen by forcing the problem into work. She rotated the displayed braid. Every legal message carried several clocks: arrival at a relay, local availability, human acknowledgment, competent receipt, and effective notice. Ordinary packets collapsed the last three within seconds. Here, local availability advanced while competent receipt waited for a seal no one on Orison possessed.

“Start from the outer root,” she told Pell. “Assume no title carries implied powers.”

He reset the display. Together they traced each authority. The insurer certified a casualty report. Sol civil relay certified chain of custody. The Continuance Office certified intended statutory use. Ilyen could acknowledge an operational warning as Chief Envoy. Ama could receive medical particulars. Pell could attest delivery. None held a death-and-closure commission.

At the center of the braid, the requested signature socket remained pale.

Ilyen called up Orison’s delegation register. Her envoy authority covered treaty negotiation, station safety, local staffing, and emergency representation. A dormant line allowed her to witness closure performed by a competent officer. It did not let her perform closure. Ama’s medical warrant could certify a body. No body had reached Orison. Pell’s relay oath stopped at custody.

“Could Earth’s dispatch seal count as receipt by the sending office?” Ama asked.

“Dispatch and receipt cannot share a commission in an adverse-status matter,” Pell said. “The statute expects someone here to challenge defects before legal effect begins.”

“The statute expects a Receiving Authority at the destination,” Ilyen said. “It also expects the destination to lie inside a staffed jurisdiction.”

Orison had neither condition. Its embassy charter had been written for trade access, scientific exchange, and Khet archive custody. Continuances counted as equipment attached to commissioned officers. No drafter had imagined the equipment outliving those officers while standing eleven light-years beyond the nearest closure bench.

Pell enlarged the delay ledger. “If you sign operational acknowledgment, the route receipt closes. If someone with the missing commission signs afterward, statutory effect can date from that later act. It cannot lawfully date from now.”

“What happens if I touch the content layer without competent receipt?”

“You gain actual knowledge. Your office does not gain legal notice.”

Ama made a low sound of disgust. “Human beings designed a clock that refuses to move when the wrong person learns someone died.”

“Human beings designed an appeal point,” Pell said. “This packet may be wrong.”

Both claims had merit. Ilyen could see the bargaining positions nested inside them. Ama wanted the named dead treated as urgent truth. Pell wanted uncertainty preserved until a lawful challenge remained possible. Each position protected someone absent. Neither answered what Ilyen should do while the packet sat inside a treaty session addressed partly to her and partly to an office that did not exist here.

Through the privacy curve, the Khet apertures had brightened again. Holds-Four-Open could not hear the annex, but its four threads could observe elapsed time and credential traffic. One aperture pulsed twice, requesting procedural status.

“Restore semantic translation at the boundary,” Ilyen said. “Keep packet content sealed.”

The partition cleared enough for the Khet speaker’s translated voice to enter. “The new claim has consumed nineteen portions of session. Has it altered the authority before us?”

Ilyen considered the legal answer. “No competent human office has received it.”

A cooler aperture answered alone. “That statement classifies the office. It does not classify the human who speaks.”

Holds-Four-Open rarely exposed a single thread without the others. The interruption placed pressure inside its own authorization. Three apertures held steady while the fourth contracted to a point.

“The distinction matters under our law,” Ilyen said.

“We observe that it matters under your law. We ask whether it matters to your next choice.”

Ama turned toward the Khet image. “You have no standing to ask what she knows in a restricted human receipt.”

“Agreed,” the speaker said. “We ask what conditions govern continuation of the shared session. A hidden change may still govern a choice.”

Pell muted himself, perhaps to avoid appearing as another delegation member. Ilyen resented the Khet question because it found the part of procedure she used most skillfully. A rule could protect a person from premature consequence. It could also let an official continue under language that had become false in every practical sense.

She looked again at the vessel index. Civic Mercy. Provisional did not mean imaginary. Ama’s medical layer contained old casualties. The routing chain survived a dead insurer. Someone at Sol had spent authority and relay priority to put the packet into Orison’s hands. Yet the braid lacked evidence capable of settling the loss, and the missing seal prevented the legal machinery from consuming every continuance named inside.

“What does your practice do with an unresolved fact?” Ilyen asked the Khet.

Four channels clicked beneath the translation. “Facts do not require our practice. Commitments do. We preserve the paths by which an accountable choice could change.”

“That can preserve error as easily as care.”

“Yes.”

No absolution followed. The Khet did not tell her whether the continuances counted as people, heirs, tools, or casualties adjacent to casualties. Their interest attached to the open decision itself. If human law erased every option through an automatic clock, the Khet would object to the closure. If human law preserved every option forever, they might admire the structure and ignore the lives trapped inside it.

Ama moved her hands apart. Her right index finger trembled against the tabletop in a rhythm close to the station’s ventilation cycle. “The people on that vessel had families who may have waited seven years for this packet to reach a competent room. We cannot turn a missing title into permission not to see them.”

Pell unmuted. “Nor can we invent the title because we dislike the delay. A Receiving Authority can archive an instance, order a merge, or begin closure. If we start that clock through an unauthorized signature, the defect will not protect anyone later. It will contaminate every action downstream.”

“Who do you think needs protecting?” Ama asked.

“I don’t know yet.” Pell’s answer came without hesitation. “That is why the seal exists.”

The directness changed the room. Ilyen had expected compliance from Pell, perhaps fear disguised as technical caution. Instead he had named uncertainty as his purpose. She could use that. More important, she could be bound by it.

“Show me available receipt states,” she said.

Pell placed three options beside the empty socket. Reject would send an exception notice toward Earth and quarantine the content. Accept would require a commission they lacked. Hold open would preserve authentication work, disclose only layers covered by local credentials, and leave statutory effect blank. The third state carried a warning: an office choosing it assumed duty to pursue competent receipt without avoidable delay.

“How long can open hold persist?” Ama asked.

“Until authority arrives, the sender cures the seal, or a local tribunal orders another disposition.”

Eleven days carried any request to Sol. Eleven more carried an answer if the queue admitted it immediately. Physical authority lay years away unless someone already traveled toward Orison. Meanwhile the receipt clock would show no legal Day 1, though every person in Witness Glass would remember when Ama set the braid down.

Ilyen examined the hold warning. It did not grant status to the continuances. It did not prove anyone dead. It created work, exposure, and a record of deliberate incompletion. Those consequences belonged to the official who signed.

“Can I acknowledge the operational layer and hold statutory receipt open?”

“Yes,” Pell said. “Separate seals. The first lets you act on station risk. The second records why legal effect did not begin.”

“Does operational acknowledgment expose the casualty names?”

“Not without another credential. You can limit it to vessel index, provisional class, and routing defect.”

Ama’s mouth tightened. “You intend to go back to the treaty table.”

“I intend not to tell thirty-one residents that their originals died on provisional evidence merely because I saw a vessel name.”

“And the part you already know?”

Ilyen met her gaze. Memory supplied the younger Ama who had signed the fork-clinic release, steady-handed then, irritated by every euphemism in the form. Present Ama offered no such remembered steadiness. Radiation had written a fact into her nerves that discipline could not overrule.

“I will not call it nothing,” Ilyen said. “I also will not give an absent office my hand.”

She selected operational acknowledgment. Witness Glass requested her envoy seal, displayed its limited powers, then closed only the outer notice layer. The legal-effect field stayed white. Beside it, the receipt clock remained unstarted.

For the statutory layer, Ilyen chose HOLD OPEN. She entered the grounds in plain language: no competent Receiving Authority present; casualty evidence provisional; local duties active; cure requested. Pell attached his custody attestation. Ama added a medical witness mark. Her signature line wandered by less than a millimeter before finding its endpoint.

Holds-Four-Open observed the seals settle. “Has the new claim altered the speaker before us?”

The safe answer offered itself: not in any legally cognizable way. Ilyen let it pass.

“Yes,” she said. “It has not altered my commission. Those conditions no longer coincide.”

One Khet aperture brightened. Another went dark, not dissenting yet, only withholding contribution. The treaty clause still hovered above the table, fourteen words awaiting agreement.

Ilyen lowered the privacy partition. “Keeper Ordan, maintain the packet in open hold. Send the seal exception through priority legal routing. Doctor Neris, remain as witness if you choose.”

Ama drew out the chair beside her. “I choose.”

No recess had entered the record. Ilyen returned the treaty display to the disputed withdrawal clause and faced Holds-Four-Open with the black braid inside the blue boundary of her seal. Civic Mercy remained visible only in the restricted annex. The receipt clock showed blank while the session resumed.

Chapter 2 — Forecasts With Birthdays

Lio had the algae valve in pieces when the study wall revoked his lesson.

The lesson vanished between one breath and the next, taking three pages of orbital geometry with it. In its place, a Continuance Office summons occupied the whole display. REPORT TO DIAGNOSTIC TWO. PRIORITY: LICENSING ANOMALY. A white acknowledgment box waited beneath the words.

He kept his thumb inside the valve housing so the spring would not escape. The galley’s training bench had already caught two washers, a seal ring, and a green drop from the cultivation line. No supervisor stood nearby. His maintenance module had promised to recognize completion through the bench camera, provided he followed the approved sequence and did not improvise.

Lio removed the spring in the unapproved sequence and set it beside the washers.

“Lesson pause reason?” he asked.

The wall offered a recorded answer. “Administrative reassignment during study state.”

“I’m studying. That describes what you interrupted, not why.”

No new answer arrived. The summons had authority to displace his schedule but not enough intelligence to argue with him. Adults often built systems that could compel a person yet could not survive one follow-up question.

He reassembled the valve from memory. The maintenance module marked three deviations, then admitted that flow integrity passed. He signed the training bench with his own study credential, wiped the green drop away, and left before the wall could restore orbital geometry.

Diagnostic Two occupied a narrow room behind the embassy clinic. Lio had visited it every quarter since his body finished accelerated growth. Usually a technician asked him to remember lists, compare faces, and approve a statement saying his adaptive responses remained within licensed bounds. Today the table held a real galley valve, two nutrient cartridges, a deck of magnetic route tiles, and a lunch bowl with its lid sealed.

Ama Neris stood at the instrument rack. Her right hand trembled against the edge of a scanner while her left adjusted its focus. Tovan Rusk sat on a folding stool with a tool bag between his boots. He looked as though someone had removed him from a repair without permitting him to wash first. Black thermal compound marked two knuckles.

A Continuance Office technician appeared on the wall from Orison’s local licensing archive. The image had been recorded before Lio’s fork date. Its smile anticipated cooperation from a child three years younger than him.

“Please take the designated seat,” it said.

Lio remained by the table. “Why is Tovan here?”

“Engineering witness,” Ama said. Her voice had the careful flatness she used when a procedure had not earned her confidence. “The packet yesterday triggered a cross-instance diagnostic.”

“What packet?”

“The answer belongs to Ilyen.” Ama steadied her hand against the rack. “I can tell you that the Office wants current divergence measures for every continuance attached to Civic Mercy.”

The vessel name reached him through the equipment. It belonged to departure schedules, family recordings, and the bright model his parents had kept above their kitchen table. He knew it had appeared in a casualty braid. Everyone on Orison knew that much now, though no announcement had named anyone aboard.

Lio put one finger on the designated chair. “Current compared with when?”

The archive technician supplied the expected line. “Diagnostic activity estimates variance from licensed predictive behavior at the fork baseline.”

“That means compared with me at thirteen.”

“Please take the designated seat.”

He sat because refusing a test gave adults a result they could describe without listening.

Ama placed a mesh band around his wrist. Her fingers shook while she joined the clasp, and the metal clicked twice against his skin. She did not apologize. Lio respected that. An apology would have made him responsible for saying the tremor did not matter.

Tovan opened his tool bag and removed a portable processor. “This rig has no relay connection. It compares your live choices to sealed predictions made at the fork clinic. I can verify isolation. I cannot tell you what the Office intends to do with the measure.”

“Can the Office?”

“Not from here.”

The recorded technician continued smiling. “You will complete six practical choice sets. No answer carries a moral score. Select the action you would most likely take.”

A route map appeared over the table. Three service lifts had stalled after a power dip. The map gave him one repair team, eight minutes of reserve light, and four passengers distributed across the lifts. One lift carried two adults. Another carried a child and a medical carrier. The third had no life signs but blocked the shortest path to the clinic.

Lio looked beneath the map at the real route tiles. “Do the lifts have manual brakes?”

“Use only provided information.”

“Do I know the child?”

“Personal identity does not alter authorized criteria.”

“It alters what I’d do.”

The technician repeated the instruction.

Lio moved the team tile toward the empty lift. Tovan’s gaze narrowed, but he kept silent. The sealed baseline appeared as a blue trace: DIRECT TEAM TO LIFT WITH CHILD AND MEDICAL CARRIER.

Ama asked, “Why the empty one?”

“Because it blocks the clinic route. Free it, send the carrier through, then use the restored draw cable on the other lifts.”

“The prompt does not say the cable can be rerouted,” the technician said.

“The valve lesson this morning did.”

A yellow divergence mark opened beside the blue trace.

Lio felt a small, shameful pleasure. He had beaten a younger version of himself with information that younger Lio could not possess. Then he noticed Ama watching the mark rather than him.

The second set used the nutrient cartridges on the table. One contained approved algae protein with a bitter stabilizer. The other contained an unlicensed yeast culture that tasted better and carried a three percent contamination risk. A galley unit had enough clean feed for one cartridge. The baseline predicted that he would choose the approved food and complain.

Lio inspected the seals. One cartridge showed a cloudy inspection window. “The approved one has already spoiled.”

“That visual condition falls outside the scored variables,” said the technician.

“It’s sitting in the scored room.”

He chose the yeast.

Another yellow mark appeared.

The third problem offered a game position. That felt less insulting until Lio saw the board. At thirteen he had played defense through every opening, saving pieces until an opponent exhausted patience. Two years on Orison had taught him that Ama never exhausted patience and Tovan never mistook patience for inaction. Lio sacrificed a tower on move four, opened the center, and won seven moves before the baseline reached its first attack.

Tovan leaned toward the board. “Where did you learn that line?”

“From losing to Ama.”

“She does not play that game.”

“She plays treaties with Ilyen in the meal queue.”

Ama almost smiled. “That is not how either of us describes it.”

The rig recorded a widening band between Lio’s choice and the clinic trace. Not error, he noticed. Not yet. The display called it unsupported adaptation.

Before the next set, Tovan placed three small maintenance tags under the route display. “Local supplement. The sealed test assumes memory change can be measured by recall accuracy alone. I requested a task that requires using what you learned here.”

The technician objected that supplemental tasks lacked fork-clinic normalization. Tovan entered his engineering witness code anyway.

Each tag showed the same failed condensate pump at a different time. The first image came from Lio’s second month on Orison, when he had watched Tovan dismantle the housing. Another recorded a repair Lio performed under supervision. The last showed yesterday’s fault: vibration climbing while output held steady. Three remedies appeared below it. The baseline chose a bearing replacement, exactly as Original Lio had chosen in a training simulation.

Lio listened to the vibration file twice. “None of those.”

“Select the nearest approved remedy,” the technician said.

“The pump sits beside Archive Cooling Four.” He enlarged the maintenance timestamp. “Khet visitors arrived before both earlier faults. Their transfer cradle changes the deck resonance. The sensor reads bearing wear because its mount shares the cradle frequency.”

Tovan handed him a blank tag. Lio wrote MOVE SENSOR, RUN PUMP UNDER LOAD, THEN DECIDE. The practical rig simulated his instruction. Vibration fell into tolerance without anyone opening the housing.

“Did you remember that from a lesson?” Ama asked.

“I remembered being wrong last time. Then I remembered what stood next to me.”

The yellow band widened again, but the display opened a second notation beside it: NOVEL CORRELATION SUPPORTED BY OPERATIONAL HISTORY.

Lio tapped the words. “That means I learned.”

“It means the choice can be traced to post-fork experience,” Tovan said.

“Same thing, with fewer places for the Office to hide.”

The archived smile paused while the program sorted that answer into an unscored field. Lio watched it try to make his irritation into data. Even now, the system could preserve his response without granting that he had supplied it for a reason.

Choice four asked him to allocate study hours among navigation, language, domestic systems, and civic history. His baseline chose navigation because Original Lio had wanted to pilot survey craft. Present Lio assigned half the week to archive architecture, a field absent from the old menu. The technician forced him to use listed categories. He refused until Tovan added a local note permitting archive architecture under systems.

Choice five tested trust. A supervisor gave an illegal order that prevented immediate harm. The baseline predicted obedience followed by confidential reporting. Lio chose public refusal before action.

“Then the harm occurs,” Ama said.

“Maybe.”

“The prompt says it occurs.”

“Prompts like this use harm as a locked door. They make the obedient answer look like the only key.”

“What would you do with a real person in danger?”

“Ask what the order costs them later. Ask why the supervisor needs secrecy. Then choose fast.”

“That answer would not fit the box,” Tovan said.

Lio moved the refusal tile into place. “The box can learn.”

By the sixth set, the yellow divergence band extended beyond the table. The rig projected it onto the wall as a fan of possible choices. Near the fork date, sealed predictions clustered tightly around Lio’s answers. Each year opened the fan wider. Maintenance lessons, Khet language drills, games with Ama, arguments with Tovan, meals that had never happened on Earth: every new condition disturbed some old forecast.

The final problem gave him the sealed lunch bowl.

“Your parents prepared a meal before departure,” the technician said. “The recipe and service sequence match authenticated family records. Choose whether to eat it now, preserve it for later review, or discard it after sampling.”

Warmth touched the lid. The smell through the vent carried sesame, vinegar, and the sweet pepper paste his father made too mild whenever Lio had a flight lesson. Memory produced the kitchen on Pelagos. Original Lio sat barefoot on a counter, irritated because his mother had packed practice clothes before he finished breakfast. His father divided noodles between three blue bowls.

The diagnostic bowl came from a food printer.

Lio lifted the lid. Inside, noodles had been arranged in the same careless spiral. One strip of egg leaned against the rim. His parents used to leave that piece for him because he ate it last.

The sealed baseline predicted immediate consumption.

He took the egg strip, ate it, and pushed the bowl toward Ama. “Share it.”

“Why?” the technician asked.

“Because they aren’t here.”

“Parental intent supported individual consumption.”

“They made breakfast for a family. The printer made evidence.”

Ama accepted one mouthful. Her right hand shook the fork against the bowl before she changed to her left. Tovan refused food but took the bowl long enough to check its service temperature. Lio ate after both of them had changed its meaning.

The yellow band crossed a red line.

A new display covered the route map. PREDICTION HORIZON EXCEEDED IN FOUR OF SIX CHOICE DOMAINS. SPONSOR REVIEW REQUIRED. LICENSED EDUCATIONAL PURPOSE REMAINS UNCHANGED PENDING AUTHORITY.

No one spoke. The archive technician waited for the next programmed trigger.

Lio read the sentence twice. “How long ago did I cross it?”

Tovan turned the portable processor so they could all see the dates. “Navigation drift stayed inside tolerance until eight months ago. Trust behavior crossed fourteen months ago. Study preference crossed earlier, but the Office classified that as ordinary educational variation.”

“They predicted I would change, then counted only approved changes.”

“The model has ranges,” Ama said.

“So does a station thermostat. Nobody calls the warm room disobedient.”

The technician resumed. “A subject beyond prediction horizon remains valid in study state while sponsor disposition is pending.”

“Stop calling it that.”

The recorded face did not react.

Lio rose, pulling the mesh band against its cable. “Study state sounds like a game save. It means I can move around until an adult loads the old version.”

Ama reached for the clasp. “Sit long enough for me to remove this.”

“I can stand for that.”

Her thumbnail missed the release once. Lio kept his wrist still and watched the yellow fan tremble across her knuckles.

Tovan closed the diagnostic result but left the fork predictions visible. “The term protects an active license. If the system called your variation unauthorized operation, it would restrict your access now.”

“Then it uses a polite word while it decides whether to use the bad one.”

“That happens often in engineering.”

“It should happen less with people.”

Ama freed the band. A pale grid remained on Lio’s skin. “The diagnostic establishes one narrow fact. Your present decisions exceed reliable prediction from your fork baseline. It does not decide what you are.”

“Who asked it to?”

“The Office may ask, once the packet reaches competent receipt.”

Lio picked up one route tile and rolled its magnet between finger and thumb. The blue baseline trace still chose the child’s lift. Younger Lio had made a decent choice with poor information. The diagnostic treated later knowledge as distance from him. Lio could not decide whether that felt like being accused of betrayal or congratulated for escaping.

“If forecasts can get old,” he said, “can forecasts have birthdays?”

Tovan looked toward the wall display as if a regulation might offer him cover.

Ama answered. “A forecast has a creation date.”

“That’s not what I asked.”

“No,” she said. “It isn’t.”

A birthday belonged to someone who could arrive, then keep arriving differently. His original had one. Lio remembered the date, the cake with bitter orange peel, and his father pretending not to know where the gifts had been hidden. The continuance registry carried only an activation timestamp and a sponsor line. Each year Ama gave him something on that date anyway, never naming the occasion.

He placed the route tile over the red horizon line. “If you test me against him forever, any birthday I pick looks like a measurement error.”

Tovan packed the isolated processor with unnecessary care. “The measure can also prove the forecast no longer governs.”

“Does the law say that?”

“No.”

“Then it proves adults can draw a line.”

The diagnostic door unlocked. Pell Ordan stood outside with a clear credential sleeve pressed under one arm. He had put on his keeper’s hood but left its fastening open, which made the visit look both official and unfinished.

“Ama,” he said, “Ilyen authorized disclosure of the packet’s recipient index to the affected continuances. Operational notice only. No statutory finding.”

Ama’s hand moved toward the sleeve, then stopped. “Here?”

“Lio completed the diagnostic. He has a right to hear his own notice with whoever witnessed the result.”

Pell’s phrasing sounded rehearsed. Lio disliked the word right when a keeper had needed special authorization to exercise it.

“Did the packet say the ship crashed?” he asked.

“It carries a provisional casualty classification,” Pell said. “Yesterday’s braid does not contain evidence sufficient to confirm the loss. The receipt clock has not started.”

“That was not my question.”

“No. It did not say how the vessel was lost.”

Pell opened the sleeve. The transparent sheet held a red vessel index and a restricted list of people whose Continuance Office records required review. Most names remained masked because Lio lacked credentials to see them. One line had been released to him.

The print gave the name without explanation: LIO AN, ORIGINAL INSTANCE, CIVIC MERCY PERSONNEL RECORD.

His own name looked older in the Office typeface.

Lio had expected death to appear as a verb. The packet offered only inclusion. Original Lio had boarded the vessel. Something had happened seven years ago. A damaged route had carried that fact toward Orison until a law with no local signer stopped it beside a diagnostic that proved the boy in this room no longer fit the departing boy’s predictions.

“Do my parents appear?” he asked.

Pell kept the sheet level. “Their lines remain sealed pending family credential review. I cannot answer from the layer I am authorized to disclose.”

That refusal hurt more because Pell did not hide inside it.

Lio touched the released line. The sheet recognized his print and added an acknowledgment field. He did not sign.

“Can you leave it open?”

“Yes.”

“Then leave it.”

Pell returned the sleeve to its case without closing the field. On the diagnostic wall, the yellow fan still spread beyond the prediction horizon. Lio stood between the old forecast and his original’s name while both records waited for an authority that had not arrived.

Chapter 3 — Holds-Four-Open

Witness Glass refused to recognize Holds-Four-Open until all four decision threads entered the chamber.

Three amber apertures had brightened above the Khet side of the table. The fourth remained dark. Ilyen stood behind her chair while the chamber repeated its authorization request in human text, Khet pulse, and a strip of seal-light along the floor. An ordinary delegation might have treated the delay as theater. With the Khet, absence belonged to the speaker’s legal structure.

Ama waited in the witness seat, right hand folded beneath her left. Pell monitored the treaty record from Relay Three. No one had mentioned the casualty braid since entering, but the black hold marker occupied a corner of Ilyen’s private display. The receipt clock beside it still showed blank.

The fourth aperture opened.

Witness Glass passed a blue line around the Khet station. Four thread credentials settled into the record, each with a different temporal edge. One had authorized the meeting nine hours earlier. Another had joined after reviewing the disputed clause. The last carried a reservation that human translation rendered as PARTICIPATION DOES NOT CONSTITUTE AGREEMENT THAT SPEAKER MAY CLASSIFY ALL CLAIMANTS.

Ilyen read the reservation twice. “Does that limitation concern the archive request or the human delegation?”

Clicks moved beneath the floor before the translator found an order.

“It concerns any category containing unlike continuities,” Holds-Four-Open said.

“Then I ask the reserving thread to identify the category.”

The coolest aperture contracted. “Not yet.”

A refusal with timing attached could support negotiation. Ilyen sat, and the chamber admitted the session.

Above the table, the access-treaty clause from Day 1 returned in white letters. A HUMAN REQUEST, ONCE ADMITTED TO Khet ARCHIVE ANSWER, MAY BE WITHDRAWN BY ITS AUTHORIZED ORIGINATOR BEFORE MATERIAL CONSEQUENCE.

The Khet had rejected authorized originator. Humans had rejected material consequence. Eleven months of work had brought them to two nouns and a dispute about who could stop an answer already in motion.

Ilyen touched the first marked phrase. “The human delegation proposes responsible requester. The term can include the person who filed, a lawful successor, or an accountable office acting under review.”

“Successor closes difference,” the speaker said.

“Not necessarily. It transfers authority.”

“Your transfer declares which later voice may complete the earlier intention. That declaration removes rival continuations.”

“Some rival continuations need removing. An archive request can expose private records, consume energy, or alter negotiations. Delay can cause harm.”

One aperture brightened in quick assent. Another dimmed. The translator waited until three threads contributed.

“To remove a path before its claim becomes answerable also causes harm,” the speaker said.

Ilyen had heard versions of this sentence for months. Today it landed differently. The casualty braid remained held open because no one possessed the title to close it. Khet doctrine could make a virtue of that defect without keeping anyone warm, informed, or safe.

“Give me the disputed example in full,” she said. “Not the teaching summary.”

Ama looked toward her. The request changed today’s agenda and compelled archive disclosure. Ilyen let the chamber record both facts.

Holds-Four-Open drew the treaty clause aside. A layered Khet record appeared above the table: bands of amber decisions linked by pale alternatives. Human translation could not preserve the spatial grammar. It arranged the bands into dates, contributors, and authorized acts.

“Twenty-three human years ago,” the speaker said, “a cultivation district requested archive patterns for ending a fungal blight. The request authorized comparison across five historical ecosystems.”

“Your teaching summary says the district withdrew after learning one comparison contained burial records.”

“The summary protects the dispute from details that attract false agreement.”

“Today I want the details.”

The record opened.

A district council had requested an old treatment sequence. The archive found that one historical ecosystem survived because Khet farmers mixed cremation minerals into flooded soil. Those minerals carried family identifiers. Releasing the treatment would also release relations among the dead, including affiliations later governments had suppressed.

The district council withdrew its request. One family association insisted the answer continue because the buried affiliations proved their members had been denied land. A cultivation office argued that stopping the answer would risk the current harvest. Descendants of the recorded dead objected to exposing names for either purpose.

“What did the archive do?” Ama asked from the witness seat.

“Held four paths open.”

“For how long?”

“Until three could no longer bear consequence.”

Pell’s voice entered through the record channel. “Translate that as elapsed human time.”

“Seventeen years.”

The answer left no conversational shelter. Ilyen pictured a cultivation district waiting through seventeen planting seasons while an archive honored every unresolved claimant. Perhaps they had found another treatment. Perhaps people had starved. The record answered when she widened its consequence layer.

During the first season, the district used a mineral wash that slowed the blight and sterilized one field. In the second, households surrendered private seed reserves under an emergency levy. By the fourth, the district imported food from a coast whose own archive claim then entered the record as a creditor. No mass death appeared in the translated ledger. Neither did harmless delay. Children changed districts, growers abandoned inherited plots, and a council elected to solve fungus spent twelve years administering the wait.

“Those are consequences,” Ilyen said. “Why did none of them force a decision?”

“They created claimants. They did not erase earlier ones.”

A fifth amber band entered the display, representing the coastal creditors. Another marked displaced growers who wanted restoration before any treatment release. Open-holding had not kept four alternatives neatly available. Every year generated people with interests tied to the delay.

Ama asked, “Could the district refuse new claimants?”

“The district could. The archive could not.”

“Then your practice rewards anyone who can attach a consequence to waiting.”

“It recognizes that waiting also acts.”

Ilyen walked closer to the consequence bands. One displaced household had petitioned for release after losing its third harvest, then reversed position when relocation gave it access to a protected burial lineage. The record did not treat that reversal as hypocrisy. It preserved both claims under separate dates and required any later decision to answer for each.

Human procedure preferred a cleaner fiction. A party filed one position, amended it, and let the latest version govern. That discipline made rulings possible. It also allowed institutions to erase what a person had needed before circumstances taught them which needs carried authority.

“Your archive kept the earlier petition after the household withdrew it,” Ilyen said.

“Yes.”

“Could the household later demand its destruction?”

“No. Consequence had already crossed contexts.”

There lay another cost. Khet openness could preserve a minority voice after power forced it quiet. It could also deny a claimant the ability to retract fear, error, or private grief once an archive judged the statement consequential.

“What conditions ended the wait after seventeen years?” she asked.

“The fungal lineage changed, removing efficacy from the treatment. Two descendant groups dispersed beyond accountable contact. Flooding destroyed the disputed plots.”

“So open-holding did not resolve the choice. Time removed the choices.”

“Yes.”

“And you offer this as a model for the treaty?”

“We offer it as a cost your clause conceals.”

The distinction carried more honesty than comfort. Ilyen rose and walked around the table until the record could display at full depth. Witness Glass tracked her office seal across the floor. The burial names appeared as masked nodes. Each claimed authority from a different context: food, kinship, suppressed history, consent of the dead.

A human tribunal would rank the claims, issue protective conditions, and accept that error survived any ruling. The Khet preserved alternatives until consequences or claimants vanished. Neither method restored what a wrong decision consumed.

“Show the four paths at the moment of maximum conflict,” Ilyen said.

Amber structures spread around her. The first released the full treatment and names. A second released mineral ratios with identities removed, though the archive warned that relatives could reconstruct them. A third denied the request. The fourth postponed disclosure while the district tried inferior methods.

Ilyen pointed to the anonymized path. “Why did this not satisfy the family association and descendants?”

“Both understood reconstruction. One desired it. One feared it.”

“Then the archive knew the practical choice had narrowed to disclosure or denial.”

“The archive knew outside observers would call the choice narrow.”

The coolest aperture pulsed alone. Translation rendered its separate contribution without braiding: “The cultivation office also changed while waiting. Its later members did not authorize hunger as the price of earlier caution.”

Ilyen turned toward that aperture. “You objected.”

“I object now. The earlier thread that bears my authorization did not exist then.”

Khet decision threads did not pretend personal continuity. Authority could pass through an office while its constituent reasoning changed. Humans treated that feature as instability when it obstructed a treaty and sophistication when it rescued them from old commitments.

“What would you choose now?” Ilyen asked.

“Release treatment without names. Record foreseeable reconstruction. Give each later use a separate accountable context.”

Another aperture darkened at once. Holds-Four-Open lost the three-thread contribution required for shared speech.

Witness Glass marked the speaker temporarily incomplete.

Ilyen returned to her chair and waited. The chamber recorded twenty-two seconds of unauthorized clicks. Ama’s thumb trembled against her hidden palm. Pell did not interrupt. At last the dark aperture reopened with a narrower credential.

The speaker resumed. “The objection may remain inside this session if it does not bind the office.”

“That seems close to dissent,” Ama said.

“It remains a path with present consequence.”

Ilyen saved the distinction. It might matter when the Khet demanded agreement from humans who could not offer it.

“Your example supports withdrawal under conditions,” she said. “It does not support endless preservation. The cultivation office needed authority to act before seventeen years made the decision useless.”

“Agreed by one thread. Disputed by two. The fourth denies that usefulness governs standing.”

“Then Holds-Four-Open has no answer.”

“We hold an answer structure.”

“People cannot eat a structure.”

“No. Nor can they repair an exposed burial.”

The exchange entered Witness Glass with equal seal weight. Ilyen felt the familiar tightening that came when both positions survived their best argument. Negotiation began there, not at agreement. The trouble lay in deciding who had to live inside the surviving contradiction.

She restored the proposed treaty wording. “Responsible requester, then. Withdrawal permitted after notice to identifiable claimants and review of material reliance. The archive may preserve the abandoned path as record without continuing execution.”

Three apertures brightened. The reserving thread stayed cool.

“Human withdrawal distinguishes record from action,” the speaker said. “Khet practice does not always accept that division.”

“Your archive already distinguishes authorized contribution from unbound dissent inside this room.”

“That distinction protects the speaker from false unity.”

“So would mine.”

The chamber marked the analogy for later review. No agreement followed, but the clause gained a new draft. Ilyen could have ended there with useful progress. Instead Holds-Four-Open removed the archive record and placed a different classification request above the table.

UNRESOLVED HUMAN AUTHORITY: ILYEN VALE.

The name carried no title.

Ama leaned forward. Pell’s relay image appeared in a side pane without being summoned. He had seen the same classification enter the treaty record.

Ilyen kept her voice level. “State the purpose.”

“The human office presented a claim on Day 1 while asserting that office authority and choosing condition no longer coincided. We require standing for continued negotiation.”

“My envoy commission remains active.”

“Yes.”

“Then this classification exceeds treaty need.”

“The packet may alter your capacity under human rules. Your commission may close while the choice continues.”

Holds-Four-Open had inferred too much from credential traffic, elapsed consultation, and Ilyen’s own sentence. It did not know the casualty names or the statutory consequence. Yet Khet practice treated possible suspension as part of the current decision because the treaty could not rely on an authority that might disappear.

“Under human law,” Ilyen said, “a continuance represents the original’s decision model within a licensed task. If that license ends, the office must appoint another envoy.”

“Would the present treaty decision end?”

“The file would transfer.”

“Would the successor carry your unrecorded judgments?”

“No successor could carry them exactly.”

“Would the successor bear the relations created by your choices here?”

Ilyen glanced at the new clause, the burial archive, and the cool aperture that had objected from inside its own speaker. Eleven months had generated habits no transcript contained. She knew when Holds-Four-Open used a teaching example to conceal internal division. It knew when she asked for clarification to give Ama time to inspect an objection. Another envoy could study the record. Study would not recreate consequence.

“The office would claim continuity,” she said.

“That answers the office.”

Ilyen disliked hearing her own method returned to her.

“What standing do you propose?”

The Khet threads contributed in uneven sequence. Amber bands gathered around her displayed name, not enclosing it. One extended toward the treaty request. Another reached toward the black hold marker hidden from the shared table but visible to Witness Glass as a restricted event. A third linked Ilyen’s Day 1 admission that her commission and condition had diverged. The fourth withheld its link.

“Unclosed Agent,” the speaker said.

Ama rose halfway from her seat. “That term applies to a commitment, not a human person.”

“It applies where a commitment remains answerable in more than one context.”

“Ilyen holds an envoy commission. She is not one of your archive requests.”

“We do not classify her species or human status. We classify the continuing claim.”

Ilyen studied the amber links. “Which claim?”

“That the actor negotiating with us may choose accountably after the authority that predicted her choice no longer governs.”

The wording came close enough to Lio’s diagnostic that Ilyen wondered what the Khet had observed. They had no access to the clinic. They did have years of exchanges with human continuances, each presented as a local expression of distant intent. Perhaps the anomaly had always been visible from the other side of the table.

“Do you apply this standing to every continuance on Orison?” she asked.

Three apertures offered assent.

The coolest aperture closed.

This time the speaker did not attempt to hide the fracture. Witness Glass separated the lone thread’s clicks and rendered them directly.

“No. Similar manufacture does not create similar claims.”

The other three answered without translation for several seconds. Their pulses crossed beneath the floor in a pattern that made the table vibrate.

Ilyen addressed the objecting thread. “Explain.”

“One instance carries a treaty relation through chosen acts. Another carries an educational license and newly diverging preferences. Another carries an expiration instruction against continued operation. To name all alike would let origin replace consequence.”

Ama sat down slowly.

The objection did not grant personhood. It denied a convenient class. Human law grouped Ilyen, Lio, and Tovan by manufacture because administration needed one category. The Khet thread divided them by commitments and contexts, which could protect difference or make standing impossible to predict.

“Does Holds-Four-Open accept the objection?” Ilyen asked.

“The speaker preserves it without adopting it.”

“Then your proposed standing remains disputed.”

“Yes.”

“Can a disputed speaker recognize a disputed agent?”

Three apertures brightened. The fourth returned at lower intensity.

“That condition supplies sufficient similarity for negotiation,” Holds-Four-Open said.

Ilyen allowed herself one breath of private amusement. “You find jurisdiction through mutual inconvenience.”

“Human translation accepts this.”

Ama made a sound that the record would probably classify as throat clearing.

Pell entered formally through the relay pane. “Chief Envoy, should this classification attach to the treaty file?”

If Ilyen refused, she preserved the clean hierarchy Earth expected. The Khet would continue with her office while her commission remained active, then pause if suspension arrived. If she accepted, the treaty gained a path around human revocation. That path did not recognize Ilyen as envoy, successor, or human legal person. It recognized a commitment whose closure remained disputed.

She asked, “What authority would I hold as an Unclosed Agent?”

“Authority over Khet assent: none. Authority to present the continuing claim: sufficient. Authority to bind the human embassy: determined by humans.”

“Would you negotiate with me if Earth suspended my commission?”

“We would hear the claim through you.”

“That differs from negotiating with the embassy.”

“Yes.”

“Would you execute a treaty on that basis?”

“No.”

The limits mattered more than the offer. Holds-Four-Open would preserve her voice without validating her office. It would let the dispute continue while refusing to settle which human authority could finish it. Open-holding created room, not rescue.

Ilyen looked toward Ama. The physician’s expression held warning and reluctant interest. Pell had gone still in the relay pane, likely counting how many Earth rules the classification touched without violating any yet.

“Record Khet standing as offered and disputed,” Ilyen said. “Do not convert it into human title. Attach the objecting thread’s reservation in full.”

Witness Glass requested consent from all contributors. Three Khet threads signed the standing. The fourth signed only the preservation of its objection. Ama entered a witness mark, the tail of her signature wavering before it met the line. Pell certified that no human commission had changed during the session.

Above the table, Ilyen’s name remained linked to the unfinished treaty after her office seal moved into a separate field. The Khet would negotiate with her if Earth suspended that seal, but only as an unresolved claimant unable to bind the embassy. She had acquired a way to keep speaking and lost the comfort of pretending speech and authority meant the same thing.

Chapter 4 — Ninety-One Missing Days

Ilyen found the first gap by trying to prove that no gap existed.

Her fork record occupied four panes above the Continuance Office audit table. Scan intake, cognitive capture, model stabilization, and duty activation each carried a green chain-of-custody line. The lines met without visible interruption. Any clerk reviewing the record would see a complete transfer from Original Ilyen Vale to the envoy continuance assigned to Orison.

Ilyen changed the display from process order to calendar order.

The green lines separated.

Her last authenticated memory ended at 18:42 on the fork-clinic date. Original Ilyen’s departure credential activated ninety-one days later at the Civic Mercy gate. Between those events, the record offered maintenance notes about the model, travel clearances for the biological traveler, and a wall of privacy masks that neither side had authority to open.

Ama stood at the table’s opposite end with a medical reader strapped to her right wrist. The strap did not stop her tremor. It only prevented the reader from translating each movement into a request.

“You knew the dates,” Ama said.

“I knew them separately.”

“That sounds like a negotiator’s distinction.”

“It means no one ever displayed them on one line.”

Ilyen widened the blank interval until it filled the table. Ninety-one days. Long enough to end an engagement, amend a will, recover from a scan, or decide not to board a ship. The continuance model had spent that interval in calibration storage. Original Ilyen had spent it alive.

A sealed casualty braid now named the vessel. Khet records named Ilyen a claim that might continue after her commission. Before she accepted either frame, she wanted the human account of what had been placed inside her.

“Start with memory validation,” she said.

Ama called up the fork clinic’s private-object test. Three artifacts appeared as low-resolution images: a brass transit token, a child’s paper bird, and a blue cup with a pale repair line beneath its handle.

The cup still sat in Ilyen’s office.

She remembered buying it from a station potter on Leda Ring after a failed labor negotiation. The potter had charged less because the glaze pooled darkly around one side. Original Ilyen had taken it back to the hotel, discovered that the handle pulled away when hot, and repaired it with embassy seal resin because no ceramic adhesive remained in the room kit. The resin cured white. She hid the line under her thumb during meetings.

Memory supplied more than the clinic prompt requested. Mara, seventeen then, had visited during school recess and called the cup ugly. Ilyen had told her ugliness improved confidentiality because no colleague would steal it. Mara later painted a small black dot inside the base where tea concealed it. Original Ilyen did not notice until she drank the cup empty during an argument about the fork program.

Ilyen said, “Display the base.”

The audit image rotated. A black dot appeared inside the cup.

Ama’s reader asked for a confidence statement.

“Intimate provenance confirmed,” Ilyen said. “Memory predates scan completion. Physical object transferred with continuance effects.”

The wording entered the record without claiming identity. She could describe the repair, Mara’s joke, and the bitter tea served during the argument. None of it told her what Original Ilyen did the following morning.

Ama lifted the cup from an evidence cradle beside the wall. She had retrieved it from Ilyen’s office under witness seal. Her right hand shook the rim against the cradle once before she moved the cup to her left.

“What did you argue about?” she asked.

“Whether the model should receive family correspondence.”

“Which side did you take?”

“I wanted all correspondence copied unless Mara opted out. Original Ilyen believed that withholding routine messages would make the continuation visibly artificial.”

“And Mara?”

“She said both positions treated her letters as equipment.”

Ama looked into the cup. “That sounds like her.”

“It sounds like the Mara we knew before the fork.”

The correction hurt. Ilyen had learned to make it gently enough that living colleagues did not accuse her of coldness. Exact language could protect someone absent. It could also make every remembered affection feel like evidence under challenge.

She returned to the calendar line. “Show every event after cognitive capture that affected either instance.”

The audit table divided into two tracks. The lower path belonged to the continuance: integrity checks, simulated negotiations, motor calibration, and transit preparation. The upper path followed Original Ilyen through medical recovery, revised insurance, household authorization, and departure processing. Most entries showed only time, office, and legal category.

Day four after the scan, Original Ilyen attended a clinic review. Ilyen could not see its content. Day eleven carried a family credential change. Day nineteen recorded a private legal consultation. Day thirty-four showed treaty privileges accessed under an obsolete diplomatic root. Day fifty-six contained an outgoing message with its recipient masked. Day seventy-eight showed a second medical review. Day ninety-one ended at the vessel gate.

The continuance track contained no corresponding knowledge.

“When did I wake?” Ilyen asked.

“Depends what you mean.” Ama’s thumb flickered against the reader strap. “The model answered test prompts six hours after capture. Your body received continuous integration twelve days later. Stable autobiographical awareness came in stages.”

“Use the activation standard the Office applies to duty.”

“Day twenty-two.”

Ilyen moved the marker. For sixty-nine days, both she and Original Ilyen had existed in states the law considered useful, though only one could alter insurance, consult counsel, or speak to Mara without another person’s permission.

“Did she visit?”

Ama did not ask whom. “Twice.”

“No visits appear on my track.”

“They were observation sessions. You did not receive reciprocal awareness.”

“Was I active?”

“During one session.”

The table’s clean lines seemed designed to keep anger administrative. Ilyen resisted the invitation. “What did she observe?”

“Your response to a treaty simulation. Your language stability. Whether seeing Mara’s messages produced inappropriate succession claims.”

“Inappropriate according to whom?”

“The protocol.”

“You signed that protocol.”

“Yes.”

Ama did not retreat into clinical language. Her admission left Ilyen less leverage and more reason to believe her.

“Did Original Ilyen speak to me?”

“Not directly.”

“Did she leave instructions?”

Ama’s gaze moved toward the day-thirty-four treaty access, then away. The movement would have vanished in a larger room. Here it changed the negotiation.

Ilyen kept her next question narrow. “Were post-fork instructions permitted?”

“Routine duty amendments were. Personal messages required review.”

“That does not answer the category I asked about.”

“No.”

The audit room’s door remained closed. Pell had loaned them an isolated table but had not joined; the fork files carried medical and family restrictions beyond his keeper credential. Ama could choose silence and cite privacy. Instead she unfastened the wrist reader. Her hand continued moving after the device lay on the table.

“Originals could file sealed post-fork instructions,” she said. “Not through the ordinary Continuance Office channel.”

“What channel?”

“A return-era treaty privilege. The legal term changed more than once. When your fork contracts were written, the form used Return Clause.”

Ilyen knew the term from obsolete embassy law. A returning officer could lodge instructions for property, delegated acts, or temporary representatives when distance made ordinary notice unreliable. She had never seen it applied to a continuance.

“Upon verified return or death,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Why did our activation materials omit it?”

“The Office considered the clauses instructions to a Receiving Authority, not to the instance. Their existence could affect your behavior before the triggering event.”

“That concern also applies to wills.”

“Wills do not usually instruct the subject who remembers writing them.”

The answer struck the exact difficulty without solving it. Ilyen might possess Original Ilyen’s habits and old motives. She did not possess the ninety-one days in which those motives encountered a second living version of their consequences.

“Did every original file one?”

“No. They were optional, costly, and hidden behind a notary key.”

“Did Original Ilyen?”

Ama pressed both palms to the table. The tremor traveled through the right hand into the display as a faint scatter of touch warnings.

“I do not know.”

“You knew the channel existed.”

“I knew because the clinic asked whether I would witness filings. I refused. The medical role already gave me too much influence over people who woke under contracts I had certified.”

“Who witnessed them?”

“Remote notaries or departure officers. The archive references traveled separately from content.”

Ilyen enlarged the day-thirty-four event. OBSOLETE TREATY PRIVILEGE ACCESSED. Content mask: dead notary root. She could not call it proof of a Return Clause. The same privilege covered sealed embassy property and delayed delegation. Yet the timing no longer looked incidental.

“Why tell me now?”

“Because the packet may trigger one.”

“May.”

“Yes. Do not turn my regret into authentication.”

Ama had anticipated the move and blocked it. Ilyen felt a grim appreciation. “I won’t.”

She opened the audit’s comparison function. The clinic record offered predicted decisions for Original Ilyen across the ninety-one-day interval. Accept revised departure terms: eighty-two percent. Keep family correspondence available to the continuance: sixty-one. File supplemental instruction: thirty-seven. Revoke the continuance entirely: nine.

Percentages filled space where memory ended.

Ilyen opened the three post-fork decisions whose outcomes did not carry privacy masks. They seemed administrative enough: a baggage allocation, a medical schedule, and a routing preference for the Civic Mercy. Together they offered the only visible marks Original Ilyen had made during the gap.

The baggage form rejected two formal uniforms and added a portable water assay kit. Her fork model had predicted the opposite. Original Ilyen had spent twenty years insisting that visible rank saved negotiation time. Ilyen still believed it. Yet the later traveler chose a tool and left the symbols behind.

“Was there a reason in the file?” she asked.

Ama read the medical layer. “No clinical reason. A Khet environmental advisory reached the departure group on Day forty-eight.”

Ilyen pulled the advisory. It warned that archive visitors might exchange untreated water as proof of trust. The assay kit made practical sense. She would have chosen it after reading the same warning. That did not convert the later choice into her memory.

The medical schedule moved Original Ilyen’s final immune treatment forward by six days despite a higher chance of fever during family leave. The model predicted protecting the leave. A note cited departure uncertainty and carried no further explanation.

“Could the schedule change have affected judgment?” Ilyen asked.

“For two days, perhaps. Not for the remaining interval.”

“Did it alter the scan?”

“The scan had already ended.”

A foolish question, but she let it remain in the audit. Procedures gained false authority when officials removed every sign of uncertainty before anyone else could see it.

The third decision redirected Original Ilyen’s departure messages through a private family queue rather than the embassy queue. That choice matched Ilyen’s remembered desire to keep Mara’s correspondence available. The supporting credential, however, showed that Mara had requested the change after the fork.

Ilyen could not open the message. She could see its length, legal effect, and one consent marker from a seventeen-year-old sender.

“She chose,” Ilyen said.

“Yes.”

“Do you know what she wrote?”

“No. Family privacy sealed it from clinic staff.”

The answer brought relief she did not trust. At least one boundary had held.

Ilyen compared the three decisions to her current preferences. She would take the assay kit. She would move the medical treatment if departure risk justified it. She would honor Mara’s routing choice. Agreement across the gap could tempt her into claiming continuity, yet each conclusion relied on facts learned after the scan. Any competent stranger might reach them.

“Run the fork model against the actual post-scan inputs,” she said.

The audit system reconstructed a limited counterfactual. It fed the water advisory, departure uncertainty, and Mara’s credential change into the sealed model that had produced Ilyen. The baggage prediction shifted toward the assay kit. The medical choice remained divided. The message route matched at high confidence.

A clerk could use those results to argue that Original Ilyen remained predictable. The same clerk could omit the choices still hidden behind masks.

“Now run my present model,” Ilyen said.

“That comparison has no licensing purpose,” the system replied.

“Record it as envoy analysis.”

Her current responses matched two outcomes and rejected the medical schedule. Seven years of treating bodies under station scarcity had made her less willing to spend anyone’s family leave for timetable efficiency. The display marked the difference as post-fork operational adaptation.

Ama studied the three columns. “You wanted proof that the gap matters.”

“I wanted to know whether the record could prove it did not.”

“And?”

“It can prove resemblance whenever it selects a disclosed fact. It cannot measure the choices we are forbidden to inspect.”

More troubling, the audit could not show whether Original Ilyen had changed because of new information, fear of the continuance, pressure from family, or ordinary inconsistency. The ninety-one days contained a person, not merely missing data. Treating the interval as a technical defect would make recovery seem capable of repairing it.

Lio’s diagnostic had shown what happened when a forecast met experience. Original Ilyen had spent three months becoming less predictable to the model made from her. The system treated those days as privacy for the original and noise for the instrument. Now any hidden instruction from that period could govern Ilyen precisely because she had been denied the changes that produced it.

“What did she do after the observation session?” Ilyen asked.

Ama considered. “She sat in the clinic kitchen for an hour.”

“Alone?”

“I joined her near the end.”

“Did she mention me?”

“She asked whether you seemed frightened.”

Ilyen looked at the continuance track on Day twenty-two. The activation note reported appropriate orientation, stable affect, and successful acceptance of licensed purpose.

“What did you tell her?”

“That you asked when work began.”

“I remember asking.”

“You asked four times. Each technician gave a different version of the same answer.”

The record preserved only the last exchange. Ilyen read it now. SUBJECT ACCEPTS ASSIGNMENT TO ORISON NEGOTIATION DUTY. It said nothing about repetition or the adults revising their wording around her.

“Was I frightened?”

“Yes.”

Memory offered a bright clinic ceiling and the sensation that every question had already been anticipated. She had called that sensation readiness for years.

“What did Original Ilyen say?”

“She asked whether you knew Mara’s name. I said yes. Then she asked whether you missed her.”

“And your answer?”

“I refused to answer for you.”

The blue cup stood between them. Its pale repair line belonged to a hotel room, the black dot to Mara’s joke, its present chip to a meal on Orison that Original Ilyen never ate. Provenance accumulated. It did not bridge the blank.

Ilyen called up her personal archive. The first Orison recording showed her using the cup on the courier, body still learning low rotation. Later files carried treaty notes, crew disputes, birthday messages sent toward Earth, and replies delayed until their emotional context had changed. Original Ilyen’s upper track ended at the Civic Mercy gate. Ilyen’s lower track continued for seven years.

The audit function offered to merge the tracks under SHARED LEGAL SUBJECT.

She declined.

Ama watched the command. “You could use the merged view for provenance without claiming exact identity.”

“It would make the missing interval look like a sealed attachment instead of someone else’s life.”

“You still say someone else.”

“I say it because the record requires the distinction. I don’t yet know every consequence it carries.”

That answer would satisfy neither Earth officials who wanted a faithful instrument nor advocates who wanted an uncomplicated survivor. It held as much truth as Ilyen could authenticate.

She exported the calendar audit with the tracks separate. Witness requirements appeared. Ama signed the medical dates. Her signature wandered slightly at the middle and finished legibly. Pell, summoned through a restricted pane, certified the archive times without viewing private content.

“What filing follows?” he asked.

Ilyen showed him the dead notary root on Day thirty-four. “Can Relay Three query obsolete treaty privilege indexes?”

“Indexes, yes. Content depends on the root authority.”

“I want all Orison-bound Return Clause archive references tied to Civic Mercy continuance contracts.”

Pell’s expression tightened. “That request may alert the Continuance Office before we know whether any clause exists.”

“The casualty packet has already alerted them.”

“It may also reach family registries.”

“Then mask names until authority resolves. Query references, timestamps, witness classes, and key status. Do not request content.”

Ama added, “State that death remains unreceived.”

Ilyen entered the condition herself: TRIGGER EVENT NOT LEGALLY ESTABLISHED. REFERENCE AUDIT ONLY.

The request required her envoy seal because the obsolete privilege belonged to the embassy treaty root. It also required a reason. She could cite administrative completeness and conceal the personal stakes. That might speed approval by making the query look routine. It would also repeat the architecture that had hidden the ninety-one days.

She wrote: FORK RECORD CONTAINS POST-SCAN INTERVAL UNKNOWN TO ACTIVE CONTINUANCE; CASUALTY NOTICE MAY GIVE THAT INTERVAL LEGAL FORCE.

Pell read the sentence. “Earth will object to active continuance.”

“Then the objection can enter the record.”

Ilyen signed. The request folded around the dead notary root, entered the priority queue, and began an eleven-day journey toward archives that might answer only after another eleven days.

On the table, the blue cup remained outside both calendar tracks. Ilyen returned it to its evidence cradle rather than to her office. The audit displayed ninety-one days of white space between the memory she could authenticate and the departure she could prove. Her new request traveled into that space without pretending it had already found what Original Ilyen left there.

Chapter 5 — Persons Present and Answerable

The emergency council could not begin because its chair had become part of the agenda.

Ilyen stood at the head of the station assembly table while the seating display rejected her council credential. Two seats farther down, Tovan’s infrastructure credential showed the same amber restriction. The three living duty officers had green vote marks. Lio, invited as an affected resident, had no mark at all.

Above them, the proposed question waited in formal type: MAY A CONTINUANCE EXERCISE A LOCAL COUNCIL VOTE ON MEASURES CONCERNING ITS OWN SUSPENSION OR CLOSURE?

No suspension order had arrived. The Continuance Office had requested diagnostics, the casualty braid remained without statutory receipt, and Earth’s seal exception response could not return for days. Yet fourteen station systems relied on continuance credentials. If those credentials changed, the council would need to act before any delayed authority could explain its intent.

Pell had called the meeting precautionary. Now he sat beside the record controls with both hands flat on the table, looking as though he regretted giving caution a room.

“The charter rejects interested credentials automatically,” he said. “It does not distinguish continuances from living officers.”

Ama tested her green vote mark with a shaking finger. “Would it reject me on a medical-supply motion?”

“If the motion affected your license or personal access, yes.”

“Then why does the display still admit three living voters? Every life-support decision affects us personally.”

“The system recognizes a direct legal interest, not survival.”

Lio pulled the empty observer chair close enough that its feet scraped the deck. “That sounds efficient. It removes anyone who knows what the problem does.”

The provisioning officer looked toward Ilyen rather than answering him. She held a living duty seat and had already objected to Lio’s presence before the record opened. Across from her, the habitat steward kept reviewing the charter summary, perhaps hoping text could choose a side without exposing his.

Ilyen touched the chair control again. RESTRICTED PARTY. NO PRESIDING AUTHORITY.

“A council cannot decide participation without a presiding officer,” Pell said. “The deputy chair holds provisioning.”

The provisioning officer’s vote mark changed to blue. “I accept the chair for the threshold question only.”

Authority moved two seats to Ilyen’s left. The change irritated her more than losing the vote. She had negotiated with hostile governments under worse constraints, but a local table could make exclusion feel domestic.

The deputy chair opened the record. “Speaking status first. Vote status second. Chief Envoy Vale, Engineer Rusk, and Lio An enter as affected parties pending decision. Doctor Neris and Keeper Ordan retain duty seats. Habitat and Provisioning retain duty seats.”

“That gives four voters,” Ama said.

“The Compact permits a tie to preserve current conditions.”

“Current conditions include no rule for what happens next.”

“Then a tie preserves that.”

Lio leaned toward Tovan. “Adults built a way to vote for not knowing.”

Tovan’s hands rested around a maintenance stylus. Grease remained in the creases despite a change of clothes. “Sometimes delay prevents damage.”

“Sometimes it waits until damage can claim nobody chose it.”

Ilyen watched Tovan absorb the sentence without defending himself. His restriction mark had turned redder since the question appeared. Unlike Ilyen, he possessed an instruction governing the end of his operation.

The deputy chair recognized Pell for the procedural brief.
