He displayed three authorities. The Continuance Office license treated post-fork learning as sponsor-owned operational state. The embassy charter assigned council seats to commissioned duties. A remote-instrument directive required local custodians to preserve instances for lawful disposition if original status became uncertain.

“Preserve,” Ama said. “Not suspend.”

“Correct. No order has reached us.” Pell enlarged the directive. “But if one does, local action may include access restriction, memory quarantine, or voluntary safe suspension pending a Receiving Authority.”

Lio raised a hand. The deputy chair ignored him because speaking status had not been granted.

Ilyen said, “I cede my affected-party time to Lio An.”

“You have not been recognized for time.”

“Then the record should show that the party with no vote attempted to share speech with the resident who has neither.”

The deputy chair’s mouth tightened. “Keeper Ordan, does the record permit a factual question from an observer?”

“If the chair requests it.”

She looked at Lio. “One question.”

He pointed at the displayed license. “What does sponsor-owned mean after the sponsor dies?”

Pell answered with care. “Original status has not been legally established.”

“I asked about the language.”

“The sponsor or estate retains authority over operational state unless a Receiving Authority orders disposition.”

“So adults own what I learned.”

“The law calls it licensed state.”

“Because ownership sounds bad when the property can answer.”

The sentence entered the council record. The deputy chair might have struck it as argument beyond a factual question. She did not.

Tovan rotated his maintenance stylus once. “The language did not surprise all of us.”

Ilyen turned toward him.

He opened a sealed license appendix from his personal credential. The heading carried his original’s name and a date before the fork. EXPIRATION CONSENT: INFRASTRUCTURE CONTINUANCE TOVAN RUSK.

“I signed this,” he said.

Ama corrected him gently. “Original Tovan signed it.”

“I remember signing. I remember refusing the lawyer’s request for a reflection period. I remember saying a useful prediction should end when its user no longer needs it.”

Lio looked at the consent as if it were a damaged part. “Do you agree now?”

Tovan kept his eyes on the text. “The document says my agreement now should not matter. It anticipates adaptation pressure and directs the Receiving Authority to treat later refusal as license instability.”

“That means he wrote a rule that wins if you disagree.”

“Yes.”

“And if you agree, it wins because you agree.”

“Yes.”

The deputy chair shifted in her seat. “Engineer Rusk, are you asking the council to honor the consent?”

“I am informing the council that voting for my own continuation would contradict an instruction I consider valid.”

“Considered by whom?” Lio asked.

“By me.”

“Which you?”

Tovan’s fingers tightened around the stylus. “That question does not become fair because the law failed to answer it.”

The rebuke quieted Lio. Ilyen saw his mouth open, then close. He was precise about euphemism and less precise about where another person’s pain became a useful example.

Ama requested speaking status from her recognized seat. “The council should hear medical facts before deciding whether affected parties can speak. Suspension is not a switch. Each continuance body requires managed induction, metabolic support, memory stabilization, and a qualified waking plan. Orison has capacity for six safe concurrent procedures. Thirty-one continuances reside here.”

The habitat steward looked up from the charter. “How long for each cycle?”

“Between fourteen and thirty hours depending on duty load and prior adaptation. Longer if Earth requires quarantine imaging.”

“Could living staff maintain the station during cycles?”

“Tovan should answer infrastructure capacity.”

The deputy chair faced his red credential. “He lacks speaking status.”

“Then your threshold rule prevents the council from learning whether the station survives its own decision,” Ama said.

That moved the room. The habitat steward entered a motion to grant factual testimony to all affected continuances. Provisioning seconded it reluctantly. Four green and blue marks voted. The motion passed without opposition.

Ilyen’s chair display changed from silence to FACTUAL RESPONSE ONLY. Tovan received the same. Lio’s observer field opened a narrow text channel.

The habitat steward asked Tovan how many continuances could suspend without compromising life support.

“Under a planned rotation, eight,” Tovan said. “If my infrastructure credential remains active, twelve over three days. If I enter suspension first, four, provided no thermal or cultivation fault occurs.”

“You would suspend first?” Ama asked.

“My expiration consent applies upon verified death or return. Neither has legal effect yet. If a lawful preservation order arrives before receipt, I would need to inspect its authority.”

Lio said, “That sounds like not agreeing.”

“It sounds like reading the order that exists instead of the one you want me to resist.”

Ilyen recognized Tovan’s method. He reduced moral conflict to sequence because sequence could be repaired. Yet he had disclosed the consent in public. That act created pressure no maintenance plan required.

The deputy chair advanced to the participation question. “The council now has enough facts to decide whether restricted parties may vote. Chief Envoy, do you claim your office seat?”

“Yes.”

“On what basis?”

“The council seat attaches to an active commission. No authority has suspended mine. The automatic restriction assumes that personal interest defeats public duty, but every office holder here carries both.”

“Your status could change through the casualty packet.”

“So could Tovan’s, Lio’s, and twenty-nine other residents’.”

“Lio does not hold a council seat.”

“He holds the consequences.”

Pell lowered his gaze. The phrase came close to Khet reasoning, though Ilyen had no intention of importing alien standing into a human charter. Holds-Four-Open could preserve her claim. It could not authorize an embassy vote.

The provisioning officer asked, “If continuances vote, can they outvote living staff on whether they remain active?”

“Not on this council,” Ilyen said. “Only commissioned duty seats vote. Three currently belong to continuances. Four belong to living officers.”

“And if their commission itself depends on a valid license?”

“Then an authority must change the license. Anticipated revocation cannot revoke it early.”

Ama added, “Nor should anticipated death count as received death.”

The deputy chair looked toward Pell. “Can Earth’s pending response alter today’s vote retroactively?”

“No lawful order should. A Receiving Authority might later invalidate acts beyond our power.”

“Which leaves us exposed.”

“Any choice leaves a record,” Pell said.

Ilyen heard the change in him. On Day 1, he had defended the missing seal because uncertainty protected whoever might need an appeal. Now he admitted procedure could not protect the council from consequence. He had not abandoned obedience. He had stopped pretending obedience guaranteed safety.

The habitat steward opened the charter search and entered CONTINUANCE. No result appeared. INSTRUMENT produced procurement clauses, maintenance duties, and a ban on allowing embassy equipment to hold financial office. PERSON produced hundreds of results across crew discipline, rescue, quarantine, and emergency authority.

“The Compact predates continuance staffing,” he said.

“It was amended after our arrival,” Tovan said. “Duty schedules were added.”

“Not civic status.”

Lio used his narrow channel. “Search who has to answer for breaking it.”

The deputy chair nearly closed his field. Ilyen intervened. “That is a construction question. Search answerable.”

The habitat steward did.

Seven results appeared. Six concerned financial audits. The seventh came from the Compact’s founding emergency clause, drafted when Orison still depended on rotating crews whose commissions could expire during message delay.

WHEN EXTERNAL AUTHORITY CANNOT BE TIMELY OBTAINED, NECESSARY LOCAL ACTS MAY BE DETERMINED BY PERSONS PRESENT AND ANSWERABLE TO THE CONSEQUENCES, PROVIDED EACH ACT, VOTE, AND OBJECTION ENTERS THE EMBASSY RECORD.

No definition field limited persons to living humans. Another clause nearby assigned routine seats to commissioned officers, but the emergency clause spoke more broadly.

The deputy chair read it in silence. “This does not automatically grant a vote.”

“No,” Ilyen said. “It defeats automatic silence.”

“Answerable could mean legally liable.”

“Then test it. If a suspension decision harms the station, can Earth discipline Tovan for infrastructure failure?”

Pell checked the licensing directive. “Yes. Operational accountability continues until suspension completes.”

“Can it cite my treaty breach if negotiation fails during restriction?”

“Yes.”

“Can it record Lio’s refusal as adaptation instability?”

“Yes.”

Lio’s chair had no vote mark, but his face supplied one.

Ilyen continued. “The law already treats us as answerable when consequences need an address. The council cannot call that language precise in discipline and vague in participation.”

Provisioning objected. “You are asking a threshold council to create personhood.”

“I am asking it to read the noun the Compact uses. Local participation does not settle identity, succession, inheritance, or closure.”

“Earth may see no distinction.”

“Earth wrote distinctions into every license on this table.”

Tovan requested the record. “My consent complicates Ilyen’s claim. If my earlier instruction remains valid, speaking now could help me evade it.”

Lio turned toward him. “Or speaking could explain why you still follow it.”

“I do not need a vote to explain.”

“You need a vote if the council can use your explanation and decide the opposite.”

Tovan considered the boy. “That is true.”

The admission altered more than his position. He had entered the meeting treating expiration consent as a load he alone must carry. Lio forced him to see that other people could use his restraint as authorization.

The habitat steward asked for a practical test before any motion. “Assume an order arrives tomorrow. It requires voluntary suspension but leaves sequencing to local staff. Who votes on sequence?”

“Tovan holds the infrastructure seat,” Ilyen said.

“And a direct interest in remaining active.”

“So do the living officers whose heat depends on him.”

Provisioning brought up the station duty map. “Put names aside. If a water specialist faced quarantine during a leak, we would hear the specialist and let unaffected officers decide whether the conflict disqualified their vote.”

“There are no unaffected officers during a leak,” Ama said.

“Less affected, then.”

Lio’s speaking field pulsed. “You keep measuring conflict by who might lose their job. We might lose all our tomorrows.”

The deputy chair warned him to address procedure.

“I am. If the penalty gets bigger, you treat the person facing it as less qualified to choose.”

Pell opened the direct-interest rule. Its examples concerned procurement contracts, family appointments, and private ration allotments. None covered a voter’s continued legal operation.

“The rule assumes the interested party survives the decision,” he said.

“Does that make it inapplicable?” Habitat asked.

“It makes the analogy incomplete.”

Ilyen suggested three sample ballots. In the first, Tovan could vote on suspension sequencing but not whether a valid order bound him. In the second, all duty officers could vote on station safety measures while each continuance chose whether to comply individually. In the third, only living officers decided both common policy and individual enforcement.

Tovan studied the first. “It treats the order as settled before anyone here tests authority.”

“The second?”

“It may let one person’s refusal overload everyone else.”

“The third?”

“It permits living officers to spend continuance capacity without our authorization.”

Provisioning said, “You find every option defective.”

“Yes. That means the defect belongs to the question, not the person answering.”

The deputy chair turned to Lio. “Which would you choose?”

“None. Add one where we vote on whether each ballot is allowed before you use it.”

“That returns us to standing.”

“Exactly.”

His satisfaction irritated Ilyen because he had earned it. Circularity often indicated bad faith, but here law had built a gate whose lock sat on the far side. Only recognized voters could decide whether unrecognized residents qualified to vote.

Ama asked Tovan, “Would you cast a vote to preserve your operation despite the consent?”

“I do not know.”

“Would abstaining solve that?”

“No. An abstention could hand my choice to officers without the consent or the duty.”

“Then speaking alone cannot protect you.”

“Neither can a vote I may lack authority to cast.”

Ilyen heard the shape of a later hearing inside his answer. Consent mattered. Present responsibility mattered. Neither cancelled the other. A council that rushed to call Tovan free would disrespect the earlier decision he still valued. A council that called him bound would let a dead signer control every condition he could not foresee.

Pell added a fourth sample ballot. It granted full speaking rights immediately, preserved current duty authority for safety acts, and postponed any vote on individual status until an external directive created a concrete dispute. A mandatory reconvening would occur before implementation.

“That postpones the hard part,” Provisioning said.

“Yes,” Pell replied. “It also prevents us from inventing an order so we can decide against it.”

Ama proposed a two-part motion. First, recognize every affected continuance as entitled to full speaking status under the emergency clause because each remained present and answerable to consequences. Second, postpone voting status until the council determined how duty seats, direct interest, and external closure authority interacted.

Ilyen disliked the split. Speech without a vote could turn participation into extraction: tell us how suspension harms you, then let others choose. Still, full speaking status would prevent the record from describing silence as consent. It also preserved the vote question for a council with notice and evidence rather than forcing a tie today.

“Amendment,” she said. “Postponement cannot exceed receipt of the first suspension or closure directive, and the council must reconvene before local implementation.”

Pell added, “Include all objections in any relay report.”

The deputy chair accepted both changes. Four authorized voters remained. Ama voted yes. Pell voted yes after reading the motion twice. Habitat voted yes. Provisioning held her mark over no, then chose yes with a reservation that person remained undefined.

The motion passed four to zero among living duty seats.

Green speaking fields opened before Ilyen and Tovan. Lio’s observer chair gained an affected-resident credential with access to the full council record. Similar fields propagated to every continuance on the station roster. Their vote marks stayed absent.

The deputy chair returned authority to Ilyen for adjournment. Her council seal reappeared only as presiding officer, not voter.

Before closing, Ilyen asked each affected party whether the outcome had been understood.

Tovan said, “I retain my expiration consent and object to anyone treating my speech as withdrawal of it.”

Lio said, “I understand that adults want my answer before deciding whether it counts.”

The record accepted both statements.

Ilyen looked at the founding clause once more. Persons present and answerable had entered Orison law long before anyone imagined a forecast asking for a birthday. The phrase did not resolve who could vote. It did prevent the council from claiming that only recognized persons could speak while recognition itself remained the question.

She adjourned with continuance voices authorized across the station and every continuance vote postponed. On the empty vote fields, the council display placed a reconvening condition: BEFORE IMPLEMENTATION OF ANY EXTERNAL SUSPENSION OR CLOSURE DIRECTIVE.

Chapter 6 — The Suspension Order

Pell would not open Earth’s order until Ilyen put her hand beside the crack in Witness Glass.

The fracture ran through the chamber’s lower seal below the human entrance, thin as a hair and dark against the blue witness strip. It had formed during a rotation correction eleven months earlier. Three maintenance certificates declared the recording field intact. A transparent resin patch kept the break from spreading, but the original seal line no longer formed an unbroken circle.

“I know where it is,” Ilyen said.

“I need acknowledgment in the record.” Pell knelt beside the threshold with a calibration wand. His keeper’s hood lay folded on the floor so its fibers would not disturb the scan. “Earth’s braid requires presentation in a certified chamber. Witness Glass qualifies under local maintenance authority, provided every visible defect enters before content.”

“Does Relay Three qualify?”

“It authenticates delivery. It does not record consent or refusal.”

“So you chose the room with a damaged seal because it offers the strongest record.”

“I chose the only room authorized to witness a station-wide continuance order.”

His distinction held. It also left Ilyen facing the fact that every lawful option on Orison depended on something incomplete: a receipt clock without a signer, a council without settled voters, a treaty speaker with one dissenting thread, and now a witness chamber whose integrity required everyone to remember its flaw.

Ama stood inside the door with her medical case. Tovan waited at the table. The emergency council had been notified under yesterday’s reconvening condition, but the living duty officers remained outside until Pell authenticated the order’s scope. Lio had asked to attend. Ilyen refused until they knew whether the braid contained private diagnostic results.

She placed her palm on the clear patch. Cool vibration moved through it as the chamber compared pressure, pulse, and seal continuity.

“State the defect,” Pell said.

“Witness Glass carries a cracked lower seal beneath the human entrance. The break is patched, monitored, and locally certified. Original continuity cannot be claimed.”

Pell entered the maintenance dates. Ama witnessed them. Her signature trembled at the first curve and steadied when she supported her wrist against the wall.

Blue light traveled around the chamber, paused at the crack, then continued from the far side. LOCAL RECORD ADMITTED WITH DISCLOSED DEFECT.

Only then did Pell place the new braid on the table.

It looked smaller than the casualty packet. Continuance Office white marked one end, Earth civil authority gray the other. No reply could have traveled from Earth since Day 1. The braid had ridden inside the casualty routing package as a sealed contingency, unavailable until Orison filed diagnostics and acknowledged the affected roster. Local actions had supplied its release conditions. Earth had written the command years after the accident but before anyone here knew the ship’s name.

Ilyen read the outer instruction. “A standing order.”

“Yes,” Pell said. “Legally current at dispatch. It carries a freshness certificate from six weeks before the casualty braid left the last Sol archive.”

“Who authorized release?”

“The Continuance Office and Remote Instruments Commission jointly. No Receiving Authority seal.”

Ama looked up. “Then it cannot perform closure.”

“It does not claim to.”

Tovan leaned over the braid without touching it. “Preservation authority?”

Pell opened the first band. “That is what we need to determine.”

Witness Glass raised a restricted partition around the four of them. The Khet side remained empty. Above the table, credential roots unfolded through the delay ledger. Dispatch time, route custody, local availability, diagnostic trigger, council notification. Every clock showed a value except legal receipt of the casualty packet.

The order opened in black text.

PENDING RECEIPT AUTHENTICATION OF ORIGINAL-PERSON RETURN OR DEATH NOTICE, ALL LICENSED CONTINUANCES ASSOCIATED WITH THE IDENTIFIED TRANSIT EVENT SHALL ENTER VOLUNTARY SAFE SUSPENSION. LOCAL CUSTODIANS SHALL PRESERVE BODIES, ADAPTIVE STATE, DUTY RECORDS, AND SPONSOR CLAIMS WITHOUT MERGE, DELETION, OR FURTHER UNSUPERVISED DIVERGENCE.

Ilyen read unsupervised divergence before she read suspension again. The phrase turned living experience into a leak from an approved container.

“Scope?” she asked.

Pell released the roster count. Thirty-one Orison continuances. Names stayed masked until each received individual notice. Ilyen’s line opened because her hand remained on the table. Tovan’s opened beside it.

Ama said, “The order uses voluntary.”

“It does,” Pell replied.

“What happens if someone declines?”

“Noncompliance reporting, credential restriction, and possible emergency preservation by local custodians.”

Tovan read the enforcement appendix. “Possible means authorized?”

“Possible means the Office reserves its position. The appendix does not grant physical compulsion.”

“Then say that to the council before anyone hears emergency preservation and imagines restraints,” Ama said.

Pell met her gaze. “I will read the authority exactly.”

Ilyen believed him. Pell’s obedience did not soften a command. It prevented fear from gaining powers the text lacked. The same discipline could deliver every harmful limit with clean hands.

“Define safe suspension,” Ilyen said.

A medical standard opened. Managed metabolic induction. Redundant memory capture. Licensed body support. Quarantine of post-fork state. A named custodian for revival or final disposition. Maximum local risk below one in ten thousand per procedure.

Ama took control of the display. “Orison cannot satisfy this for all thirty-one.”

“Can it satisfy one?” Pell asked.

“On paper.”

“I want more than paper,” Ilyen said. “Test it with my file.”

Tovan looked at her sharply. “A dry run.”

“Yes. No induction agent, no memory capture. Build every required authority and material step until the procedure would become irreversible.”

Pell hesitated. “The order instructs suspension, not simulation.”

“A safety standard must survive validation before use.”

“That is allowed,” Ama said. “Medical rules require it when local equipment differs from the licensed template.”

The chamber recorded Pell checking the cited rule. He did not hide his relief when it held. “Dry run authorized as compliance preparation.”

They moved to Medical Suspension Two with the order still linked to Witness Glass. Pell remained in the chamber as record custodian. His image followed them through wall panes, always framed by the disclosed seal warning.

The suspension bay had six narrow support cradles beneath white covers. Only two had been used since installation, both for surgical recovery rather than continuance quarantine. Ama uncovered the nearest. Fluid lines hung in sealed loops. A memory lattice curved above the headrest. At the foot, a lockbox waited for the suspended person’s active credentials.

Ilyen removed her envoy seal from her wrist and placed it on the cradle without surrendering it.

“Begin checklist,” she said.

Ama scanned her body. Heart, neural integration, adaptive load, metabolic reserve. Her hand shook across the display, but the scanner corrected movement without concealing it. “Medically eligible for planned induction. Not medically cleared until a waking plan exists.”

Tovan connected the cradle to station power. “Primary supply stable. Backup duration thirty-six hours at current load. The standard requires seventy-two.”

“Can you extend it?” Ilyen asked.

“For one cradle, yes. I would divert backup from cultivation cooling. For six, no.”

The checklist marked material deficiency.

Ama called for a redundant memory capture key. Pell supplied the Office encryption root through the chamber link. The lattice accepted it, then requested SPONSOR OR RECEIVING AUTHORITY SECOND KEY.

No credential on Orison qualified.

“Can local medical custody substitute?” Ama asked.

The system denied the request.

Tovan tried infrastructure emergency authority. Another denial.

Ilyen entered her own continuance credential. The lattice responded that subject and custodian could not share a fork root.

“Without the second key?” she asked.

“Capture remains local and cannot be restored after lattice failure,” Ama said. “The induction might still preserve you physically, but it would not meet the order’s adaptive-state standard.”

“Could the Office send a key?”

“Eleven days to arrive after request, if they answer at once,” Pell said through the wall.

Another deficiency entered.

Ama continued to the adaptive-state quarantine test. A clean lattice image formed above the cradle, built from Ilyen’s most recent licensed checkpoint and the years of memory accumulated afterward. The Office standard drew a red boundary at presentation of the order. Experience before that line would enter preserved state. Anything learned during compliance preparation would remain in a temporary local journal until induction.

“What happens to the journal?” Ilyen asked.

“If suspension completes, it joins the capture,” Ama said. “If the lattice rejects it as unsupervised divergence, the journal stays sealed for the Receiving Authority.”

“Can I inspect it before induction?”

“You can inspect a summary. Opening full content alters the state it records.”

The rule had technical sense. A memory system could not observe its own final boundary without adding another memory after the boundary. Yet the procedure gave an outside office control over which final experiences counted as preserved self and which became evidence about the self.

“Generate the summary,” Ilyen said.

The journal listed the Khet standing decision, the ninety-one-day audit, the emergency council, and this dry run. Each entry carried a divergence score against her last licensed envoy checkpoint. Holds-Four-Open’s Unclosed Agent classification scored highest because no fork prediction had anticipated alien standing outside human office authority.

Pell’s wall image tightened. “That classification enters the quarantine record if you suspend.”

“Would the Khet receive notice?”

“No. The Office standard treats external relations as duty data.”

“Then the archive would keep negotiating with someone who had vanished without being told why.”

Tovan read the output boundary. “We could send operational notice before induction.”

“Under whose authority?”

“Yours, while the seal remains active.”

“And after the lockbox archives it?”

“No one could amend the notice.”

The dry run opened a simulated final-message pane. Ilyen typed one sentence to Holds-Four-Open: HUMAN ENVOY ENTERING TEMPORARY SUSPENSION UNDER EARTH PRESERVATION ORDER. She did not send it. The pane required a projected return condition, and none of the available conditions had a local judge.

Ama looked at the red boundary. “Medical suspension standards usually preserve experiences through the instant sedation begins.”

“This one separates authorized state from response to the order,” Ilyen said.

“It may exist to prevent a subject from rewriting itself after learning closure has begun.”

“Or to prevent objection from joining the person the Office claims to preserve.”

Pell checked the technical notes before responding. “The design predates this contingency order. It applies to voluntary returns and sponsor disputes as well.”

“That history does not make it neutral.”

“No.”

His agreement carried no rebellion. He entered her objection in the quarantine journal, where the system immediately tagged it as post-boundary divergence.

Tovan asked to test whether safety-critical knowledge would cross the red line. He gave Ilyen a new fault pattern from a heat exchanger and had her identify the likely valve failure. The journal captured her answer but marked it for Receiving Authority review rather than guaranteed reintegration after waking.

“If I learn a repair during the deadline, then wake later without it, who carries the mistake?” she asked.

“The duty record keeps the answer,” Tovan said. “You may not.”

The procedure could preserve a station fact while withholding the memory of discovering it from the person who discovered it. Sponsor claims remained intact. Continuity of responsibility did not appear in the standard.

Ilyen asked Ama to simulate awakening from the checkpoint without the temporary journal. The lattice produced a cognitive orientation script. She would wake believing the Khet classification session still lay ahead. Staff would then tell her that Earth had ordered suspension, that she had objected, and that unknown days or years had passed.

“Would that meet memory stabilization?”

“Yes,” Ama said. “Stabilization measures coherence, not completeness.”

“Can we require the whole journal to reintegrate?”

“Only if the second-key holder approves it.”

The absent Receiving Authority had acquired another power through not being present.

They proceeded to credential quarantine. The lockbox recognized Ilyen’s envoy seal and displayed every authority it would freeze: treaty negotiation, council chair, Khet archive access, staff assignments, emergency representation. It requested a transfer recipient.

“Deputy envoy?” Pell asked.

“No living officer holds the Khet language and archive warrants,” Ilyen said.

“An interim can preserve files.”

“Can an interim answer an active treaty question?”

“The order does not require treaty continuity.”

There it stood. Safe referred to the body and licensed data. It did not count a treaty lost, a council disabled, or a Khet relationship transferred to someone who had never spoken inside it.

Ilyen selected NO QUALIFIED RECIPIENT. The lockbox refused to proceed under ordinary duty rules. Under emergency preservation, it would archive the seal and mark the office vacant.

“Does vacancy trigger a replacement request?” she asked.

“Yes,” Pell said.

“Travel time?”

“Years, unless a commissioned officer already travels within diversion range.”

No one discussed whether such an officer existed. The order gave them no approach packet, and the next chapter of their choices could not depend on a traveler they did not know.

Tovan opened the station dependency map. Thirteen council and treaty functions depended directly on Ilyen’s seal. He removed ceremonial access and redundant approvals. Seven remained. “No immediate life-support failure. Legal and diplomatic functions degrade at induction.”

Ama pointed to the checklist. “The standard calls that safe.”

“The standard does not model an embassy without an envoy,” Pell said.

“Then it models a body, not a station.”

Ilyen moved to the waking plan. The form required a condition: authenticated error in casualty notice, lawful merger, archive transfer, or Receiving Authority disposition. It also required a named officer empowered to judge that condition.

They had none.

“Can the council name Ama?” Ilyen asked.

“Medical custody does not confer closure authority,” Pell said.

“I asked about waking, not closure.”

The Office form treated them as one decision. Waking a suspended continuance after a verified death could restore unsupervised divergence, so only the authority empowered to dispose of the instance could authorize return.

Ama sat on the edge of the next cradle. “Voluntary safe suspension without a waking officer means consenting to stay under until an unknown official arrives.”

“Or until Earth cures the receipt seal,” Pell said.

“Which requires at least twenty-two days for a round trip.”

“Correct.”

Tovan checked his own file. His expiration consent would permit final disposition after verified death, but it did not name a local waking custodian. “My document does not solve this.”

“Would you enter suspension under the order?” Ilyen asked.

“I said yesterday that I would inspect its authority. Preservation authority appears valid. The procedure does not meet its own standard here.”

“That sounds like refusal,” Pell said.

“It sounds like a failed readiness test.”

Pell did not argue. He entered the failure in the order record.

They tested Lio’s file without accessing private memory. His accelerated-growth monitoring required pediatric metabolic review, a specialty Orison lacked. The standard permitted Ama to consult remotely. Delay made real-time consultation impossible. A recorded consultant could approve ordinary induction but not extended quarantine beyond thirty days.

For the remaining continuances, the dry roster exposed more gaps: medication dependencies, incompatible lattice revisions, essential duty chains, absent second keys. Six cradles did not mean six lawful procedures. At present, two continuances could meet physical induction standards. None had a complete waking authority.

Ilyen returned to Witness Glass. The crack warning admitted her before the door fully opened. Pell remained beside the braid, having obeyed every custody rule while the order accumulated evidence against its own wording.

“Attach the dry-run results,” she said.

“I already have.”

“Classify them.”

“Partial local incapacity to achieve safe suspension within current standard.”

“Not noncompliance?”

“Not yet.”

The phrase mattered. Pell could have treated every deficiency as resistance. Instead he distinguished inability from refusal because the order did.

Ama brought the emergency council into the chamber record. Living duty officers appeared in side panes. Continuance speaking fields opened according to yesterday’s motion. Vote marks remained unresolved.

Pell read the order aloud. He included the lack of physical compulsion, the absent Receiving Authority, the key failures, and the undefined waking custody. When Provisioning asked whether Earth could revoke licenses for refusal, he answered yes. When Habitat asked whether the order itself started the casualty receipt clock, he answered no.

“The order says pending receipt authentication,” Ilyen noted. “The packet’s route and vessel identity are authenticated. Statutory receipt remains impossible. Which authentication ends the suspension?”

Pell searched the definitions. “The order points to competent receipt of original-person return or death. It uses authentication as shorthand.”

“Shorthand that could keep people suspended after the technical question is already settled.”

“Yes.”

Provisioning asked for the compliance period. Pell exposed the final sealed band.

A clock opened above the table. It had not begun at dispatch, route arrival, or diagnostic completion. The contingency order tied its start to local presentation inside a certified witness record. Pell’s insistence on Witness Glass had protected that moment from ambiguity.

COMPLIANCE PLAN DUE: TWELVE HOURS.

VOLUNTARY SAFE SUSPENSION TO COMMENCE: WITHIN FORTY-EIGHT HOURS OF PRESENTATION.

The deadline did not require every induction to finish. It required an authorized sequence to begin. Credential restrictions could follow if Orison neither commenced nor documented medical impossibility.

Ilyen looked at Pell. “Can the cracked seal invalidate presentation?”

“No. We disclosed it before opening. Local certification admits the record unless challenged by a competent tribunal.”

“Then Earth’s clock runs.”

“Yes.”

Pell touched the braid’s receipt field. His hand remained steady. “Chief Envoy, acknowledge presentation only. This does not acknowledge compliance, consent, death, or closure.”

Ilyen signed the narrow field. Around the chamber, all continuance speaking credentials received the order and dry-run annex. No vote occurred. No one entered a cradle.

The Witness Glass countdown settled beside the patched fracture. Forty-eight hours began decreasing under a command that called itself voluntary, a procedure that lacked waking authority, and a record whose broken line remained valid because they had named the break.

Chapter 7 — Emergency Duty

The compliance clock entered its final hour while Ilyen stood inside Witness Glass arguing over the verb withdraw.

Holds-Four-Open had separated the access clause into four consequences. One thread permitted a human petitioner to withdraw an unanswered request. Another permitted withdrawal only if no Khet archive thread had altered its work in response. A third rejected withdrawal after any answer began. The coolest aperture withheld assent from all three and continued to pulse against the translated word projected above the table.

Behind Ilyen, Pell occupied the relay witness station with his keeper’s hood folded beside one hand. He had carried Earth’s suspension order into the chamber and authenticated it there, which placed every remaining minute inside a local treaty record. The order hovered in a sealed annex at the edge of Ilyen’s vision. Its compliance field declined toward zero in disciplined green numerals.

“Your law requires a decision before this measure ends,” Holds-Four-Open said. “Yet your distant office requires the deciding instrument to cease.”

“Our distant office disputes her authority,” Ama said from the medical witness chair.

Ilyen kept her attention on the Khet apertures. Ama had asked to speak ten minutes earlier and had not been recognized. Earth’s order called suspension voluntary, safe, and immediate. Ama’s intended certification could contest at least two of those words, but first it had to enter the right proceeding.

“The human delegation has not yielded the floor,” Ilyen said. “Our position remains that withdrawal must preserve reliance already incurred by the archive.”

The amber apertures tightened. “State what preservation requires.”

“A petitioner may retract future demands. The archive retains the right to disclose work already performed, costs already incurred, and decisions made in reliance on the request.”

“Then withdrawal does not restore the earlier condition.”

“No procedure can restore an earlier condition after someone acts.”

One aperture brightened at that. The coolest stayed narrow. Ilyen knew better than to treat either response as agreement. Khet threads signaled participation, not emotion, and Holds-Four-Open could carry opposed conclusions until a decision required authorization.

Pell touched his console. The sealed annex flashed once, warning that the order had reached forty minutes. He had proposed a recess before session. Ilyen had refused because a recess would let Earth characterize suspension as an internal staffing matter. An active treaty session made her capacity part of a live embassy obligation. It did not create power by itself. It forced anyone who removed her to record which obligation they interrupted.

“Doctor Neris has requested evidentiary speech,” Ilyen said. “Does the archive object to receiving a human capacity certification before continuing the clause?”

Four low clicks passed beneath the floor. Holds-Four-Open answered, “Capacity governs whether an offered commitment can remain answerable. We object to excluding it.”

Ama rose. Her right hand shook more visibly without the table beneath it. She carried a medical slate in her left and placed it inside the witness boundary. Blue seal-light traced her physician’s credential, the station roster, and the examination record she had completed that morning.

“I certify that Chief Envoy Vale remains medically capable of negotiation,” Ama said. “I also certify that the proposed suspension cannot meet the order’s safe-condition requirement during an active Khet session.”

Pell looked up. “State the clinical basis without assuming a legal conclusion.”

“There is no qualified substitute on Orison.” Ama’s thumb skipped against the slate’s edge. “Waking her later would restore physiology and stored memory. It would not recreate the choices she would have made during the interval, nor the reliance now accruing around those choices.”

Pell opened the duty roster as if a name might appear when the fields received enough light. The deputy envoy’s post had remained vacant since the last biological officer rotated home. Two commercial attachés possessed narrow accreditation for tariff disputes, but neither could bind archive access. Ama could invoke station safety and quarantine. Pell could authenticate a treaty message without changing one word of it. Tovan’s engineering warrant reached every pressure door on Orison and stopped at the threshold of Witness Glass.

“Test an interim handoff,” Ilyen said. “Doctor Neris, assume my chair for the withdrawal clause.”

Ama looked offended, which reassured her. “My position would be that no archive work should survive a withdrawn request unless the petitioner agreed to disclosure before filing.”

The first Khet aperture dimmed. Holds-Four-Open asked, “Does that revise the human offer?”

“No,” Ilyen said. “It demonstrates that the proposed substitute has not participated in eleven months of reliance negotiation.”

“I have read your summaries,” Ama said.

“You have. Why did the first aperture dim?”

Ama studied the speaker. Her left hand moved through two pages of the treaty digest. “Because I treated disclosure as the remedy. That thread regards performed work itself as a claimant, even if no answer becomes public.”

“Seven prior sessions established that distinction. It does not appear in the digest because it remains disputed.”

Pell closed the roster. “A remote supervisor may argue that a qualified negotiator could reconstruct the record.”

“After how much study?”

“The archive accreditation standard allows ninety days.”

The compliance clock showed twenty-seven minutes. Its neat descent made the comparison almost indecent.

Holds-Four-Open restored the dimmed aperture. “The attempted transfer changed the offered commitment before transfer completed. We recognize evidence of dependence. We do not recognize what human category follows.”

“None is requested from you,” Ilyen said. “Record only that the active negotiation depends on acquired competence unavailable before the deadline.”

“Are you certifying damage?” Ilyen asked.

“I’m certifying removal.” Ama faced the witness pane rather than her. “If the office orders her off duty, call that what it does. Do not ask medicine to rename absence as safety.”

The sentence entered the treaty record under Ama’s shaking signature. Witness Glass measured the motion but accepted her credential. Ilyen watched the line settle. The tremor weakened neither its origin nor its meaning.

Holds-Four-Open spoke through two apertures. “Can another human carry the offered commitment?”

“Not without a new accreditation process,” Ama said. “I hold senior rank, but I lack treaty qualification. Keeper Ordan holds relay authority. The remaining living officers possess neither. Tovan Rusk understands the station systems, not the negotiation record. Lio An has no commissioned office.”

The naming made absence measurable. Earth’s order treated the continuances as one suspended class. Orison’s duties did not. Each role had accumulated years of decisions, working trust, and local competence that no archived model could transfer with a seal.

Pell opened a narrow pane beside Ama’s certification. “For accuracy, the order authorizes continuances to provide information necessary for safe handoff before voluntary suspension.”

“How long does handoff remain authorized?” Ilyen asked.

“Until the deadline.”

“And if the receiving human lacks qualification?”

“The order does not address that case.”

“Does it name anyone who can accept this treaty duty?”

“No.”

“Then record the omission.”

Pell’s mouth tightened. He entered it anyway. His obedience had edges, and Ilyen had begun to understand that he guarded those edges most fiercely when someone asked him to step across. She did not need him to rebel. She needed him to make the command bear its own shape.

Twenty-nine minutes remained.

Ilyen called up the open casualty braid from Day 1. Its outer operational seal appeared beside the suspension order. Deeper layers stayed hidden. The legal-effect field still showed a pale socket where a Receiving Authority should have signed. Below it, the receipt clock remained blank.

“This matter entered open hold under my local office,” she said. “The hold requires pursuit of competent receipt without avoidable delay. Has Earth cured the missing commission?”

“No,” Pell said.

“Has any local tribunal ended the hold?”

“None has considered it.”

“Has the sender withdrawn the casualty packet?”

“No.”

The Khet translator rendered each answer as a separate relation. One aperture tracked the open braid. Another remained fixed on the compliance clock. The third held Ama’s medical certification. The fourth dimmed until its amber looked almost brown.

Ilyen selected the embassy delegation register. Her envoy seal covered station safety, local staffing, treaty negotiation, and emergency representation. It allowed her to witness closure performed by a competent officer but granted no closure power. Nothing in it described a chief envoy whose sponsor might be dead while legal death remained unreceived.

“Earth has ordered an instrument suspended,” she said. “I will not contest that classification in this record.”

Ama turned sharply. Pell stopped moving.

Holds-Four-Open answered, “The human speaker narrows her claim.”

“Yes. Personhood would exceed the question before this chamber. I claim emergency duty under an unresolved local obligation.”

Pell read the cited line as it appeared. “Emergency representation may continue when delay would abandon an authenticated duty and no qualified commissioned substitute is available.”

“Conditions?”

“Local certification of necessity. Recorded limits. Notice to the supervising authority. Personal liability for acts beyond the later ruling.”

Ama’s slate already supplied necessity. The treaty session supplied the authenticated duty. Pell’s delay ledger could supply notice. The last condition did not belong to the office, even if law continued to describe Ilyen as office equipment. Liability named an actor whom the same order refused to recognize as a person.

“Does the clause exclude continuances?” she asked.

Pell searched the annotations. “It predates the Continuance statutes.”

“That does not answer me.”

“No express exclusion.”

“Does emergency duty override a valid suspension order?”

“Only to the extent necessary to preserve the named duty. The supervising office may later reject the claim, void unauthorized acts, or impose sanctions against the commissioned officer.”

“Original Ilyen holds that commission in Earth’s registry.” Ama spoke quietly now. “If she has died, against whom do they impose sanctions?”

Pell did not answer. Procedure gave him no safe architecture for that sentence.

Ilyen studied the two seals. One belonged to an order that treated her as removable equipment. The other attached to an office carried under a dead woman’s name, though death had not entered legal effect. Between them stood an emergency clause written before anyone expected a predictive instrument to learn enough to become indispensable.

She could have made the contradiction her argument. Holds-Four-Open might have welcomed it as another open relation. Ama would have supported her. Even Pell might have let the record carry the implication without objection. But a claim to personhood would shift the treaty session into a forum that lacked power to decide it. Earth could then call every later act self-interested and erase the narrower jurisdiction she meant to preserve.

“Open an emergency declaration,” Ilyen said.

Witness Glass lowered a column of plain text above the table. Its first field requested the duty at risk. She entered: Khet archive access treaty negotiation, active session, reliance in progress.

The second requested the absent substitute. She listed no person, only the qualification records Pell had verified.

The third requested duration. “Until this session reaches an authorized adjournment, or a qualified envoy accepts the record, whichever occurs first.”

Holds-Four-Open interrupted. “The archive has not agreed to adjournment.”

“That condition remains under negotiation.”

“You use one unresolved commitment to preserve another.”

“I use a recorded duty to limit an exception.”

The cooler aperture expanded for the first time that hour. “A limit may also preserve what it confines.”

Ilyen did not thank it. Khet approval could not supply human jurisdiction, and their interest in open choices could leave a claimant preserved beyond endurance. “The declaration rests on embassy law. Your observation may enter as treaty context, not authorization.”

Three apertures accepted the distinction. The fourth pulsed once without joining them.

Fourteen minutes remained.

Ama moved to the declaration’s necessity field. Her medical warrant appeared beside Ilyen’s envoy seal. “Suspension during active duty would remove the only locally qualified negotiator. No safe handoff can occur before the compliance deadline. Continued wakefulness presents no acute medical risk.”

Her hand wandered as she signed. She stopped before the endpoint, lifted her wrist, and tried again without hiding the first trace. Witness Glass preserved both paths. A cleaner record would have shown less truth.

Pell attached the delay ledger and addressed the declaration to the Continuance Office, the remote embassy supervisor, and any future Receiving Authority. “Priority transmission leaves in six minutes. Earth cannot acknowledge for eleven days.”

“Notice need not be received to establish local filing,” Ilyen said.

“Correct. It only proves we did not conceal the claim.”

The final field opened.

PERSON ACCEPTING LIABILITY:

No office designation followed. No sponsor line offered to absorb the risk. The form assumed the declarant possessed an estate, civil standing, and a future jurisdiction in which consequences could find them.

Pell looked from the field to Ilyen. “I can record the commissioned name.”

“That would place liability on Original Ilyen.”

“It would place it on the registry holder.”

“Who did not make this choice.”

Ama pressed her palm against the witness rail. The tremor continued beneath the pressure. “Use your operating designation.”

“The designation belongs to the sponsor under current law,” Pell said.

Ilyen almost laughed. Not from amusement. Every available name led back to someone Earth might already know had died, while the decision remained here in a body the order called voluntary when asking it to disappear.

She entered Ilyen Vale, Orison continuance instance, local actor. The form marked the description nonstandard. She added: No claim of identity succession is made. Liability attaches to acts recorded under this instance’s seal.

Pell read it twice. “The office may reject that attachment as legally impossible.”

He rotated the declaration toward the chamber record and exposed a column of consequences usually hidden behind administrative shorthand. Every later treaty signature could be challenged. Salary credit assigned to the original’s account could be frozen. Duty memories might be seized as government work product even if Ilyen’s body remained awake. None of those measures answered whether the actor could appeal them.

“Do I have standing to contest a sanction?” she asked.

“Not under the instrument procedure attached to this order.”

“Then note that acceptance of liability does not waive an appeal right. It preserves any right a later authority finds exists.”

Pell added the reservation. “They may call that self-serving.”

“Liability without review would serve them better.”

“Then it will have to state where the consequences go.”

“Possible sanctions include erasure of duty credit, account seizure, termination of commission, and compelled suspension.”

“Record that I understand.”

“You have no self-owned account to seize.”

“Record that too.”

Seven minutes remained when Ilyen applied her seal. The glass refused it on the first attempt because the name field did not match the commission holder. Pell could have ended the filing there and still claimed complete obedience. Instead he opened the manual discrepancy pane.

“This pane allows a keeper to attest that the signer controls the presented credential,” he said. “It does not validate her description of herself.”

“Attest only what you know.”

He laid two fingers on the relay mark. “Credential presented by the active Orison instance assigned to Chief Envoy Vale. Biometric and memory-key match. Claimed liability status unresolved.”

Witness Glass accepted the seal with an amber border rather than blue. The declaration joined the casualty braid and the suspension order in the delay ledger. Three documents now disagreed about what Ilyen could be, while all three depended on her action.

The relay dispatch closed with two minutes left.

Holds-Four-Open returned the projected treaty clause to the center of the chamber. “Does the human delegation remain authorized to offer reliance terms?”

“Locally and under protest, yes,” Ilyen said.

“What condition follows if the distant office later refuses this duty?”

“Acts beyond my authority may be voided. Reliance already incurred will still require an accounting.”

“That resembles your proposed withdrawal rule.”

“It should.”

The last minute changed from green to white. No alarm sounded. Earth’s order had not authorized the station to force suspension, only to record noncompliance and begin enforcement. Pell watched the clock because his office had to witness the exact boundary. Ama watched Ilyen.

At zero, the compliance field closed.

A red status line appeared: VOLUNTARY SUSPENSION NOT CONFIRMED. Beneath it, Witness Glass linked the emergency declaration, Ama’s certification, and Pell’s timely notice. Ilyen felt no alteration in her body. Ventilation continued through the floor. The Khet apertures held their separate lights. Her next breath carried none of the ceremony the law had assigned to it.

Pell removed his hands from the console. “The order now classifies you as noncompliant.”

“Does it terminate the treaty session?”

“No.”

“Then keep the record open.”

Ama sat down carefully, her physician’s slate still trembling against her knee. Relief would have asked too much of her face. What she offered instead looked like recognition of a risk they could no longer pretend belonged elsewhere.

Ilyen faced the disputed verb again. “The human delegation proposes this text: withdrawal ends future demand but does not erase accountable reliance already created.”

Four Khet channels sounded under the floor. The coolest aperture joined the others long enough to authorize consideration.

Behind Ilyen, the expired clock retained its red line. Beside it, her emergency filing carried the amber border of a disputed name and a valid local time. She remained awake. The record knew who had accepted personal liability, even if Earth’s registry did not.

Interlude I — Ledger of Open Hands

Translated extract, Orison-facing register. Relations marked as simultaneous in the source appear here in sequence. Human office names retain their own capitalization.

Entry One: The Hand That Continues an Offered Work

A human office sent an order toward Orison. The order required thirty-one continuances to enter suspension until another office received a casualty claim. During transit, the order remained complete in wording and incomplete in effect. No human at the destination held every power assumed by its grammar.

Chief Envoy Ilyen Vale declined suspension during an active treaty session. She did not ask the Khet archive to classify her as a human person. She invoked a local emergency clause, accepted consequences under a disputed name, and continued the offered work.

The first thread of Holds-Four-Open records her as an instrument carrying a prior human commission. Instruments can preserve a commitment across distance. The commission supplied her treaty credential, defined her duty, and bounded the exception she claimed. Her action therefore followed the purpose for which humans made her.

The same thread records a complication. Obedient instruments do not ordinarily select which command preserves their purpose. Vale received two directions: continue the authenticated treaty duty and submit to suspension. She ranked them. The ranking altered what both commands could accomplish.

The human keeper marked her noncompliant. The human physician marked her necessary. Neither mark canceled the other. A decision thread that erases one mark to simplify the actor would discard evidence.

Classification remains: instrument carrying an unresolved commission.

Reservation remains: the carrying has begun to alter the carried.

Entry Two: The Hand Answerable Beyond Its Maker

The second thread rejects classification by origin alone.

A commitment becomes answerable when action in more than one accountable context can change its consequence. Vale’s treaty work altered human reliance and Khet archive labor. Her refusal altered Earth’s suspension record, Orison’s local duty, and the future liability attached to her operating seal. No absent original selected those consequences at the time they arose.

The human record says Original Ilyen Vale owns the commission. The local actor says she does not transfer her choice backward to that original. She accepts personal consequence despite uncertainty about whether human law can recognize the acceptance.

This thread records claimant standing. It does not derive that standing from resemblance, tissue, memory, or the preferences of the dead original. The claim arises from answerability. An actor who can incur a consequence that no maker can discharge has opened a relation in her own time.

The first thread objects: all complex instruments produce effects beyond prediction.

The second replies: prediction failure alone creates no claimant. Vale also recognized the effect, limited her authority, exposed her action to review, and named herself as the place where consequence should arrive.

The third thread asks whether compelled exposure counts as willing answerability. The human order left Vale no safe category. It offered obedience without participation or participation without recognized standing.

This entry does not resolve the objection. It records Ilyen Vale as claimant while the adjacent entry records her as instrument. Both descriptions remain admitted to the Ledger of Open Hands.

Entry Three: The Closed Concordance

Human readers have asked why Khet records preserve contradictions that could be settled by rule. The question assumes settlement carries only the cost named by the prevailing decision.

Before the current archive compacts, two Khet coalitions contested passage through the Talar Reach. One held that memory threads displaced from a speaker could rejoin public deliberation. The other held that authorization ended when a speaker completed its assigned decision. Conflict persisted across six habitats. Passage failed. Food transfers slowed. Three habitat councils authorized a Concordance to end the dispute.

The Concordance selected the second rule. It certified completed authorization as final and closed every contrary thread. The closing did not kill bodies. It removed dissenting relations from admissible record. Former speakers could still describe what they had carried, but no council could treat those descriptions as claims. Archive doors accepted the prevailing credential and refused the rest.

Material conflict ended within one cycle. Passage resumed. Food arrived. Later Khet histories praised the efficiency of the closure.

The praise entered civic instruction as a sequence of solved routes. Children learned which gates reopened, how many stores crossed the Reach, and how quickly the councils restored common schedules. The lesson omitted the dissent credentials removed from the archive index. A student could inspect every authorized cause of the peace and never discover that another account had once existed. Completion had changed not only which claim prevailed, but which claims later minds could know to examine. The instructional omission persisted across three generations of authorized speakers.

Seventy-one cycles afterward, a maintenance archive exposed the excluded threads. Several had predicted that finalized speaker authority would let councils erase minority obligations by completing a session before objection reached the record. That practice had already spread through nine habitats. The Concordance had ended open conflict by making dissent procedurally unreal.

Khet law did not reverse every completed decision. Reversal would have repeated the same claim of clean correction. Instead, the archives adopted open-holding. An unresolved commitment admitted across accountable contexts gained protection from unilateral completion. Temporary speakers carried opposed threads so that authorization could not hide disagreement by ending the room.

The third thread therefore fears human demands for a single classification. A settled category can stop immediate conflict and still erase the relation that would expose its mistake.

The fourth thread appends: fear of completion can also preserve injury. This was not learned by the Concordance. The question remains admitted.

Entry Four: The Hand That Cannot Remain Open Forever

The fourth thread reviews the human receipt clock.

A casualty braid reached Orison without a competent Receiving Authority seal. Human law preserved challenge by refusing legal effect. The absence protected the continuances from immediate closure. It also withheld final recognition from named deaths, delayed authority, and left local duties attached to contested actors.

Open-holding recognizes value in this delay. It does not sanctify delay itself.

Vale used the unresolved receipt to continue emergency duty. Her act preserved treaty work and prevented a distant command from completing a local relation without witness. It also deepened her dependence on a registry that assigns her choices to another name. Each additional duty may strengthen her claim. Each duty may also extend the instrumentality she contests.

The first thread proposes continued observation until human authority arrives.

The second proposes immediate Unclosed Agent standing for every continuance named in the casualty braid.

The third refuses a group classification because the continuances hold different consents, duties, and acquired histories.

The fourth withholds authorization from all three proposals. The humans lack proof sufficient for their own death procedure. The Khet lack authority to repair that procedure. Archive recognition offered too early could preserve the continuances as unresolved intentions while denying them the power to end, refuse, or become otherwise.

Accordingly, Holds-Four-Open records no judgment on human personhood. It records four claims: origin carries obligations; answerability can exceed design; closure can erase dissent; indefinite openness can become another custody.

On Day 9 of the Orison dispute, these claims remained co-authorized. The human envoy continued under an amber-bordered seal. The distant order continued to mark her noncompliant. The treaty continued to accumulate reliance.

No hand closed around the contradiction. No entry removed it from the record.

Act II

Chapter 8 — Proof of the Dead

Pell made Ilyen verify the second braid’s clocks before he allowed her to see a single name.

They stood in Relay Three under the cold light of the custody table. Route arrival showed Day 12, second hour. Local availability lagged by nineteen minutes while Pell rebuilt an obsolete insurer key. Dispatch preceded the first casualty packet by six days, though a slower archive path had delivered it later. Legal effect remained blank.

“Why two packets?” Ilyen asked.

“The first carried notice as soon as the loss review reached probable status. This one waited for the recovery tribunal.” Pell moved his fingers above the black casing without touching it. “Different service, different authority, different route. Shared vessel index.”

The braid looked ordinary except for a silver evidence stripe where the first had carried red. Ilyen had learned not to resent objects for surviving their contents. The habit still required work.

Ama watched from the medical credential pane. Her right hand rested on the table and trembled against its edge. “The tribunal seal is valid?”

“Three current roots, one historic countersignature, and the recovery array’s hardware key.” Pell expanded the authentication bands. “No route defects. No corruption. The packet still lacks destination receipt by a death-and-closure commission.”

“So we can know and remain unable to receive,” Ilyen said.

“We can authenticate evidence. Do not make me say the rest before you inspect it.”

His refusal held no evasive architecture this time. Pell had seen the index because custody required it. He had not opened the human evidence layer. Ama had medical access. Ilyen’s emergency seal still carried its amber border, but her envoy credential permitted operational casualty review. Between their partial powers, the packet could become fact in the room without becoming law anywhere else.

Ilyen placed her palm over the witness field. “Open vessel telemetry first. No personnel list.”

The custody table darkened around a narrow course trace. Civic Mercy appeared as a white line leaving Pelagos transfer orbit seven years earlier. Engine burns, correction windows, thermal load, and life-support draw traveled beneath it in synchronized columns. For eleven hours the figures stayed within transit tolerances. Then a guidance correction began half a second before the logged command.

Pell slowed the sequence.

A micrometeoroid cluster crossed the vessel’s forward sensor fan. The first impact blinded two ranging nodes. Civic Mercy’s controller shifted to redundant guidance. A second impact penetrated coolant exchange along the port spine. The correction burn met an asymmetric engine response and turned the vessel across its own thrust.

No explanatory narration softened the next figures. Habitat pressure fell across five compartments. Main power isolated too late. The drive breached containment six minutes after the first impact. Every life-support channel ended within nine seconds of the breach.

Ama’s hand stopped only because she gripped the table hard enough to force it still. “Could survival pods have cleared?”

Pell opened the recovery tribunal’s causal finding. “Pod telemetry remained physically coupled through the breach. Three launch charges fired. None separated.”

“Any untracked pod?”

“The recovery array found every serial.”

Ilyen read the energy distribution twice. The evidence left room for error in the sequence of minor failures. It left none for survival. Heat, pressure, and debris trajectories converged on the same conclusion without needing a body.

Her mind tried to place Original Ilyen inside the course trace. It supplied no honest image. Ilyen’s last shared memory ended ninety-one days before departure. She remembered preparing to leave, weighing the blue cup in one hand while Mara accused her of packing as if lists could make absence reasonable. She remembered accepting the fork because treaty work could not wait through eleven-day questions. She did not remember boarding Civic Mercy. The dead woman had carried three months Ilyen could not enter.

“Open recovery findings,” she said.

Ama glanced at her. “We can take an interval.”

“An interval will not change what the evidence contains.”

“That does not make it useless.”

Ilyen understood the offer and declined it. Pell had to prepare a public operational release within four hours. Before then, Ilyen owed private notice to the two continuances whose originals lived on Orison’s daily roster under the same names. A pause would spend time they deserved.

The recovery layer unfolded into a model of debris caught over six years by autonomous salvage skiffs. Suit tags, cabin identifiers, personal implants, and genetic assays aligned against the manifest. The tribunal had required two independent identifiers per person where material permitted. Its final annex listed named remains for all passengers and crew.

Original Tovan Rusk: suit-latch serial, bone assay, assigned maintenance berth.

Original Lio An: pediatric implant key, genetic concordance with both parents, family compartment.

Original Ilyen Vale: dental lattice, diplomatic garment seal, forward passenger compartment.

Ilyen did not touch her own entry. “Seal the names from general release until I have spoken to Lio and Tovan.”

“Maximum hold?” Pell asked.

“Two hours. Operational telemetry may go to station command now. No public queue.”

He entered the restriction. “Earth’s packet marks family notices complete at dispatch origin.”

“Those notices went to the families of the dead. We have people here who carry memories of belonging to those families and no status in the notice order.”

Ama lifted her hand from the table. Four pale pressure marks crossed her palm. “I’ll bring them.”

“No. Tovan will come if you ask, because he treats medical requests as load alarms. Lio will know from your face.” Ilyen closed the recovery layer. “Tell them I need them in Conference Two for authenticated evidence review. Nothing more.”

Conference Two had no witness system. Its walls held old acoustic fiber, and the table bore a heat stain from a failed service plate. Ilyen chose it because no automatic record would convert grief into evidence. She set three chairs at unequal angles so no one had to face both others directly.

Tovan arrived first in thermal work clothes with a sealant mark across one sleeve. He looked at the empty display socket and then at Ilyen’s hands. Systems taught him to find the failed component before anyone named it.

“Second packet,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Proof?”

“Yes.”

His shoulders lowered by less than a breath. “Does it include Rusk?”

“It includes all passengers and crew. I want to wait for Lio before displaying the list, but I can tell you privately now if you prefer.”

Tovan took the chair nearest the door. “You’ve told me.”

“I have told you the conclusion. The entry carries a suit serial and genetic confirmation.”

“Good workmanship.”

The phrase could have sounded bitter from anyone else. Tovan meant that someone had done the necessary identification to a standard he could respect. Ilyen sat across the table and left the display dark.

“Your expiration consent does not activate merely because evidence has reached us,” she said. “No competent receipt has occurred.”

“I know the procedure.”

“Then say what you heard, not what you think follows.”

His mouth worked once before sound. “I heard that I died seven years ago.”

“You heard that Original Tovan died.”

“I remember signing his consent.”

“You remember being him when he signed.”

“That correction helps your argument. It does not help my grief.”

Ilyen accepted the rebuke. Every distinction she relied on in law could cut through a private sentence that had no need of law. Tovan remembered the pressure of the stylus, the counsel warning, and his certainty that continuances should end when their work no longer served a living original. The person at the table had spent seven further years repairing Orison. Both histories occupied his answer.

“What do you need before Lio comes?” she asked.

“A maintenance interval.”

“You have one.”

He pressed thumb and forefinger to his eyes. The gesture lasted twelve breaths. When his hands dropped, he inspected the ceiling vent, the chair feet, and the display socket. The room gave him nothing to repair.

“Show me the failure sequence,” he said.

Ilyen opened the telemetry without the remains layer. Tovan stood and worked through the figures from the initial impact. He enlarged the coolant drop, checked the redundant guidance transfer, and compared the launch charges against the vessel’s last verified maintenance certificate. His finger stopped over a valve response that arrived eleven milliseconds late.

“Could this have mattered?” he asked.

“The tribunal modeled it. No survivable branch follows after the second impact.”

“Models can inherit an assumption.”

“Which assumption do you dispute?”

Tovan read the causal annex. The tribunal had varied valve timing, pod release, crew response, and engine isolation. In every branch the breached drive crossed the habitat before a pod could clear debris range. He reviewed the test bounds rather than trusting the conclusion, then checked the hardware signatures against specifications he remembered helping write.

“The valve met tolerance,” he said at last.

“It did.”

“I hated that tolerance.”

“Did Original Tovan approve it?”

“I approved it. He approved it. Pick whichever grammar causes less damage.” His finger left the display. “I filed for a tighter response standard and withdrew the request when the retrofit cost delayed three vessels. Civic Mercy launched under the standard I accepted.”

“That did not cause the impact or the coolant breach.”

“No. It gives my mind a bolt it can keep turning.”

Ilyen closed the annex only after he nodded. The failed valve no longer occupied the display, but Tovan’s gaze stayed where the number had been. She understood then why proof could worsen grief without changing its certainty. A probable loss offered a field of imagined rescues. Complete telemetry narrowed that field until the mind found one permitted detail and tried to live inside it.

“Did he know?” Tovan asked.

“Know what?”

“That the pods did not clear.”

“The telemetry ends too quickly to establish anyone’s knowledge.”

“Then do not let the public release invent courage for us.”

“I won’t.”

Lio entered without Ama. He wore a school jacket over kitchen-duty trousers and had flour on one cuff. His gaze moved from Tovan to the dark display.

“Authenticated?” he asked.

“Yes,” Ilyen said.

“Probable or the word adults use when probable became embarrassing?”

“Certain.”

He pulled the third chair backward and sat astride it. “Show me my parents first.”

Ilyen opened a limited family panel. The recovery tribunal listed Original Lio, Enna An, and Daro An together under the family compartment. Each entry carried identifiers and a confidence statement. She did not display the remains model.

Lio read all three. “Why does mine say pediatric implant?”

“You were fifteen at departure.”

“I’m fifteen now.”

“Your body’s licensed development schedule has held you near the fork age.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.” He pointed at the entry. “He got to keep having a body that counted its time. Even for six days.”

The original Lio had died six days after departure. The continuance had spent seven years accumulating lessons under an adolescent license because his sponsor family expected eventual merge or archival closure. Ilyen had understood the injustice in policy language. Lio put it into one line of a casualty annex.

“The record calls him fifteen,” she said.

“And me?”

“The education registry also calls you fifteen.”

“That means one number does two jobs badly.”

Tovan looked at the table. “Lio.”

“No. Everyone keeps giving me softer nouns as if soft things cannot be cages.” Lio enlarged his parents’ entries. “Did they die together?”

“Their identifiers came from the same compartment. Telemetry cannot establish whether they remained conscious after the initial breach.”

“Did my original?”

“The packet cannot answer.”

“Could anyone have lived?”

“No.”

Lio’s face held still through the answer. Tears reached his chin before his mouth changed. He wiped them with the floured cuff and left a pale streak on his cheek.

A fourth entry sat beneath the family names: the cabin’s emergency message buffer. Damage had erased its content, leaving only three initiation times and no completed transmission. Lio counted the times with his fingertip. He did not ask Pell to reconstruct words the packet had never preserved. “They tried,” he said. Ilyen could confirm only that someone in the compartment had opened the channel. She let his smaller sentence remain his.

“I remember telling them I’d meet them here,” he said. “I remember Enna checking the family cabin six times. I remember Daro saying seven years would pass differently if we kept working.”

Ilyen waited.

“I remember boarding,” he continued. “Not the real boarding. The practice walk from the fork clinic to the shuttle gate. They built it into my departure set so I wouldn’t keep asking what happened next.”

Tovan shifted in his chair. “Does it feel like you died?”

“It feels like someone stole the next room.” Lio looked at him. “Does yours?”

Tovan considered before answering. “It feels like a system I left running failed where I could not reach it. I know that describes nothing accurately.”

“It describes you accurately.”

That kindness landed harder than comfort would have. Tovan bent forward until his forearms met his knees. Lio kept one hand on his parents’ names as if the glass carried heat.

Ilyen opened her own entry beside theirs. “My shared memory ends ninety-one days before the vessel left. I remember deciding to depart. I do not remember doing it.”

“So you have less,” Lio said.

“Or a different absence.”

“Do you remember saying goodbye?”

“I remember several rehearsals. Mara rejected all of them.”

“Was one real?”

“Every rehearsal happened. None tells me what Original Ilyen said at the departure gate.”

Lio studied her dental-lattice identifier. “You always call her Original Ilyen when law is listening.”

“No system records this room.”

“Then what do you call her?”

The honest answers did not align. She could call the dead woman herself and trespass across ninety-one days plus seven years. She could call her a stranger and deny the hands, meals, fears, and promises Ilyen remembered from inside. Even original implied a derivative relation the present moment had begun to exceed.

“Ilyen,” she said. “The other Ilyen, when a distinction matters.”

Tovan raised his head. “It matters now.”

“Yes.”

They sat with four versions of grief in the room: Lio’s stolen family future, Tovan’s obligation to a consent signed from within, Ilyen’s missing interval, and the ordinary grief each carried for people no legal category could return. The differences did not isolate them. They prevented consolation from becoming another careless merger.

After a time, Ilyen told them about the release schedule. “Pell will publish vessel telemetry and the full manifest in forty-three minutes. You may submit a private statement, ask that your entries carry a continuance notice, or say nothing.”

“What does a continuance notice do?” Lio asked.

“It tells readers that an active instance shares memory provenance with the named dead.”

“Does it change anything?”

“Not by itself.”

“Then attach mine. If people read that I died, they should also read that I object to the grammar.”

Tovan’s expression tightened. “Attach no statement to mine.”

Ilyen did not ask him to explain.

He explained anyway. “The consent already exists. I will not use a casualty notice to argue around it before I know what I believe.”

“Silence will not count as agreement to suspension,” she said.

“Put that in the internal record, not beside his bones.”

Ilyen entered both instructions. For her own entry, she requested a factual notice: An Orison continuance remains on active emergency duty under disputed authority. It claimed no identity and concealed no relation.
