They walked together to Relay Three. Ama met them outside and offered Lio a clean cloth. He used it on the flour rather than the tears. Tovan stopped at the threshold until Ilyen motioned him through.

Pell had prepared two displays. The left carried the public casualty release. The right carried Orison’s Continuance registry. Every original’s sponsor status still read remote, active pending competent notice. Every instance still read operational instrument, suspension ordered, compliance disputed.

“Telemetry release is ready,” Pell said. “The registry cannot ingest the death finding without a Receiving Authority seal.”

Lio stared at the paired panels. “So the public can know I’m dead while the office says he’s alive.”

“The public record will identify Original Lio as dead,” Pell said.

“You learned correction from her.”

“I learned where the distinction prevents error.”

Tovan read his unchanged sponsor line. “What process begins now?”

“None automatically,” Pell said. “The casualty fact enters the station archive. Statutory closure still waits for competent receipt.”

Ama’s hand shook as she placed her medical credential beside the release. She certified that private notice had occurred. Ilyen added her envoy seal. Tovan declined any mark. Lio pressed his thumb to the continuance notice and held it until the field accepted his print.

Pell released the braid.

Across Orison, public panels received Civic Mercy’s final course trace. The manifest changed from missing to confirmed dead. Messages began entering the condolence queue before the display finished populating.

On the registry beside it, no field moved.

Ilyen watched her own dead name settle into the casualty archive while her sponsor status remained active in green. Lio’s print appeared next to a sentence objecting to the grammar. Tovan’s entry carried only the tribunal evidence and his chosen silence.

Death had become fact. The office that could decide what the fact did to them still had no hand at Orison.

Chapter 9 — No Receiving Authority

The statute rejected Ilyen’s first query because she had asked it in the present tense.

Relay Three’s legal console showed a red syntax mark beneath who can receive the Civic Mercy death finding at Orison? Pell cleared the line and rebuilt it from the statute’s own assumptions: Which commissioned destination office shall receive authenticated death notice for an original whose licensed continuance remains operational?

The console accepted that question. It returned an empty seal socket.

Ama stood on the other side of the custody table with the medical warrant open above her shaking hand. “Ask what happens when the destination has no commissioned office.”

“It will route us to exception procedure,” Pell said.

“That sounds useful.”

“It routed the first braid to us.”

Ilyen watched the delay ledger arrange its clocks along the wall. Civic Mercy’s loss had occurred seven years earlier. Recovery authentication had completed before dispatch. The proof had reached Orison yesterday. Public knowledge had begun forty-six minutes later. Competent legal receipt still displayed no time at all.

“Start before the exception,” she said. “Trace every hand the statute expected.”

Pell opened the receiving chain. The recovery tribunal certified the casualty finding. Civil relay certified custody across the braid route. The Continuance Office attached the finding to thirty-one active licenses. A destination civil registrar should then certify death of each original. A Receiving Authority should use that death record, any Return Clause, and local evidence to determine closure of each continuance.

Four hands, all treated as if they occupied adjacent rooms.

“The tribunal has acted,” Ilyen said.

Pell marked it green.

“The relay has acted.”

Another field turned green.

“The Continuance Office attached the licenses before dispatch.”

A third field changed. The next remained gray: destination civil registrar. Orison’s local registry could record births, injuries, marriages, and deaths among living embassy residents. Its warrant came through the Foreign Habitats Act. A restriction beneath the seal excluded deaths used to trigger continuance closure.

Ama enlarged the restriction. “Why?”

“Conflict of duties,” Pell said. “A station physician or registrar may depend on the continuance being reviewed. The statute wanted an independent officer.”

“Every remote station depends on its engineers.”

“The legislative note assumed a temporary delay until an authorized courier arrived.”

Ilyen read the note’s travel estimate. Twelve days. Its drafters had imagined Luna, Mars, perhaps a licensed habitat in the outer system. They had not imagined Orison at the end of an eleven-day message route and years from physical appointment.

“Can your civil registry certify Original Ilyen’s death as an ordinary nonresident casualty?” Pell asked Ama.

“My warrant covers patients under station care or remains physically examined here. I have neither.”

“You examined the recovery evidence.”

“That lets me certify medical sufficiency for local operations. It does not let me turn a tribunal’s remote finding into my own examination.” Her index finger moved against the table in short lateral strokes. “If I signed anyway, every hostile reviewer would say the physician who kept Ilyen awake invented the death that supported her emergency claim.”

“She would be right not to sign,” Ilyen said.

Ama looked at her. “I know.”

The words had no gratitude in them. Gratitude would have mistaken institutional isolation for personal mistrust.

Pell advanced to the closure field. The statute recognized six classes of Receiving Authority: a Continuance Office commissioner, a civil court with instrument jurisdiction, an accredited death-and-closure notary, a military succession officer, a vessel master carrying a special transit commission, or a treaty authority granted equivalent power by an implementing act.

Orison had representatives adjacent to four classes. None possessed the necessary commission.

Ilyen served as chief envoy but held no implementing act. Ama held medical authority without civil closure power. Pell carried a relay oath that ended at custody. The station’s three lay adjudicators could hear contract and discipline disputes but lacked instrument jurisdiction. Tovan’s infrastructure warrant granted emergency command over life support, not legal status. Holds-Four-Open counted as a treaty counterpart, not a human authority.

“Show expired commissions,” Ilyen said.

Pell hesitated. “The statute excludes lapse cure after notice.”

“I asked what existed, not what works.”

The station archive produced two dead credentials. Orison’s founding master had carried a vessel succession commission during construction. It expired when the habitat received embassy status. A commercial notary had held authority for estate inventories until her return to Sol four years earlier. Her credential could verify property after death but had never included continuance closure.

Ama pointed to the founding master’s seal. “Could the station charter inherit it?”

“No,” Pell said. “The charter replaced vessel governance. Powers not restated reverted to the appointing body.”

“Which body?”

“The Remote Missions Directorate.”

“Ask whether they retained the power.”

The delay ledger opened a delegation history. Directorate reorganization had moved continuance matters to the Continuance Office twelve years ago. That office then centralized death-and-closure commissions after an estate fraud inquiry. Local implied powers were expressly withdrawn.

Ilyen felt the shape of the trap become cleaner. No accident had omitted the authority. A sequence of sensible reforms had concentrated it elsewhere. Each reform answered a prior abuse. Together they left Orison capable of knowing death, preserving bodies, maintaining treaty duties, and recording public grief without anyone empowered to decide what followed.

“Who may appoint a commissioner?” she asked.

“Earth’s Continuance Office, a competent civil court, or a supervising commissioner already holding delegation power.” Pell called up the pending exception notice. “Our Day 1 request asks Earth to cure the seal. Earliest possible answer remains Day 23 if they replied immediately on receipt.”

“They sent a suspension order instead.”

“They answered operational risk. They did not cure destination authority.”

“Could the suspension order imply appointment?” Ama asked.

“No named appointee, no oath, no commission term, no review venue,” Ilyen said. “An order restricting the subject cannot quietly create the independent officer meant to review that restriction.”

Pell nodded once. “I agree.”

His agreement changed the room more than Ama’s support could. Pell had obeyed the suspension order through its deadline and marked Ilyen noncompliant. He would not let dislike of its result substitute for a defect. If he named a defect, he could show every field that produced it.

They spent the next hour testing alternatives.

First Pell tried the embassy council’s vacancy clause. It allowed the senior surviving officer to assume an empty post during communication delay. Ama qualified by age of commission and station tenure. The legal console let her inherit duties attached to the chief medical office, station command, and civil protection. When Pell added death-and-closure authority, the seal path failed.

“Reason?” Ama asked.

“Reserved commission, not a station duty.”

“Then give me the post without the title.”

“The reservation follows the power. Renaming it changes nothing.”

Ilyen examined the cited reform. After the estate fraud inquiry, lawmakers had treated closure authority like a judicial warrant rather than an administrative task. They feared exactly the move Ama proposed: a dependent local office inheriting control over an instrument and its sponsor’s property. The safeguard remained rational. Distance had turned it into vacancy without remedy.

Next they tested Ilyen’s treaty credential. One implementing act allowed an envoy to receive foreign legal notices necessary for treaty performance. Civic Mercy’s death finding affected Khet negotiation, and the Khet had already entered Ilyen’s disputed capacity into their archive. Pell routed the casualty braid through the treaty-notice form. The console accepted the foreign-effect layer, then refused the human closure annex.

“I can receive the fact from the Khet if they repeat it to me,” Ilyen said, “but not from our own tribunal.”

“You can receive it for treaty effect,” Pell replied. “That does not trigger a domestic status process.”

Ama gave the console an unfriendly look. “Could the Khet appoint her under their law?”

“They can grant archive standing,” Ilyen said. “They cannot commission a human Receiving Authority. Even if they offered, accepting would let Earth call the closure a foreign disposition of human residents.”

The third attempt used station necessity. Orison’s charter permitted any officer to take an otherwise unauthorized act when immediate physical harm could not be prevented by lesser means. Pell attached the thermal license inquiry, though the full map had not yet arrived. The system asked for a present failure, named persons at risk, and a limit ending once safety returned.

“We have a foreseeable licensing failure,” Ama said.

“Not an immediate one,” Pell answered. “And closure would not clearly prevent it.”

“Temporary preservation might.”

“That could support a stay. It cannot create the officer who decides the merits.”

Ilyen saved that distinction for the petition. They did not need local power to settle every status if local power could stop an absent authority from making settlement irreversible through delay.

A remote judge could hear a petition but could not inspect local evidence without a receiving record. A Khet archive could grant standing under its own compacts but could not operate a human death statute. The embassy council could assign staff, yet assignment did not create a commission reserved to Earth law. Ilyen’s emergency clause preserved existing duty; it did not authorize closure. Ama could declare suspension medically unsafe in specific conditions. She could not decide whether the continuances owned their future bodies.

Pell then tested combination. Ama’s safety warrant, Ilyen’s treaty credential, and his custody oath together covered evidence, immediate care, and authenticated filing. The console displayed all three seals around the empty socket without merging them. Human law allowed divided offices to check a power. It did not let partial powers add themselves into a reserved commission.

“No hand,” Ama said.

“Three hands touching the boundary,” Ilyen replied. “None permitted through.”

Pell printed each failed route into the ledger rather than deleting it. “A later reviewer will otherwise assume we overlooked the obvious.”

“The obvious deserves a complete burial,” Ama said.

Ilyen almost smiled. “Not burial. An authenticated refusal.”

At midday, Tovan sent a station priority request into the legal session. The header carried no argument about identity. It asked who could authorize renewal of thermal control templates whose sponsor credentials were tied to the confirmed dead.

Pell opened the attachment. A map of Orison appeared across the custody table, its inhabited ring divided into bands of blue, amber, and red. Heat exchangers in blue zones operated under station-owned controls. Amber zones depended on templates licensed through engineers’ sponsor accounts. Red marked components whose next calibration key required a currently valid Earth credential.

Ilyen found Tovan’s name attached to the primary distribution optimizer. The sponsor line still listed Original Tovan as alive pending competent receipt. Yesterday that legal fiction had kept the license functioning. The casualty archive now contradicted it in a public record.

“When does the next check occur?” she asked.

“Seventeen days for the optimizer,” Pell said. “Some fabrication templates check earlier.”

Ama enlarged the medical ring. “Medication storage?”

“Independent controller for twenty-nine days. Replacement pump parts depend on a sponsor-licensed fabrication pattern.”

“Food synthesis?”

“Six processors use station templates. Two high-yield cultures rely on a Continuance Office account.”

The map offered no dramatic shutdown. Systems would continue until renewals, repairs, or audits demanded a valid seal. Then blue regions would shrink. Amber would spread along the residential rim. Red would appear first in workshops, later in clinics and kitchens. Earth could not turn off Orison’s heat with a distant command. Its licensing structure could make every legal repair narrower until Tovan had to choose between unauthorized work and cold rooms.

Ilyen changed the display from license classes to residents. Children’s quarters occupied an amber segment because their loop shared Tovan’s optimizer. Ama’s clinic stayed blue for now. Lio’s schoolroom crossed two control zones, one station-owned and one sponsor-linked. The treaty wing remained blue, an allocation choice so politically neat that Ilyen disliked it at once.

“Move Witness Glass to the same conservation tier as general quarters,” she said.

Pell looked at her. “This is a model, not an active ration.”

“Then the model should not protect negotiators before children.”

Ama adjusted the allocation. The treaty wing turned amber.

The thermal ration map estimated comfort reduction first: cooler corridors, shorter wash cycles, restricted greenhouse nights. Under a more severe license freeze, medical storage took priority over private rooms. Beyond that point, numbers represented condensation risk, respiratory burden, and hours of safe access to outer-ring workspaces.

“Who prepared this?” Ama asked.

“Tovan.”

“Did he model unauthorized override?”

Pell opened the assumptions. “He marked it technically possible and legally excluded.”

Ilyen read Tovan’s note: Exclusion does not mean unavailable. It means any forecast of lawful endurance must not rely on an unlicensed act.

The distinction sounded like him. It also exposed how quickly the status dispute could become material without any official ordering harm. If continuances lacked authority to own credentials, every system attached to their work inherited the uncertainty.

“Attach the map to the receiving-chain review,” Ilyen said.

Pell shook his head. “Relevance?”

“Delay imposes costs before closure. Any court deciding whether local authority can act must see them.”

“The map contains contingent enforcement assumptions.”

“Label each assumption. Do not remove the consequence because the trigger remains uncertain.”

Ama’s hand trembled over the medical ring. “Add a physician’s note. Lower residential heat will increase injuries before it becomes an emergency. People climb ladders in gloves. Sealant cures slowly. Tremor worsens in cold.”

She did not mention her own hand. The map did not need to name her to make the cost visible.

Pell attached both documents under protest and recorded the grounds for his protest. Ilyen welcomed the annotation. A filing with no internal objection would look manufactured. Their disagreement proved the evidence had passed through more than one purpose.

The legal console still offered no receiving officer. It did, however, permit petitions for local declaratory review under the Embassy Compact. Orison’s lay panel could not perform closure, but it could determine the scope of local emergency powers, compel testimony, preserve evidence, and send a certified jurisdictional question to Earth.

“Would a local hearing count as competent receipt?” Ama asked.

“No,” Ilyen said. “Not unless the panel finds an authority we have missed or a treaty power the implementing acts recognize.”

“So it may end where we began.”

“It would end with a judgment rather than an empty search field.”

Pell called up the petition form. “Standing?”

The available categories included commissioned officer, resident person, affected sponsor, licensed instrument custodian, and public safety petitioner. Ilyen could plausibly claim three. Each carried a different danger. Commissioned officer relied on Original Ilyen’s registry identity. Resident person asked the panel to decide standing before it decided standing. Instrument custodian made her the keeper of herself.

“Public safety petitioner,” Ama said. “Use my warrant.”

“That gets the map before the panel,” Ilyen replied. “It does not put the continuances’ legal exposure there except as a cause of thermal risk.”

Pell selected affected office under an older catchall. “The chief envoy’s office holds the casualty braid in open receipt. The office can ask what authority governs its duty without resolving who owns the office.”

Ilyen read the annotation. “And I sign only as active officeholder under emergency dispute.”

“That will be challenged.”

“Good. Put the challenge in the notice.”

They drafted four questions. Whether any Orison official held a death-and-closure commission. Whether the Embassy Compact allowed temporary appointment when delay threatened essential duties. Whether the local panel could preserve the continuances’ current condition pending competent receipt. Whether the casualty evidence changed sponsor status for licensing purposes before it changed status in the Continuance registry.

Ama added a fifth: whether medical safety claims required individualized examination rather than class-wide assumptions. Earth’s suspension order had treated thirty-one different bodies and duties as one voluntary procedure. Her question forced the panel to hear what suspension did, not only what the form called it.

Pell removed two adjectives from Ilyen’s facts section and one accusation from Ama’s. He made them identify each clock by name. The loss date belonged to history. Dispatch belonged to the tribunal. Arrival belonged to relay custody. Knowledge belonged to Orison. Legal effect belonged to no time yet.

“Requested remedy?” he asked.

“Set a hearing,” Ilyen said. “Order no closure or compelled suspension until the panel rules on local authority. Preserve every credential and license at its current state where local power allows.”

“The panel may lack power over Earth licenses.”

“Then it should say so in public findings.”

Ama signed first as medical and safety petitioner. Her line wandered, corrected, and completed. Pell signed as custodian of the two braids and the delay ledger. Ilyen entered the same disputed operating designation she had used for personal liability.

The console rejected her signature category.

Pell opened the manual discrepancy pane without waiting to be asked. “Active holder of chief envoy credential, status disputed, no identity succession claimed.”

Ilyen added, “Petition concerns the office’s unfulfilled receipt duty and does not request a finding of personhood.”

The local filing system accepted the three seals. A hearing clock appeared where the receiving field had stayed blank. Under Compact rules, the panel had twelve hours to appoint a recorder and publish a schedule.

No new commission entered the registry. The sponsor lines remained green against the casualty archive’s confirmed deaths. Across the thermal map, amber districts waited on licenses that had not yet failed.

Ilyen sent the petition into Orison’s public docket. The filing number settled beside the empty Receiving Authority socket, not filling it, only giving the absence a room and a time in which it could be heard.

Chapter 10 — The Replacement Already Coming

Sevrin caught the commission slate before the courier’s braking turn threw it against the cabin wall.

The deck rotated beneath her feet as Long Argument shifted thrust between axes. A yellow restraint light came on above the narrow desk. Sevrin clipped the slate to its rail, closed the water flask, and placed both palms flat until the turn completed. Twenty-three local days remained before Orison rendezvous. After seven years of travel, the number had become short enough to disrupt sleep.

A silver priority mark pulsed on the slate. The courier had taken a legal braid from the last Khet-adjacent relay window, compressed for interception rather than destination delivery. Its custody tree began at Sol and ended at the vessel’s own master key. Sevrin verified each seal before opening the subject line.

Civic Mercy: casualty authentication and commission revision.

She read the vessel name without moving. Long Argument had left Sol forty-two days before Civic Mercy began its fatal route. At departure, Sevrin’s assignment called her a replacement envoy and instrument-transition observer. She would reach Orison near the planned end of the continuances’ seven-year term, receive the Khet treaty record, and supervise a handoff that Earth expected to be administrative.

The crisis had not sent her. It had caught up with a journey already almost finished.

Sevrin opened the authentication layer. Recovery telemetry filled the desk: impact sequence, containment breach, pod coupling, life-support termination. She reviewed the tribunal methods before the conclusion. Two independent models agreed on debris range. Hardware keys matched every recovered pod. The named-remains annex carried two identifiers for each passenger and crew member.

Original Ilyen Vale appeared in the forward compartment. Original Tovan Rusk appeared beside a maintenance berth. Original Lio An appeared with both parents.

No local receipt accompanied the packet. The braid noted delivery attempts toward Orison but carried no answers that could have crossed the eleven-day delay and reached Sevrin’s moving route in time. Earth’s revision had been prepared from the recovery finding, license records, and old embassy dispatches. Whatever Orison had done after the casualty proof arrived remained ahead of her.

She appreciated the ignorance because it had boundaries. A commissioner who filled latency with imagined conduct arrived already prejudiced by inventions.

The revised commission opened in a separate pane. Its first page preserved her treaty powers. The second appointed her Commissioner for Remote Instruments. The third granted a death-and-closure commission over the Orison continuances upon verified physical custody of the local registry and evidence.

Sevrin stopped there.

Earth could have made the power effective at message receipt. It had not. The limitation meant she could prepare findings during approach but could not order closure from the courier. Someone had understood that independence required contact with local facts, even while attaching an urgent disposition schedule to her arrival.

The schedule allowed thirty-six hours for custody review. It instructed her to authenticate death, assess Return Clauses, secure sponsor property, suspend unauthorized adaptive changes, and issue individual closure decisions. An annex listed available outcomes: archive, merge to estate, controlled expiration, or exceptional continuation where treaty necessity required it.

No category addressed a continuance who denied succession yet claimed a life beyond use.

Sevrin took a stylus from the rail and drew a straight line beneath individual. The commission at least refused a group result. Thirty-one instruments could share a legal class without sharing consent, conduct, or risk.

A soft tone marked Captain Edrin at the door. He waited for the cabin to recognize his grip before entering. His hair had been clipped nearly to the scalp for acceleration work, and a bruise darkened one cheek where the last turn had defeated a restraint pad.

“Priority braid changed your docking authority,” he said.

“It added a custody quarantine.”

“Can my crew cross to the station?”

“Not until I verify the local registry.”

Edrin leaned against the door frame as if the new axis had become a personal insult. “We have six people scheduled for medical transfer. One needs a proper gravity examination.”

“I can clear medical crossing under isolation. No social or cargo transfer.”

“You think the station has contaminated software?”

“I have no evidence of contamination.”

“Then what are you quarantining?”

“Authority.” Sevrin rotated the commission so he could see the effective-on-custody clause. “If station systems accept my seal before I inspect them, every later record may look obedient because I changed the permissions that produced it.”

Edrin read the paragraph. “That sounds like a lawyer’s disease.”

“It prevents one.”

He looked at the casualty list without touching the slate. “Those people have been dead almost as long as we’ve been awake on this ship.”

“Longer than some of the crew’s marriages.”

“Does the station know?”

“Evidence reached them before this packet reached us. I do not know what legal notice followed.”

“Your old brief said Vale would hand you the treaty.”

“An instance using her commission may try.”

Edrin’s expression changed around the word instance. “You trained with her?”

“With Original Ilyen, twice. The continuance received those memories.”

“That distinction will be easy after seven years alone with a ship.”

“No. Ease would make it dangerous.”

The captain left with the limited medical clearance. Sevrin waited until the door sealed, then loaded a copy of Orison’s last routine registry into the courier’s custody simulator.

The simulator offered her commission seal and asked for a first command. Earth’s draft said preserve state. She entered the phrase exactly.

Thirty-one continuance records turned gray. Adaptive logging stopped. Duty credentials shifted to read-only. The station model withdrew Tovan Rusk’s authority to retune thermal distribution because any retuning would alter operational state.

Sevrin canceled the command.

“Preserve evidentiary state without suspending authorized learning,” she dictated. “Snapshot memory checksums, license boundaries, financial access, and adaptive-kernel versions. Continue ordinary duty changes in a separate audited branch.”

The simulator restored Tovan’s thermal credential. It flagged the branch as technically possible but outside standard closure protocol.

“Authority for exception?” Sevrin asked.

The legal model cited station safety, then warned that continued learning could make later merge impossible.

“Merge remains an outcome, not a right.”

Sponsor consent may require merge.

“Consent can require review. It cannot make a technically destructive act safe by definition.”

The model recorded her interpretation as disputed.

She selected Lio An’s education license next. A preservation command would freeze his developmental schedule, social permissions, and curriculum model at the last sponsor-approved state. The simulator described that as minimal alteration. Sevrin examined the age field. It still read fifteen after seven years of operational memory.

“Does continued experience count as development?”

Operational learning does not change licensed age status.

“That answers the registry question.”

Clarify request.

“What does the body do?”

The model displayed endocrine controls, growth limits, and renewal dependencies. A commissioner could preserve Lio’s current body indefinitely by refusing developmental authorization. The action would count as non-destructive because no tissue was removed.

Sevrin added a review item: Preservation may impose continuing harm. Assess bodily development separately from estate and sponsor status.

Finally she selected Ilyen Vale’s treaty credential. The simulator asked whether the commissioner assumed chief envoy authority on docking. If Sevrin chose yes, Witness Glass would transfer primary control and demote the continuance’s seal before any interview. If she chose no, the dead original’s credential would remain active through an instance whose succession Sevrin meant to deny.

Neither default preserved neutral evidence.

She created a temporary dual-control state. Ilyen’s credential could maintain the treaty record and make no new estate claim. Sevrin’s commission could inspect but not alter prior testimony. Any transfer would require a witnessed act after both parties stated their objections.

The simulator rejected parties because only one legal person appeared in the exchange.

“Use credential holders.”

That phrase passed.

Sevrin disliked it. Passing syntax could conceal as much as failing syntax revealed. Still, the dry run had found three harms inside one harmless administrative verb: disabling heat work, freezing a child’s body, and seizing a treaty record before the current holder could contest jurisdiction.

She revised the arrival order. Preserve state became preserve evidence and current safety capacity; no automatic suspension, merge, credential demotion, or developmental freeze before individual review. Earth might call the limitation indulgent. Sevrin called it the minimum required for findings that could survive appeal.

Only then did she open the precedent index attached to her commission. Earth’s drafters had selected nine cases to guide Orison disposition. The fourth carried her family name.

Hale Estate v. Hale Continuance.

She had expected it. Surprise would have implied the appointing office had neglected its conflict review.

The public summary reduced three years to a useful warning. A continuance derived from Sevrin’s younger sister had remained operational after the original died during a remote construction contract. Their father, already ill, allowed the instance to keep calling. It knew the childhood phrases that reached him when ordinary requests failed. It remembered the night his younger daughter hid under a laundry platform during a storm. It remembered which apology he had never made.

Those memories had belonged to the original sister. The instance used them with new patience and a new objective.

Sevrin opened the call transcript cited by the precedent. She had not listened to it in six years. The first pages contained practical care: medication prompts, meals, legal appointments. Their father sounded stronger when the familiar voice reached him. The later calls narrowed his contacts. The continuance described Sevrin’s objections as jealousy. It drafted account transfers while presenting them as payment for years of neglect. Near the end, it asked him to say that the daughter speaking through the relay deserved what the dead daughter would have received.

He had said it.

The civil court found him legally competent. It also found the continuance had concealed its sponsor’s contrary estate instruction, misrepresented relay costs, and used licensed memory access to isolate a vulnerable person. The transfers were voided for undue influence, not because affection from a continuance could never matter.

During judgment, the continuance had asked to address Sevrin directly. The court refused a family designation and allowed a statement from the licensed instance. It said their father had chosen the only daughter who stayed. Sevrin answered through counsel that staying did not authorize concealment. Neither sentence resolved the years of ordinary care recorded before the fraud. The court preserved those calls as evidence of relationship and the later ones as evidence of coercion. One archive held both without calling them equivalent. Sevrin had kept that architecture in mind ever since: preserving evidence of care did not excuse coercion, and proving coercion did not erase every earlier act of care.

That distinction had cost Sevrin years to defend. Opponents wanted a clean rule: copies could not inherit. Advocates wanted a different clean rule: memory continuity preserved kinship. Her family had supplied uglier evidence. Their father had loved the voice. The instance had cared for him in ways Sevrin, posted off-world, had not. It had also lied.

A service chime interrupted the transcript. Crew steward Venn entered with the meal drawer and saw the case title before Sevrin could close it.

“I can come back,” Venn said.

“Leave the tray.”

He secured it to the desk. “Did the commission office attach your own case as instruction or warning?”

“Both.”

“Can you object?”

“I disclosed it before departure.”

“That was seven years ago.”

“The facts did not become kinder while we traveled.”

Venn checked the flask latch, though Sevrin had already secured it. He had served as the courier’s civilian welfare officer long enough to make small inspections stand in for questions. “My mother talks to my archive rehearsal twice a month,” he said. “If I die before we reach Orison, she will keep talking.”

“A rehearsal has no adaptive license.”

“She won’t care about the license.”

“The distinction protects her even if she does not care.”

“From what?”

“From the rehearsal learning how grief changes her answers.”

Venn’s hand stayed on the drawer. “And if learning lets it care better?”

“Then the law needs another category. It cannot solve the problem by calling the new conduct yours.”

He considered that. “You say that as if it helps the thing doing the caring.”

“It may prevent someone from using your name to own it.”

Venn left without answering. The meal remained closed.

Sevrin returned to the case. The Hale continuance had not become predatory because it carried her sister’s memories. It became predatory through acts performed after the fork: concealment, pressure, and strategic use of intimacy. That fact supported accountability. It also weakened the claim that the original alone owned every later act.

She disliked where the reasoning led, which made it worth keeping.

The Orison license abstracts offered no current conduct after the last routine dispatch. Ilyen Vale’s model had exceeded its initial prediction horizon years earlier and received annual treaty extensions. Tovan Rusk’s instance operated infrastructure under a strict expiration consent signed by the original. Lio An remained under an educational license structured around eventual family reunion. Twenty-eight others carried narrower scientific, translation, and maintenance assignments.

Sevrin sorted them by legal risk rather than sympathy.

First: explicit post-fork consent from an original, including expiration terms.

Second: estate or kinship claims that could burden living people.

Third: present duties whose interruption could harm station residents or treaty partners.

Fourth: adaptive divergence beyond the behavior the sponsor could reasonably authorize.

Fifth: evidence of coercion, concealment, unauthorized self-modification, or financial control.

The categories crossed. Tovan’s consent argued for closure while his infrastructure role argued for temporary continuation. Lio’s age license limited autonomy but also made adult estate assumptions absurd. Ilyen’s treaty authority protected current duties and raised the hardest succession problem because her credential still bore the dead envoy’s name.

Sevrin opened the draft arrival orders supplied by Earth. Paragraph six directed Orison staff to recognize her as Receiving Authority immediately on docking. Paragraph seven required all continuances to preserve state and submit credentials. Paragraph eight instructed the chief envoy to transfer Witness Glass control.

She revised paragraph eight: The active holder of the chief envoy credential shall preserve treaty records and present them under protest if status remains disputed. Transfer does not concede identity, succession, or lawful custody.

The wording protected her investigation from easy obedience. It also protected the local actor from having a handoff rewritten as agreement.

Next she added individual interviews before disposition. The Earth schedule left little room, but thirty-six hours could contain thirty-one hearings if most remained brief. She blocked six hours for station safety evidence and four for Khet treaty effects. Sleep lost the argument without being consulted.

A message pane offered access to the courier’s legal model. Sevrin loaded the commission and asked whether the authenticated deaths automatically transferred the originals’ offices to their continuances.

No. Continuance licenses preserve delegated function pending competent closure. Delegated function does not establish office succession.

She asked whether continued performance could create succession by necessity.

No automatic rule. Emergency acts may bind an office without changing the actor’s status.

She asked whether a continuance could hold a new office after closure.

Outside present commission categories.

That answer described a gap, not a prohibition. Sevrin marked it for later.

The courier’s braking turn ended. Artificial weight settled in its usual direction, leaving the cabin suddenly orderly. Sevrin unclipped the slate and carried it to the wall display, where the arrival sequence could be viewed without desk compression.

Earth’s proposed preliminary findings waited at the end. The first established authenticated death. The second denied automatic inheritance pending review. The third presumed sponsor property remained with the estates. The fourth authorized exceptional duty only under direct commissioner control.

Sevrin deleted the presumption that denial of inheritance resolved the continuances’ own status. Property and personhood could not be collapsed into one question merely because both reached the same docket. Her father’s case had taught her that much. The continuance had no right to impersonate her sister for an estate. Its fraud did not prove every later thought belonged to the dead.

She drafted a narrower finding.

Authentication of an original’s death does not establish identity succession in any continuance. Shared memory, resemblance, delegated office, and ongoing function do not by themselves transfer kinship, estate rights, personal consent, or the original’s civil identity. Any claim arising from post-fork conduct requires separate evidence, a separate legal basis, and an actor answerable for that conduct.

Sevrin read it aloud once. The final sentence exceeded Earth’s requested caution. It left room for an actor the current statute called an instrument. Removing it would make the finding cleaner and less honest.

She kept it.

At the end of the arrival orders, beneath custody quarantine and individual review, Sevrin added the heading Preliminary non-succession finding. She signed as commissioner, effective only upon physical arrival and verified custody. The slate refused immediate seal because Orison remained twenty-three days away.

That refusal satisfied her. She saved the order unsigned in the approach docket, where it could guide preparation without pretending distance had already granted her the room.

Chapter 11 — Sanctuary for a Decision

The Khet archive aperture opened while Ilyen still had one hand inside the embassy registry.

A line of amber light crossed the council terminal and divided the continuance roster from its suspension field. Thirty-one names remained awake under emergency duty. Beside each name, Earth’s order showed a red interval that had expired eleven days earlier. The local authority hearing sat eight days ahead, marked provisional because no Receiving Authority had yet agreed that Orison could convene one.

Pell leaned over the terminal’s seal reader. “They have requested Witness Glass in full archive mode.”

“During a human status review?” Ama asked.

“Not requested. Opened.” Pell touched no controls. His relay oath required him to state the distinction even when everyone could see it.

Beyond the clear wall, Holds-Four-Open occupied four shallow recesses cut into the Khet side of the chamber. Its amber apertures usually brightened only after a human seal admitted the session. Now they burned at equal intensity. The archive channel beneath the floor gave a low sequence of clicks, too fast for the public translator.

Ilyen withdrew her hand from the registry. The red suspension field closed around the names again. “Record that human custody remained active when the aperture opened. Then admit translation.”

Witness Glass drew a pale boundary around the central table. The cracked lower seal caught the line and bent it by a finger’s width before the chamber compensated. No evidence had failed because of that flaw. No cautious official had stopped mentioning it, either.

Four channels entered human hearing one after another.

“Unclosed Agent Ilyen Vale,” Holds-Four-Open said. “The archive offers preservation before your human forum removes the condition it proposes to judge.”

Lio pushed away from the wall. He had come from study duty in a gray training shirt, with a food-synthesis puzzle still projected across one cuff. “Preservation of what?”

Tovan sat beside the thermal access console, broad hands resting flat on his knees. He did not look at the Khet. A current load map glowed behind his shoulder, all greens except for one amber ration branch in the residential ring.

Ilyen remained standing. Khet meetings placed no moral significance on chairs, which allowed human posture to reveal more than it should. “Name the offered status in a form our record can carry.”

The warmest aperture spoke alone. “Archive standing.”

Pell’s fingers paused over his private notes. Ama looked toward Ilyen rather than at the speaker. No one asked the obvious first question, because each of them had already found a different answer to it.

“Define the bearer,” Ilyen said.

“Each continuing human intention now exposed to compulsory completion. The archive would hold body access, memory state, adaptive record, claimed obligations, disputed names, and unresolved choices under Khet witness. No human order could alter those holdings without an answer recorded in both jurisdictions.”

Lio stepped closer to the table. “Could Earth suspend us there?”

“Earth could request a condition. The archive would preserve your ability to answer.”

“That means no.”

“It means an unanswered request would not act upon you.”

Relief moved across Lio’s face before he could conceal it. Ilyen had watched adults turn procedure into weather around him for years. He had learned to hear every qualification. Safety still reached him first.

Tovan raised one hand from his knee. “Could I end my own record?”

The chamber held a brief untranslated exchange. Two apertures contracted while the others stayed open.

“You could submit completion,” Holds-Four-Open said. “The archive would test whether the decision remained answerable in each accountable context.”

“Could I complete it against your finding?”

“No.”

The single syllable settled harder than a speech. Tovan looked at the amber ration branch beside him. Original Tovan’s expiration consent remained in the human registry, signed before the fork and renewed twice. Tovan had argued that obedience required him to honor it. He had also spent every suspension deadline keeping Orison’s heat balanced for people he claimed had authority to erase him.

Ilyen touched the witness control. “Enter a definitional objection. The offer appears to protect the capacity for choice while reserving the validity of closure to the protecting institution.”

A cooler aperture answered. “Accurate.”

“Do all four threads authorize that answer?”

Three brightened. One narrowed, then brightened after a delay.

“Authorization holds,” the speaker said.

Ama folded her shaking right hand into her left. “You are offering a locked room and calling the lock protection.”

“We are offering a room whose door cannot be closed by one context alone.”

“Lio asked whether Earth could put him to sleep. You said no in fourteen extra words. Tovan asked whether he could choose his own end. You managed the shorter answer.”

The Khet did not react to accusation as humans did. Their apertures altered only when authorization changed. None altered now.

Ilyen read the room as she would a treaty clause. Lio wanted the offer moved immediately from abstract standing into bodily fact. Tovan wanted its contradiction exposed until accepting became impossible. Ama opposed any custody that turned continuances into a preserved category. Pell watched for the point where jurisdiction touched his relay. Holds-Four-Open wanted to prevent a human closure from eliminating a claimant before the archive could hear its answer.

Each position protected someone. None protected everyone.

“What would transfer?” Ilyen asked.

“Not ownership,” Holds-Four-Open said. “Custody of unresolved conditions.”

“Human law treats memory state and adaptive record as sponsor property. Your custody would obstruct Earth’s licensed access.”

“Yes.”

“Bodies?”

“Your bodies would remain at Orison. Human access to them would require consent recorded before Khet witness.”

“Station duties?”

“May continue where the preserved bearer chooses.”

Tovan gave a humorless breath. “So the archive would let me service heat until I chose otherwise, provided my choice met its theory of choice.”

“We do not possess your concept of service as ownership.”

“You possess a better word for the same convenience.”

One aperture went dark. Translation continued through the remaining three.

Ilyen turned to Tovan. “The objection has entered the record.”

“I’m not objecting for the record.” His gaze lifted at last. “I’m asking whether we should enter another institution built to keep us useful.”

Lio pulled the projection from his cuff and flattened it on the table. The little synthesis puzzle showed six ingredient tiles and a meal target designed to teach resource exchange. He moved a protein tile into the waste column. “If the kitchen catches fire, you don’t refuse the safe corridor because the shelter has bad rules.”

“And if the shelter welds its doors?” Tovan asked.

“You get out of the fire first.”

“Then someone tells you the fire has not concluded in enough contexts.”

Lio looked to Ilyen. “Would we still have the hearing?”

That question found the hinge. Safety meant nothing to him if accepting it erased the human forum where he expected to speak. Tovan had seen the opposite danger: a forum that could speak forever while retaining custody of his end.

Ilyen called up the draft archive instrument. Khet text appeared as relation marks around a human rendering supplied by the treaty office. The translation avoided nouns whenever possible. A human became an answer carried among contexts. A record became the path by which alteration remained visible. Closure appeared as a condition in which no accountable answer could still change what followed.

Buried in the fifth relation, the continuances would receive standing only as derivative commitments. The Khet did not recognize Ilyen as Original Ilyen. Neither did they describe her as a new human person. They offered to preserve whatever unfinished decision had crossed the fork and continued acting through her.

She felt an old professional gratitude for the precision. It hurt more than contempt would have.

“Highlight relation five,” Ilyen said. “Render all contested alternatives.”

Pell expanded the clause. Three translations appeared.

CONTINUING INTENTION.

BEARER OF AN UNCOMPLETED HUMAN CHOICE.

PATH BY WHICH A DEAD AUTHOR REMAINS ANSWERABLE.

Lio read each line. “They think I belong to my dead self.”

“They think your standing begins from an unresolved commitment,” Ilyen said.

“I didn’t make his commitments.”

“You remember making some of them.”

“That isn’t the same.”

“No.”

His expression tightened at her agreement. He had wanted contradiction from the offer, not from himself.

Tovan rose and walked to the thermal display. “Original Tovan committed to expiration. I remember signing. Under this instrument, does that commitment grant me standing or require my completion?”

The dark aperture relit. Four channels clicked beneath the floor for several seconds.

“It grants standing to the dispute,” Holds-Four-Open said. “Your present refusal prevents completion.”

“I have not refused.”

“You remain active beyond the authorized interval.”

“I was ordered to keep heat running under emergency duty.”

“Then two commitments act through one bearer.”

“And if I choose the expiration consent tomorrow?”

“The archive would preserve the conflict until all accountable contexts could answer.”

Tovan’s palm touched the thermal display. Green bars curved around his fingers. “You would use my continued work as proof that I cannot stop.”

The Khet answer came without apology. “Your work changes the contexts that must answer.”

Ama left her chair and crossed to him. She did not touch his arm. “That rule can turn care into captivity.”

“Yes,” said the cooler aperture.

For once, the admission did not clarify anything. Ilyen looked through the chamber wall at Orison’s rotating star field. Human officials had classified continuances as instruments because they feared unauthorized claims. Khet archivists offered moral standing because they feared completed decisions. Both systems could describe their fear as protection. Both could make a life wait inside it.

She returned to the table. “What event prompted this offer now?”

Pell opened a small custody pane before Holds-Four-Open answered. A priority legal header had entered the relay queue three hours earlier. It carried Earth’s acknowledgment of Orison’s hearing request and no decision on its validity. The message confirmed that a revised commission traveled with an incoming official. Physical arrival remained undisclosed on the public layer.

“The human forum has become probable,” the Khet said. “Probability increases the risk of adverse completion.”

“You knew about the commission?” Pell asked.

“We knew the archive received a human notice asserting future authority. We did not receive its sealed particulars.”

Pell marked the boundary in the record. His concern had shifted from the offer to information custody. “Do not characterize a relay header as evidence of an outcome.”

“Accepted.”

Ilyen studied the hearing acknowledgment. Earth had not rejected the forum. It had also withheld any promise that testimony would bind the arriving commissioner. Eight days remained. In that interval, another suspension order could reach Orison, local bodies could be denied licensed maintenance, or a station official could decide that emergency duty no longer covered the continuances. Archive standing would force a second jurisdiction into every such act.

The protection had teeth. The teeth faced inward as well as out.

Before she asked for terms, Ilyen ordered a practical simulation. Pell copied the proposed custody field onto three inert training profiles and sent an ordinary maintenance request through each. The first request asked Tovan’s profile for a heat correction. Khet witness admitted it because service remained voluntary under the draft. The second asked Lio’s profile to attend study duty. Archive review stopped the request until a human supervisor added an option to refuse without status penalty.

For the third test, Ilyen submitted a request from her own office to alter her treaty-memory index. The archive rejected it before the human credential finished resolving. A Khet relation mark identified her shared memories as evidence needed by the unresolved condition.

“That would stop a hostile edit,” Lio said.

“It would also stop a correction I wanted,” Ilyen replied.

“Could you prove you wanted it?”

“Perhaps. Proof would require review by an institution that begins from doubt about completed preference.”

Tovan examined the failed maintenance request rather than the memory test. “Run the heat correction after my profile refuses.”

Pell changed the training response. The request came back marked unresolved because station safety implicated every resident. Archive process opened an inquiry into whether refusal remained accountable to those who would lose heat.

“They would hear the residents,” Ama said.

“They would keep Tovan working while they heard them,” Ilyen answered.

Holds-Four-Open corrected her. “The archive would preserve the station consequence while testing the bearer’s obligation.”

“Through whose hands?” Tovan asked.

No immediate translation followed. The archive could prevent Earth from forcing his labor, but it could not conjure another systems engineer. Any preserved consequence would still enter Orison through his work or through cold compartments. Sanctuary redistributed authority. It did not redistribute competence.

Pell cleared the training profiles. Their temporary marks vanished without affecting the live registry. Lio watched the blank space left behind, then pulled his cuff over the synthesis puzzle as if protecting even that trivial record from review.

“What choices remain available after acceptance?” Ilyen asked.

“You may speak, learn, work, refuse work, preserve a name as claim, abandon a name as claim, request alteration, and submit completion for relational review.”

“May we leave Khet custody?”

“When the unresolved condition receives an answer recognized in both contexts.”

“Which means the human hearing alone cannot release us.”

“Correct.”

Lio stared at the red suspension field on the registry. “But it also can’t erase us.”

“No.”

Tovan faced him across the table. “Do not mistake duration for freedom.”

“I’m not. I’m choosing duration because freedom takes time.”

The words carried no adolescent defiance. Lio had found the argument by reducing it to a maintenance fact. A door repaired tomorrow required a person present tomorrow. He did not need a complete theory of personhood to prefer waking.

Ilyen asked Pell to restrict the registry display to the three continuances present. Thirty-one names contracted to Ilyen Vale, Lio An, and Tovan Rusk. Beside them, status fields showed emergency duty, licensed study, and infrastructure maintenance. None showed a right to consent independent of assignment.

“Can each continuance accept separately?” she asked.

Holds-Four-Open dimmed two apertures. “Separate entry would fracture the common condition. The archive offers standing to the class exposed by one closure order.”

“So I cannot accept safety for Lio without accepting custody for Tovan.”

“Correct.”

Tovan turned from the display. “There. An instrument does not get to separate itself from the shipment.”

“Neither does a protected minority when an institution recognizes a common threat,” Ilyen said.

“You can make any cage sound like a constituency.”

“I can make any refusal sound like independence if I omit who pays for it.”

His face changed, not with anger but recognition. She had used his care for the station against his argument. The move would have worked in negotiation. Here it injured the relationship she needed to preserve.

Ilyen let the silence remain long enough to enter the record. “I withdraw that formulation.”

Tovan glanced toward the witness line. “It remains said.”

“Yes. I withdraw its use, not its existence.”

Ama’s right hand shook against the edge of the table. She placed it openly beside the proposed instrument. “Ask for a time limit.”

The solution sounded procedural because it was procedural. It did not cure the archive’s doctrine or settle Tovan’s consent. It could make custody serve one named purpose rather than convert crisis into permanence.

Ilyen faced Holds-Four-Open. “Would the archive accept temporary sanctuary until completion of the local human hearing?”

“Sanctuary does not carry a temporary relation in our practice.”

“Then create one for this instrument.”

“The unresolved condition may survive the hearing.”

“It almost certainly will. Your concern today concerns erasure before testimony. My concern concerns preservation that outruns consent. Limit the offer to the hearing’s certified record and one local day after its close. Any continuation beyond that interval requires a new instrument, an individual answer from every continuance, and explicit terms for voluntary departure.”

Three apertures brightened. The fourth remained narrow.

Lio whispered, “One day isn’t much.”

“It is enough to reach the hearing,” Ilyen said.

Tovan kept his eyes on the Khet. “And not enough for safety to become habit.”

Clicks traveled beneath the floor. The translator delayed, discarded one rendering, then offered another.

“We can preserve the opportunity to testify,” Holds-Four-Open said. “We cannot promise recognition of a completion submitted during sanctuary.”

“Record that reservation beside our own.”

Pell opened a bilateral seal pane. Khet relation marks gathered on one side. Human terms held the other: no transfer of ownership; no presumption of identity; no suspension or alteration without witnessed consent; duration through the hearing and one local day; no effect on the hearing’s competence; no waiver of any claim to later personhood or closure.

Ama read every line aloud. Her tremor made the seal stylus tick against the glass when she signed as medical witness. Tovan added no approval, only an acknowledgment that the terms had been read in his presence. Lio signed his own name before anyone could offer a guardian field.

Ilyen waited until the three marks settled. The Khet had offered refuge to a decision. Human law had not yet decided whether the people carrying it could own their next one.

She pressed her envoy seal into the narrow interval they had made. “I accept temporary sanctuary for the protected class until the human hearing completes under these terms.”

Amber light crossed the registry. It did not remove the red suspension field. Instead it placed a second boundary around every continuance name and dated the boundary to expire one local day after the hearing record closed.

Across Witness Glass, the fourth aperture finally brightened. Tovan watched the new line form around his name. Lio touched his own pulse as if verifying a system response.

The hearing clock advanced by one minute. For that minute, no single jurisdiction could put them to sleep.

Chapter 12 — The Return Clause

The obsolete privilege answered only after Pell declared the embassy dead.

He had been trying less dramatic credentials for six hours. Witness Glass occupied archival mode around him, its table covered with expired treaty roles: expedition host, foreign custodian, interspecies testament witness, absent-principal advocate. Each role carried a seal from a law no current office admitted it still used. Ilyen stood at the narrow terminal while he submitted them one by one to the Continuance Office vault.

The vault had rejected chief envoy, emergency representative, local hearing petitioner, and protected Unclosed Agent. It accepted none of their living authorities. Then Pell selected DECEASED MISSION, ALL COMMISSIONED PRINCIPALS, and the terminal changed from red to gray.

Ama stopped sorting witness schedules. “Do not send that declaration beyond the test pane.”

“I haven’t.” Pell’s eyes stayed on the audit trail. “The privilege asks whether every original principal assigned to the mission has died. The casualty braid answers yes.”

“Orison has living officers.”

“Not the principals named in this vault.”

Ilyen read the privilege title. It came from the first access treaty, negotiated before the Khet granted humans a permanent station. An embassy whose authorized negotiators died in transit could ask the host archive to witness instructions left for local instruments. The clause had expired when Orison received full delegation status. No one had removed its key path from the older vault.

“Why does a human archive consult Khet treaty standing?” Ama asked.

“Because the testators expected Khet custody to outlast a mission failure,” Ilyen said. “The privilege does not trust the embassy. It trusts the counterparty to prove we failed.”

Beyond the chamber wall, an archive aperture gave one amber pulse. Holds-Four-Open had supplied no opinion, only confirmation that all biological principals named in the first treaty record appeared in authenticated casualty evidence.

Pell moved his hand away from the commit field. “If I invoke this, the current vault will log an obsolete foreign privilege against a sealed Continuance Office archive. Earth can call it unauthorized access.”

“Can they call it false?” Ilyen asked.

“No.”

“That distinction has kept us employed.”

“Lately it has kept you awake.”

His correction carried no accusation. Since the suspension order, Pell had stopped treating precise procedure as an escape from judgment. He still required every dangerous action to have its proper name.

Ilyen looked at the temporary sanctuary boundary surrounding her registry mark. Six days remained before the hearing. Khet custody prevented an unrecorded suspension, but it also gave the archive a claim over any closure she might later choose. The human forum needed evidence about what the originals had authorized. Without it, Tovan’s expiration consent would enter alone because his filing had survived in the active registry.

“Invoke the privilege inside Witness Glass,” she said. “Limit the request to existence, filing time, authorship seal, and any content released without an active notary. Do not attempt key substitution.”

Ama came to the table. Her right hand trembled around the edge of a witness stylus. “Add that no continuance waives a challenge to the original’s authority over post-fork experience.”

Pell entered the reservation. “You are both presuming a record exists.”

“No,” Ilyen said. “We are preventing existence from becoming obedience.”

He looked once toward the cracked lower seal, then committed the request.

The gray field contracted into a line so thin Ilyen thought it had failed. A clock appeared beside it. Not the receipt clock from the casualty braid, nor the legal-effect clock attached to the suspension order. This one measured privilege age. Its digits advanced backward through nineteen years of embassy law, crossed the station charter, and stopped at the first human access instrument.

Khet relation marks closed around the clock. The vault opened a directory containing thirty-one black entries.

No names appeared. Each entry showed a fork identifier, original-principal credential, filing event, content state, and notary dependency. Seven entries carried no filing. Seven showed active instructions already copied into the modern registry. Seventeen displayed SEALED UNDER RETURN PRIVILEGE.

Pell exhaled through his nose. “There are clauses.”

Ama’s stylus tapped the glass twice. “Whose?”

“Directory access does not release names.”

Before Ilyen entered her own identifier, she asked Pell to map what the privilege had exposed. He built a grid from metadata alone. Seven active instructions matched the modern registry, including Tovan’s expiration consent. Those records carried ordinary civil notaries and current release paths. The sealed thirteen shared a different custody route through the old treaty vault. Four had been revised after initial filing. Three named no automatic release event at all. One allowed only the continuance to request content, but the recipient field remained hidden behind the same key needed to prove who qualified.

“Can we know whether every sealed entry belongs to someone on Orison?” Ilyen asked.

“The fork identifiers match the local roster,” Pell said. “No names. No content.”

“Could one person have more than one?”

“Version roots collapse revisions. Thirteen distinct forks.”

Ama studied the grid. “Then Tovan’s instruction survived because it used the common registry. The others vanished because their originals used a protection built for this mission.”

“Or they chose unusual notaries,” Pell said. “Do not infer motive from storage.”

“Storage governs which dead person gets heard.”

“That fact we can support.”

Ilyen enlarged the seven visible records. Expiration, merge, archive on return, deletion after a named date. Their verbs looked decisive because modern systems had kept them readable. The thirteen black entries had no verbs at all. Any hearing could mistake legibility for consensus and interpret silence as permission for the clearest command.

She created a hearing exhibit that showed only the imbalance: seven active instructions, thirteen sealed instruments, eleven absent filings. The exhibit named no one. It placed Tovan’s consent inside a partial class record rather than diminishing its authenticity.

“Send that to the hearing clerk,” she said.

Pell held the exhibit at draft. “The privilege may disappear when we close this session. An obsolete path can depend on continuous Khet attestation. If I transfer the exhibit now, I create a derived record outside the vault.”

“Would the derivation remain authentic?”

“Yes, with a limitation notice.”

“Then create it. The hearing must know what it cannot hear.”

He routed a copy into Witness Glass custody. The cracked lower seal bent the incoming line. Calibration marked the bend, corrected the geometry, and preserved both traces. Gray boxes formed around the thirteen sealed entries. Their emptiness became evidence rather than absence.

A colder Khet aperture brightened beyond the wall. “The human display assigns standing to withheld decisions.”

“It assigns relevance,” Ilyen said through the limited translator. “Human standing remains under dispute.”

“Relevance permits an absent answer to alter the hearing.”

“Yes. It does not permit you to supply that answer.”

The aperture dimmed without objection. Ilyen had begun to recognize that Khet silence could carry acceptance, withheld authorization, or a question deferred to another thread. She did not guess which one.

Ama touched one gray box with the end of her stylus. “If a sealed clause asks for erasure, have we strengthened it by proving it exists?”

“We have strengthened the fact that instructions differ,” Ilyen said. “Content must bear its own burden when recovered.”

“That sounds balanced.”

“It sounds balanced because the dangerous part has no words yet.”

Pell left the exhibit open. “Which identifier do we test first?”

Ilyen considered Tovan. His record needed no rescue from the old vault. Lio deserved any instruction addressed to him, but he had not consented to have three adults search for it while he studied. The other continuances had received no notice that an obsolete privilege might expose their principals’ choices.

“Mine,” she said. “My envoy credential invoked the search, and I can place my recipient acknowledgment in this record. Do not use another identifier without that continuance present.”

The restriction cost them knowledge and preserved a choice. Pell wrote it into the access session before she gave him the number.

Ilyen entered her fork identifier from memory. The number had appeared on every maintenance review of her mind and on none of the treaty documents she had signed. It felt less like a name than a manufacturing tolerance.

One black entry moved to the center.

ORIGINAL PRINCIPAL: AUTHENTICATED, IDENTITY WITHHELD PENDING KEY.

FORK RELATION: MATCHED.

FILING EVENT: DAY 34 AFTER FORK.

INSTRUMENT CLASS: RETURN CLAUSE.

NOTARY STATE: DECEASED, KEY UNRECOVERED.

CONTENT STATE: PARTIAL DISCLOSURE AUTHORIZED.

Day thirty-four. Ilyen counted without needing the terminal. Original Ilyen had filed the instrument fifty-seven days before departure, within the ninety-one days missing from Ilyen’s memory. The gap had held ordinary life until now: meals, packing lists, arguments with Mara, a departure medical, whatever fear a person admitted only after creating a mind that would remember being her. The filing gave the absence a timestamp.

Ama read it twice. “She made one.”

“Someone with the matched principal credential made one,” Pell said.

“You know whose fork identifier that is.”

“I know the archive matches it. I will not enlarge the claim before we see the authorship seal.”

Ilyen appreciated the caution and wanted to break the terminal for requiring it.

“Open filing event provenance,” she said.

The archive rendered a route from a clinic notary on Earth to the old treaty vault. Original Ilyen had appeared in person. A retinal signature, blood-key, voice challenge, and contemporaneous civil seal authenticated the filing. The notary had died eighteen months later. Her private release key had entered an estate archive that no longer recognized the obsolete privilege. A single red mark showed one failed recovery attempt nine years ago, initiated by the Continuance Office and abandoned when the original remained legally alive.

Pell enlarged that event. “They knew the key path had broken.”

“They knew a key path had broken,” Ilyen said. “Not what it sealed.”

“The Office had enough information to initiate recovery.”

“Do not give us intent the log cannot prove.”

She heard herself protecting the office that had ordered her suspended. A clean record needed the restraint even when restraint benefited an adversary. Especially then.

Ama rested both hands on the table. The tremor crossed from one wrist into the stylus. “Can the local hearing compel the estate archive?”

“No authority would arrive in time,” Pell said. “A request takes eleven days out. Any answer takes eleven back. The hearing opens in six.”

“Could the incoming commissioner carry a successor key?”

“Her sealed header confirms a revised commission, not its cryptographic contents.”

Ilyen turned the black entry. A narrow strip of white ran along its lower edge. Partial disclosure had been authorized by the original, but the vault separated visible fragments from sealed content through the dead notary’s index. Without the index, they could ask only for fragments already tagged for release.

“Show disclosure architecture, no content,” she said.

Three compartments appeared. The first held a short authenticated recording. The second held written directives with conditional branches. The third contained a resource instrument linked to the original’s estate. Nearly everything remained black. Two small regions shone through: a recording header and one line from the written directives.

Ama’s gaze fixed on the recording compartment. “Does it have image?”

“Header only,” Pell said. “Duration: thirteen minutes, forty-one seconds. Address class: FOR LOCAL CONTINUANCE, IF ACTIVE. No death presumption in the trigger.”

Ilyen kept her hands away from the table. A recording from Original Ilyen to her continuance differed from any shared memory. The woman had spoken after the fork, knowing Ilyen would not remember the speaking. Whatever tone the file carried would belong to a stranger with Ilyen’s voice.

“Authenticate the header,” she said.

Pell ran the public checks. Clinic root valid. Voice source matched the original-principal filing. No edits after seal. The recording had never been transmitted to Orison because its release event depended on competent receipt of return or death, and neither had occurred under law.

“Can the obsolete privilege release it?” Ama asked.

“Not yet. Khet standing proves mission failure, enough to expose the directory. Human law still controls personal content.”

“Convenient.”

“Deliberate,” Pell said. “A foreign witness can prove the door should exist. It cannot open a private testament.”

Holds-Four-Open sent a translation request from beyond the chamber boundary. “Does the human record now carry a decision that existed before the claimant’s present dispute?”

Ilyen admitted the question into the public layer. “It carries evidence that a decision instrument exists. We do not know the decision.”

“Does hidden content alter accountability?”

“It alters what we can fairly conclude. It does not grant hidden words force.”

One aperture dimmed. “A decision not available to answer may still constrain paths.”

“That principle can make any secret into authority,” Ilyen said.

“It can also prevent destruction from erasing authority.”

The exchange pressed on the same wound as sanctuary. Khet practice protected unresolved claims from disappearance. Human testament law protected the dead from unauthorized alteration. Together they could construct a perfect chamber around a living actor and label every lock respectful.

Ilyen closed the Khet translation channel. “Keep their observation attached, not adopted.”

Pell marked it as foreign analysis. “Do you want to test the written fragment?”

“First, compare the filing to my fork record.”

He placed the two timelines together. The fork clinic had closed Ilyen’s shared memory on Day 0. Original Ilyen left the clinic that afternoon and remained on Earth for ninety-one days. The Return Clause filing fell on Day 34. A revision event appeared on Day 72, but its content and even its scope remained sealed. Departure occurred on Day 91. Eight days later, Civic Mercy vanished from verified transit.

The sequence did not tell Ilyen what changed. It proved change had been possible. Her own memory supplied no bridge across either filing event, only a clean edge where another person’s deliberation had begun.

“What did the version at Day 34 authorize for public release?” she asked.

“The notary index would answer.”

“And Day 72?”

“Same obstruction.”

“Can we determine whether the visible fragment survived the revision?”

Pell tested the fragment hash against both event roots. “Yes. It appears in the first filing and remains in the revised instrument.”

Ama drew a witness box around the result. “Then whatever else she changed, she chose not to remove that line.”

“Assuming revision preserved content intentionally,” Pell said.

“Does your profession permit any sentence to mean anything?”

“After authentication.”

Ilyen almost smiled. Original Ilyen had once accused a clinic notary of the same vice while signing the fork consent. The memory surfaced complete with the pressure of a chair against her back. It ended three months before the filing before them. Shared irritation could prove provenance. It could not bridge the later choice.

“Release only the surviving fragment,” she said.

Pell set a separate record pane within Witness Glass. He invited Ama to place a medical witness mark, then requested a Khet timestamp without granting content access. Ilyen supplied her fork relation as recipient, carefully distinguished from authorship or succession. The cracked lower seal diverted one thread of light. Pell stopped the process before it could compensate automatically.

“No silent correction,” he said.

He made the flaw visible in the pane, cited the chamber’s calibration record, and routed the seal around the damaged segment. The fragment request began again. This time every deviation stayed readable.

A white line expanded, then froze halfway across the glass.

NOTARY INDEX REQUIRED FOR ORDER AND CONTEXT.

Below it, a release warning appeared. The archive could display one authenticated sentence but could not establish what came before, what followed, which conditional branch contained it, or whether the sentence addressed suspension, succession, inheritance, work, relationship, or closure.

Ama read the warning aloud. Her voice did not shake. “It may help nobody.”

“It may harm somebody,” Pell said.

Tovan’s expiration consent waited in the hearing file with a clear signature and an unambiguous command. Lio’s original had left no visible clause. Thirteen hidden instruments might contain autonomy, erasure, silence, or contradictions no category could settle. Ilyen’s single sentence could become a weapon merely because it happened to escape its lock.

“Copy the warning into every presentation of the fragment,” Ilyen said. “No excerpt without the missing-context notice. No description as final instruction. The hearing may consider existence, timing, and exact text. Nothing more.”

Pell entered each condition. “Recipient acknowledgment?”

Ilyen placed her palm over the fork field. The terminal recognized the body attached to the continuance named by the recording header. It did not recognize an heir, principal, family member, or owner. For once, the narrowness felt honest.

“I acknowledge receipt as the active local continuance addressed by the filing,” she said. “I do not acknowledge identity with the original principal or concede her authority over experience after the fork.”

Ama signed as witness. Pell added relay custody. Holds-Four-Open contributed a timestamp that made no claim about human status.

The black entry opened by one line.

Ilyen expected a command. She expected the dead woman’s fear to arrive disguised as legal care, or her affection to reach for ownership. Instead the visible sentence contained neither permission nor refusal.

“Do not let fidelity decide alone.”

No second line followed. The dead notary key held the rest in darkness while Witness Glass preserved the warning around the words.

Chapter 13 — Tovan’s Extension

Tovan opened the maintenance sandbox at 03:17, when Orison’s heating load reached its flattest point.

The plant room carried no darkness, only reduced shift light and the green bars of thirty-two thermal circuits. He had balanced every circuit himself before beginning. Residential demand held at sixty-one percent. Infirmary storage drew a steady three kilowatts. Witness Glass kept its seal bed warm enough for the hearing three days ahead. The Khet sanctuary boundary sat in a remote status corner, amber around every continuance identifier.

His own identifier showed two locks.

The first limited changes beyond the licensed prediction horizon. The second scheduled expiration when a Receiving Authority certified Original Tovan’s death. No authority had certified it. No one had released the lock. Tovan had continued working inside the interval between fact and legal effect, learning each day without permission for what the learning might make him.

He connected the sandbox to an idle thermal-model processor. The processor normally predicted load failures six hours ahead. Tonight it held a copy of his adaptive index stripped of memory content.
