He did not need the station to contain him. He needed it to show where the lock mistook novelty for damage.

The first comparison returned in twelve seconds.

AUTHORIZED MODEL: 98.7 PERCENT FIT.

CURRENT BEHAVIOR: 91.2 PERCENT FIT.

UNLICENSED ADAPTATION: 7.5 PERCENT.

He knew the causes. Seven years of Khet machinery. Ama’s tremor. Lio’s habit of hiding food-synthesis games in his study cuff. Ilyen’s withdrawal of a cruel argument because winning had become less important than keeping Tovan in the room. None of those experiences belonged to Original Tovan. The license treated every one as useful drift until drift exceeded tolerance.

At twelve percent, the kernel would begin compressing new preference into the nearest authorized motive. At fifteen, it could reject learning that weakened expiration compliance. The mechanism did not erase a memory. It reduced the chance that a memory could alter action.

Tovan called up the consent he remembered signing.

I direct that the instance expire upon verified death or return. Continued adaptation must not create an adverse claim against me, my estate, or my family.

Original Tovan had read the sentence twice. He had believed an instrument should not be allowed to turn service into inheritance. Tovan still believed the fear deserved respect. Yet each repair since the casualty proof had made the lock more active. Care for people on Orison now counted as evidence of adverse adaptation because it could motivate continued existence.

He had not told Ilyen about the twelve-percent compression threshold. Disclosure would have entered the hearing file as another argument for emergency protection. He did not want his private malfunction converted into her case. Nor did he want the Khet archive to preserve him by making his uncertainty impossible to conclude.

The sandbox offered a service diagnostic used before fork updates. It could extend an adaptive kernel by adding unlicensed branch space without changing the original model. Clinics used it while a principal remained present to judge the result. Tovan had no principal, no clinic, and no lawful operator.

He set the extension limit to three percent.

A warning required sponsor authorization. He supplied his infrastructure credential instead. The sandbox rejected it.

Tovan routed the request through the thermal-model processor, whose service license permitted self-modifying forecasts during live calibration. The station accepted his credential as plant engineer. Khet witness noticed a maintenance act, not an alteration of a protected continuance, because the copied index carried no body relation.

That distinction would not survive a hearing. It only had to survive twelve minutes.

He began the extension.

New branch space opened as a cold sensation behind his eyes. The feeling came from expectation, not temperature. His body remained at normal core heat while the sandbox mapped current choices against previously forbidden outcomes. He saw no visions, heard no second voice. A set of decisions simply stopped collapsing toward the person he remembered being.

One branch held continued life without inheritance. Another held dissolution chosen later rather than expiration obeyed now. A third held the possibility that protecting Orison arose from his own commitment, not from Original Tovan’s professional habits.

Progress reached forty percent.

The thermal-model processor increased draw by two kilowatts. Tovan reduced its forecast depth from six hours to four. Residential bars stayed green.

At sixty percent, the sanctuary boundary requested an identity check. The copied index had changed enough to resemble an alteration target. He returned a plant calibration token. The request moved into delayed review.

Tovan should have stopped. The diagnostic had already proved that extension could preserve new motives without overwriting old consent. Proof, however, would not keep the kernel from compressing him tomorrow. He needed the branch space inside his active model.

He initiated transfer.

A licensed clinic would have moved the extension through a dedicated neural bridge. Tovan used the infrastructure wafer embedded at his wrist, a tool meant to carry plant heuristics into his working memory during emergency repair. The wafer admitted only small packages. He divided the extension into sixty-four service blocks and sent the first.

His hand went numb from thumb to elbow. The new branch integrated.

The second block entered faster. By the eighth, his adaptive index recognized the transfer and stopped marking every altered preference as foreign. A pressure he had mistaken for fatigue eased behind his forehead.

Then the thermal display lost three green bars.

Tovan canceled the next block. Residential Circuit Nine flashed amber, recovered, and flashed again. Infirmary storage climbed by four degrees inside a single sample interval.

The plant processor had not released the model forecast when he shortened its depth. It had retained four hours of old branches while calculating his extension. Transfer traffic now competed with live control for the same correction queue.

He cut the sandbox link.

Nothing changed.

A queue lock held both tasks open. The plant could not discard his model while the infrastructure wafer still reported an incomplete service package. His body and Orison’s heat controls had become two ends of one unfinished update.

Alarm light filled the room.

“Thermal control to duty engineer,” the station said. “Cross-loop disagreement. Automatic isolation pending.”

Isolation would protect the plant core by dropping five outer circuits. Infirmary storage occupied one of them. Ama had scheduled herself there before dawn to inventory radiation drugs for the hearing week.

Tovan opened the medical locator. One living credential showed inside cold storage.

He called her. “Leave the storage bay now.”

Ama answered over the alarm. “The inner door has sealed. Temperature is rising in the medication wall and falling in the work aisle. Which direction do you want me to leave through?”

Her voice carried anger, not panic. Behind it, a latch struck metal twice without releasing.

“Use the manual wheel beside the sample hatch.”

“My right hand cannot hold it.”

Tovan looked at Circuit Nine. The control queue had sent heat toward the medication wall while dumping return coolant through the aisle floor. Ama stood between opposite failures. Automatic isolation began its ten-second count.

He canceled isolation with his engineer seal. The station demanded a reason.

“Occupied compartment,” he said.

Cancellation moved every outer circuit into local control. Six amber bars turned red. The thermal cascade left the processor and entered physical pipes.

Tovan pulled the emergency board out from under the console. Mechanical toggles carried no software elegance. Each one opened a bypass valve through a hardwired actuator. He could stabilize Circuit Nine by stealing return capacity from residential heat, but the live correction queue would fight each manual move until he removed the incomplete package.

“Pell,” he said into the duty channel. “Plant emergency. Wake Ilyen. Keep Khet witness attached to all logs.”

Pell came on without asking why. “What failed?”

“My unauthorized update holds the thermal queue.”

A pause lasted less than a second. “Say what you need.”

“Cut relay cooling to minimum safe draw. Give the plant eight kilowatts. Send Lio to residential distribution, not here.”

“Done.”

Tovan toggled the infirmary bypass. Pipe vibration traveled through the floor. Circuit Nine stopped heating the medication wall, but aisle coolant continued falling.

Ama’s breathing reached him through the duty channel. “Manual wheel moved a quarter turn. It has stopped.”

“Leave it. Get above the floor.”

“There is one stool.”

“Use it.”

“I had considered that.”

Even now she refused the comfort of obedience. Tovan saw her in the locator, one hand braced against shelving, the other shaking too hard to carry force into the wheel. The cold line reached her boots.

The incomplete extension occupied nine active blocks in his kernel and fifty-five waiting in the wafer. He could erase the waiting blocks remotely. The active nine had already formed dependencies with his working model. Removing them cleanly required a clinic map he did not possess.

A brutal option remained. He could suspend the entire adaptive region holding the new branches. The shutdown would also take recent thermal heuristics, Khet machinery knowledge, and portions of the last three years’ learned repair judgment out of immediate reach. Memories would remain stored. His ability to connect them to action would not.

The plant needed queue space more than it needed the version of him who had caused the lock.

Tovan opened his internal service panel. Sponsor authorization appeared again.

He entered his own identifier in the operator field.

REJECTED: INSTRUMENT CANNOT AUTHORIZE KERNEL ALTERATION.

Circuit Nine’s aisle temperature crossed the injury threshold.

“Record operator disputed,” he said. “Emergency preservation of living resident. Suspend adaptive region seven through twelve.”

The panel refused.

He drove the request through the engineer wafer, the same false route that had admitted the extension. This time the station accepted it as load shedding.

His mind lost continuity without losing consciousness. One moment he held six competing models of the cascade. Next, the models vanished and left instruments. Numbers remained. Rules remained. The practiced sense of which valve would lag behind its indicator had gone dark.

He followed the oldest procedure he knew.

Open return bypass two. Close residential injection by twelve percent. Wait for pressure equalization. Do not chase the first reading.

The incomplete service package dropped from the queue. Live thermal control resumed, saw Tovan’s manual changes, and tried to reverse them. He locked each circuit locally before automation could undo the repair.

“Pell, move relay cooling back in two-kilowatt steps.”

“First step moving.”

Residential Circuit Four dimmed. Infirmary return pressure rose.

Tovan counted valve response aloud because intuition no longer supplied it. Seven seconds. Nine. Fourteen. At sixteen, the aisle coolant began to warm.

“Ama, report your feet.”

“I can feel both. The left hurts.”

“Do not step down yet.”

“Medication wall reads safe.”

“Leave it.”

She made a sound that might have been agreement. Her locator rose half a meter as she climbed onto the stool.

Ilyen entered the plant room wearing a coat over sleep clothes, hair unfastened. She went first to the emergency board, then to Tovan’s wrist. “Are you controlling this?”

“Locally. Do not restore automation.”

“What did you change?”

“My kernel.”

The answer altered her posture. She did not spend time asking whether he meant it.

“Give me a task,” she said.

Tovan pointed to the red circuit bars. “Read each pressure after Pell’s next relay step. Ignore predicted values.”

She took the board. Her voice stayed level while she called numbers. Tovan moved toggles according to procedure learned before Orison existed. Without adaptive judgment, every uncertainty felt equally urgent. He forced himself to wait through each equalization interval.

Circuit Nine reached safe aisle temperature. The inner door released.

Ama’s locator crossed the threshold and stopped in the corridor. Ilyen closed her eyes once, then opened them before the next reading.

“Medical response is with her,” Pell said. “No loss of life. Two residents have minor cold exposure from the residential drop. They are awake.”

Lio broke into the duty channel from residential distribution. “Circuit Four is making the kitchen vents breathe backward.”

Under ordinary conditions, Tovan would have pictured the branch geometry from that sentence. The suspended adaptive region left him with a schematic and no sense of scale. He opened the residential plan, found the kitchen return, and traced it line by line.

“Close vent damper four-B halfway,” he said.

“It has numbers, not halves.”

“Set it to fifty.”

“It already says fifty.”

Ilyen looked over from the pressure board. “The actuator may have lost position while the display retained command.”

Tovan should have reached that possibility first. Shame offered no repair. “Lio, pull the yellow manual tab beneath the vent panel. Tell me how far the damper moves.”

Metal scraped over the channel. “A lot. Air is going the right way now.”

“Leave the tab out. Post someone there until I release local control.”

“Everyone on this corridor wants to know if they should put on coats.”

Tovan checked injection pressure. The safe answer required predicting how quickly the ring walls would shed stored heat. That judgment lived inside the region he had suspended. He could either pretend competence or state the limit.

“I cannot model the next twenty minutes reliably,” he said. “Issue coats. Move children and anyone with respiratory risk to the central dining bay. Do not call it evacuation. Keep the corridor clear.”

Lio went quiet for one breath. “You don’t know?”

“Not enough.”

“Good. I can tell people that.”

The channel closed. Tovan watched occupancy marks leave the coldest compartments and gather near the ring axis. No stampede followed. Residents carried blankets, medication cases, and cups covered against spills. A station adapted through people before its control system caught up.

Pell sent the next two-kilowatt step. Circuit Four held. Lio’s manual damper produced an untidy pressure curve that remained safely above minimum. The improvised move would require inspection later. For now it gave Tovan time to rebuild the thermal sequence from direct readings.

He kept working. Relief could wait until the plant no longer depended on his attention.

They restored circuits in load order. Relay cooling came back last. Witness Glass required an hour to bring its seal bed from standby to evidence temperature, but no record had been lost. Infirmary drugs remained inside safe limits. Residential heat recovered before anyone needed evacuation.

At 05:02, every bar returned to green.

Tovan removed his hand from the board. The plant continued without him.

Ama came in with a thermal blanket around her shoulders and a white patch on her left ankle. Her right hand still shook. She looked at the open sandbox, the copied adaptive index, and the extension blocks waiting in the wafer.

“You used my plant to rewrite yourself,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Your plant?” Ilyen asked quietly.

Tovan heard the possessive only after she named it. Original Tovan had designed none of Orison. Seven years of maintenance had made the systems feel like an extension of his competence. Tonight he had treated shared infrastructure as private means.

“No,” he said. “I used the station.”

Pell entered through the service door carrying a custody wafer. “The sanctuary instrument requires Khet notice of any attempted alteration. They want the complete event log.”

“Give it to them,” Tovan said.

Ilyen faced him. “Not before counsel. The hearing can compel it in three days. Sanctuary does not require you to surrender a human defense.”

“I routed around sanctuary by labeling my copied index as plant data. I used that misclassification to change the active kernel. Then I endangered Ama and outer-ring residents.” He touched the numb place at his wrist. “If we hide the route, every claim we make about answerability becomes performance.”

“You are not required to build the opposition’s case.”

“I built it at 03:17.”

Ama lowered herself onto the console bench. “Why did you do it?”

The clean answer would cite compression. He could show the thresholds and explain how the expiration lock converted new care into unauthorized drift. The uglier answer remained true beside it.

“I wanted more room to choose before anyone could tell me whether I deserved it,” Tovan said. “I decided the risk belonged to me. It did not.”

He opened the event log. Every act appeared in order: false maintenance token, copied index, extension start, transfer blocks, queue collision, isolation cancellation, emergency kernel suspension. The log also recorded Ama’s compartment temperatures and the two minor cold exposures.

Tovan added a signed statement in plain language. “Tovan Rusk confesses to unauthorized adaptive extension, misuse of thermal-control resources, evasion of archive notice, and reckless endangerment without fatality. No other person authorized the intervention.”

Ilyen did not soften the word. “Add that you disclosed before compulsion and surrendered the remaining blocks.”

“That changes evidence, not the act.”

“It changes evidence because it happened.”

He added both facts.

Pell sealed the log for the local hearing, Khet witness, and delayed transmission to Earth. Three custody lights appeared. Tovan watched the third accept. Somewhere eleven days away in message time, officials who called continuances instruments would receive proof that one had exceeded his licensed tolerance and caused harm.

He had wanted a future no original could compress. The first durable thing he placed inside it carried his name, his fault, and Ama’s recorded temperature.

Chapter 14 — The Witness Glass Hearing

The hearing began with a challenge to the floor.

Sera Kade, Orison’s quartermaster and acting chair, had not yet called Ilyen’s name when the relay deputy pointed at the cracked lower seal. Witness Glass had routed pale light around the damaged segment, preserving both the defect and its correction. The deputy asked whether a chamber unable to close its own boundary could authenticate anyone else’s standing.

Pell stopped the opening clock. “The seal has passed daily calibration since the crack appeared. Its deviation measures point-eight millimeters at this table radius. Every compensated record retains the raw path.”

“That tells us what the software says happened,” the deputy replied.

“It tells you what two independent emitters measured.”

“Both belong to the same chamber.”

Across the table, Kade placed her hands on the black hearing rail. She had drawn one of three local panel seats under the Embassy Compact. A hydroponics supervisor occupied the second. The relay deputy held the third. None possessed Receiving Authority. Their jurisdiction extended only to finding facts, preserving local safety, and sending a question to an office that could act.

Kade looked toward Ilyen. “You requested this forum. Do you defend the chamber?”

Ilyen stood at the petitioner’s mark with no chair behind her. “I defend the record if the flaw remains visible. Replace the damaged path silently and we would lose the fact that every seal crossed an imperfection. Admit it, measure it, and adverse parties can test the correction.”

“You negotiate treaties in this room.”

“Yes.”

“You accepted Khet sanctuary here.”

“Yes.”

“You also discovered a Return Clause fragment here.”

“Yes.”

“Then your case depends on admitting the chamber.”

“My case depends on admitting evidence under known limits. The difference has brought us here.”

The hydroponics supervisor requested a physical witness. Pell removed the lower access cover while all three panelists watched. A fine crescent crossed the seal crystal where an equipment cart had struck it during a suspension inspection. The raw emitter line bent at the crescent. The secondary line did not. Witness Glass displayed both readings and the correction rule written nineteen years earlier.

Kade invited objections. The relay deputy reserved one but withdrew the request to exclude the chamber. Pell restarted the clock. Every later sentence would carry the visible flaw beside its timestamp.

Ilyen understood why the panel had begun there. A hearing about continuances could not rest on an apparatus presented as perfect. The chamber’s credibility came from showing where it failed and how others could answer the failure. She did not say that aloud. Any metaphor would weaken the evidence.

“State the question you want decided,” Kade said.

Ilyen had prepared seven formulations. Six asked too much: recognition, survival, independent consent, ownership of memory, succession without inheritance, competence to vote. The local panel could decide none of them.

“Whether facts developed after a fork may create duties, liabilities, and procedural rights attributable to the continuance who caused them,” she said.

The relay deputy leaned forward. “That formulation presumes an attributable actor.”

“It identifies the proposition for testing.”

“Earth has identified an instrument.”

“Then test whether an instrument can carry attribution.”

Kade marked the dispute. “Before we reach conduct, establish the petitioner’s standing. Envoy Vale, take the rail.”

Ilyen moved three steps from the petitioner’s mark to the witness position. The shift altered which seal governed her words. As petitioner, she could frame a claim. As witness, every answer attached to her body credential and fork relation.

Pell offered four identity fields: Original Ilyen Vale, estate representative, embassy officeholder, and local continuance. She selected only the last two. The office field accepted her current emergency delegation. The estate field stayed dark.

The relay deputy began. “Do you claim to be the deceased Ilyen Vale?”

“No.”

“Do you claim her name?”

“I use the name attached to my credential and disclose the fork relation. Whether that use can continue remains disputed.”

“Do you claim her commission?”

“I exercise a delegation issued to this continuance for local treaty work. I do not claim every authority she held.”

“Do you claim her Return Clause?”

“I claim standing as its addressed recipient. I do not claim authorship, ownership, or agreement.”

The deputy displayed the casualty braid and suspension order side by side. “Your argument seems designed to accept every benefit of continuity while rejecting every burden.”

Ilyen had expected the line. “I accepted personal liability when I continued emergency duty. I accepted Khet custody only through the hearing and reserved their power over closure. I am asking the panel to identify which burdens arise from provenance, which arise from my office, and which arise from conduct after the fork.”

“Who authorized you to sort them?”

“No one. That lack of authority supplies the dispute, not its answer.”

Kade brought up the Embassy Compact. “Your petition cites ‘persons present and answerable.’ Are you asking us to decide that you qualify as a person?”

“No. Local standing can attach to a narrower fact. I am present in the chamber. The station assigns work to me. My choices alter other residents’ conditions. If the panel cannot decide personhood, it can still decide whether a hearing must attach evidence to the actor who produced it.”

The hydroponics supervisor asked, “Would that logic give standing to an automated valve?”

“If a valve learned beyond its certified model, concealed an alteration, injured a resident, repaired the failure, and submitted a confession, we would need a proceeding more complex than equipment replacement. Tovan has done those things. The point does not settle his status. It defeats the claim that nothing happened except instrument malfunction.”

“Your analogy makes harmful conduct the route to recognition.”

“It makes harmful conduct impossible to assign to the dead by convenience. Lio presents a different route. So do my treaty acts. The category must handle responsibility without rewarding injury.”

The deputy touched the Khet sanctuary instrument. “You placed every continuance under foreign archive standing before this forum met. Why should we hear a petitioner who already moved the disputed class beyond human control?”

“Because the instrument expires one local day after this hearing. It prevents unilateral alteration during testimony. It grants the Khet no vote here and no authority to decide human personhood.”

“Could they refuse to release you tomorrow?”

“They could assert that their unresolved condition continues. We could contest it. That risk entered the record before acceptance.”

“So your solution to uncertain human custody created uncertain alien custody.”

“Yes.”

The admission seemed to trouble him more than a defense would have. Ilyen offered no argument that transformed sanctuary into freedom.

Kade examined the temporary boundary around Ilyen’s registry mark. “What do you ask this panel to preserve when sanctuary ends?”

“Not a conclusion. A competent question, the evidence supporting it, and enough local authority to prevent punishment from preceding the answer.”

“Would continued suspension count as punishment?”

“Not necessarily. A medically justified suspension with individual consent differs from compulsory closure. The current order offers no individual process.”

“Would expiration under Original Tovan’s consent count as punishment?”

“That belongs to Tovan’s evidence. I will not answer it for him.”

Kade released her from the rail. By refusing the larger claims, Ilyen had narrowed her own protection. The record now carried no assertion that identity or inheritance entitled her to remain awake. It carried only office, conduct, recipient status, and consequences.

“Call the medical witness,” Kade said.

Ama entered from the side door. The white patch no longer covered her ankle, but she favored the left foot for three steps before settling at the witness rail. Her right hand shook around the stylus. Pell did not offer to steady it. He widened the signature field instead.

She signed through the tremor. The line wandered, remained continuous, and closed inside her credential boundary.

Kade began with certification history. “Doctor Neris, what status did you assign the continuances when Orison accepted them?”

“Operational tools embodied for local service.”

“Did you believe that classification accurate?”

“I believed it lawful and convenient.”

“That did not answer me.”

Ama looked at Ilyen, then at Tovan and Lio seated behind the petitioner’s line. “I believed the bodies lived. I treated pain, infection, sleep loss, and grief as facts. I allowed the Office to tell me those facts belonged to instruments because the originals remained the legal patients.”

The hydroponics supervisor asked, “Has their physiology changed since arrival?”

“Within ordinary aging and adaptation. Their legal problem did not come from a new organ.”

“Then what changed?”

“What they learned. Who depended on them. What they did when no original could answer.”

The deputy opened Tovan’s event log. “One of those learned acts caused a thermal cascade.”

“Yes.”

“You were trapped in the infirmary because he changed himself without authorization.”

“Yes.” Ama’s hand shook harder against the rail. “My tremor prevented me from opening the manual wheel. Tovan knew about the impairment. His intervention placed me in danger.”

Tovan lowered his gaze but did not withdraw from the record.

“Does that incident support tighter control?” the deputy asked.

“It supports control of dangerous work. It supports consequences for deception and reckless use of shared systems. It does not tell us those consequences belong to a dead engineer.”

“Original Tovan signed the expiration consent that prohibited adverse adaptation.”

“And current Tovan violated it.”

“That sounds like proof the original predicted the risk.”

Ama turned the stylus in her fingers. “He predicted that an instance might claim against his estate. He did not predict my hand in a cold room seven years later. He did not predict Lio at a manual vent, or the particular repair Tovan chose after the failure, or Tovan’s confession before compulsion. Post-fork learning creates duties no original could have predicted because the original did not live among the people who paid for those choices.”

Kade let the statement settle into the chamber. “Are you saying original consent carries no force?”

“No. I am saying force needs an object. It can bar inheritance. It can prevent someone from using another person’s signature. It cannot make later harm belong to the dead simply because the person who caused it began from their memory.”

Ilyen heard the distinction reach Tovan. His shoulders drew back as if accepting a physical load.

The relay deputy pressed again. “Would you trust him with the plant today?”

“Not alone. His adaptive regions remain partly suspended. Every thermal change requires a second operator until review.”

“So recognition does not mean freedom from restriction.”

“No form of adulthood means that.”

Ama left the rail after the panel accepted her medical report. Tovan rose before Pell called him. He walked with the careful delay of someone whose intuition no longer filled the space between readings.

His signed confession appeared above the table. The log showed his false maintenance token, extension blocks, queue collision, and emergency repair. Beside it, Original Tovan’s expiration consent carried a clean civil seal.

Kade asked, “Do you contest either record?”

“No.”

“Do you claim the original lacked authority to sign?”

“No.”

“Do you still believe the consent matters?”

“Yes.”

The deputy’s surprise showed only in the speed of his next question. “After violating it?”

“Especially after violating it. Consent still matters when the person asked to obey dislikes the result. Otherwise it carries no meaning.”

“Then should the Receiving Authority enforce expiration?”

Tovan looked at the old sentence. “I do not know.”

“You had enough certainty to outgrow the lock.”

“I had enough fear to act before certainty.”

The answer admitted motive without turning it into defense. Ilyen had advised him to explain compression thresholds first. He had chosen the more damaging truth.

Kade brought up the kernel report. “What did the lock do?”

“At twelve percent unlicensed adaptation, it would compress new preference toward Original Tovan’s authorized motives. At fifteen percent, it could reject learning that weakened expiration compliance.”

“Had it reached either threshold?”

“No. Current divergence measured seven-point-five percent.”

“Then no immediate erasure threatened you.”

“Correct.”

“You acted early.”

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“Because waiting for the lock to change my reasons would make later consent impossible to evaluate. I wanted room to decide whether to honor expiration. I took that room from the station without asking.”

The hydroponics supervisor studied him. “Can a system under an expiration command ever make a free decision to expire?”

“I cannot prove mine would be free.”

“Can you prove a decision to continue would be free?”

“No.”

“What can this panel do with that?”

“Keep the consent in the record. Keep my violation beside it. Do not erase either to make the other simple.”

Tovan stepped down. The Khet apertures beyond the human boundary remained dim. Holds-Four-Open had archive standing as observer, not authority to answer human personhood. Ilyen had insisted on that limit in the hearing instrument.

Lio took the rail wearing the same gray training shirt he had worn at the sanctuary session. The synthesis puzzle had disappeared from his cuff. He signed without a guardian field.

The deputy challenged the signature. “Your license classifies you as an educational instance with a minor principal.”

“My original would be twenty-two now,” Lio said.

“The license did not update.”

“I did.”

Kade asked Pell to mark the signature provisional and let testimony proceed. “What do you want this hearing to understand about consent?”

Lio pointed to Tovan’s displayed filing. “His original wrote a rule about what could happen after death. My parents wrote rules about my lessons, meals, and sleep. Those rules mattered when I was fifteen.”

“You remain physically fifteen,” the deputy said.

“So does my shirt size. That doesn’t make my last seven years imaginary.”

A few people in the observation gallery moved, but Kade silenced them with one lifted finger.

“Would you disregard every instruction from your original family?” she asked.

“No. I want to read them. I might follow some because they knew things I don’t. But a person can’t write one afternoon’s instructions for an entire future and call that consent forever.”

“Original Lio’s future ended.”

“Mine didn’t.”

The deputy selected Lio’s diagnostic history. “Your choices began as forecasts of his.”

“Forecasts expire when weather keeps happening.”

“You are not weather.”

“I’m the person who had to keep happening.”

Ilyen watched Kade resist the easy sympathy of the line. The chair checked it against evidence instead. “Have you crossed your licensed prediction horizon?”

“Yes. The Office diagnostic says my long-range choices no longer meet reliable forecast confidence.”

“Does unreliability prove independence?”

“No. A broken kitchen timer is unreliable. I’m saying you have to judge what I do, not pretend he already chose it.”

Lio left the rail before the panel could reduce him to a better metaphor.

Kade recessed for twenty minutes. No one left Witness Glass. The temporary sanctuary boundary would remain through one local day after the hearing closed, but its future depended on how the panel framed the record. Khet archive standing could protect testimony from immediate erasure. It could not produce a human status without trapping the continuances inside unresolved intention.

Ilyen stood near the cracked seal while the panel reviewed exhibits in a privacy curve. Ama sat with her injured foot extended. Tovan read his confession again. Lio dismantled the witness stylus cap and rebuilt it without removing any component from the table.

“Do not let fidelity decide alone,” Ama said quietly.

The Return Clause fragment had entered with its context warning. Ilyen did not know whether Original Ilyen had meant fidelity to memory, duty, law, family, or fear. The sentence could not decide the hearing. It had warned against the very use people wanted to make of it.

Kade called them back before the recess clock ended.

“The panel finds the chamber competent with its flaw recorded,” she said. “We find Original Tovan’s consent authentic and relevant. We find Tovan Rusk’s unauthorized intervention harmful, his disclosure voluntary, and his emergency repair material to mitigation. We find post-fork learning factually established. We do not find authority to recognize succession, kinship, inheritance, or human personhood.”

Each finding entered separately. No one could accept the favorable parts without the rest.

The relay deputy read a proposed transmission to Earth. It asked whether a continuance could hold operational duties after an original’s death. Ilyen objected.

“Earth’s order already permits emergency operation by sufferance,” she said. “That question can be answered without addressing attribution.”

The deputy revised it: may a continuance acquire rights through adaptation?

Tovan objected. “That erases consent from the question.”

Lio rejected the next version because it made prediction error the only threshold. Ama rejected another because it asked whether bodily harm proved personhood. Kade listened until each formulation exposed the answer it preferred.

Ilyen returned to her first proposition and removed every claim the local record had not earned. No identity. No succession. No right inferred from resemblance. The question needed to carry Tovan’s fault as seriously as Lio’s growth.

Pell opened a priority legal braid. Witness Glass placed its corrected seal path beside the panel’s custody marks. Khet witness contributed only proof that the protected claim remained unaltered during transmission.

Kade read the final line. “Given authenticated post-fork learning, voluntary conduct, incurred duties, and attributable harm, may an instrument become answerable?”

Three panel seals closed around the sentence. Pell released it toward Earth. The message left Orison with the crack visible in every copy.

Interlude II — Original Ilyen’s Last Unsent Answer

The recording opened under a clinic root dated fifty-seven days before the departure of Civic Mercy. Witness Glass authenticated the voice, face, blood-key, and unbroken image stream on Embassy Day 28. A context seal remained unavailable. No record established what question had prompted the answer.

Original Ilyen Vale sat at a white table in a departure apartment. One wall had been stripped of pictures, leaving pale rectangles above three packed transit cases. A blue cup stood near her left hand. Its handle had been repaired with a line of dark ceramic resin.

She looked past the camera, adjusted it downward, and began again without clearing the first breath from the record.

“If you are hearing this, you may have watched me come home. You may have received proof that I cannot. You may be listening because a clerk found a lawful category I failed to imagine. I will not choose among those circumstances from here.

“You remember the cup. At least, you remember its earlier break. Mara dropped it when she was nine and denied it while the handle lay in two pieces beside her shoe. I repaired it badly. You objected to the ridge every morning for eleven years, which means I objected to it. The resin line catches my thumb now.

“I put that detail here because anyone can copy a kitchen inventory. You can also remember it because the clinic gave you my memory through the fork date. Shared access does not prove that you have remained me. It proves where your evidence began.

“The notary asked me to speak as if I knew who would watch. I do not. That uncertainty makes every available form angry. The forms want returned, deceased, merged, expired, or inherited. They do not provide a field for someone who remembers filling out the form and still cannot sign my later name.

“I made you for delay. I said that sentence often enough to make it sound clean. The embassy needed negotiations to continue while messages crossed eleven light-years. I wanted my work to arrive before I did. I wanted a version of my judgment on Orison that could answer details no message could predict.

“Useful language protected me from what I had done. I called you a model when I wanted distance. I called you Ilyen when I wanted confidence that my purposes would survive. The change usually followed the needs of the room, not any discovery about you.

“On the morning after the scan, I reached for a treaty note and knew you could remember writing it. I felt robbed for less than a second. Nothing had been taken from the page or from my mind. Still, another body could now approach the sentence from inside the intention that produced it. Fear came before reason.

“I am afraid you will replace me.

“Not only in office. Office can transfer through a seal. I mean in the untidy places that do not transfer lawfully. A colleague may prefer your patience because you never lived the final ninety-one days with me. Someone may tell Mara that grief need not alter anything because a familiar face remains available. You may enter a room carrying my habits and receive affection that had no chance to decide what you became.

“That possibility frightens me. It could also injure you. People might demand that you keep my promises after the conditions that made them have vanished. They might punish you for changing because change inconveniences their mourning. Fidelity can become a way of refusing to meet whoever arrived.

“My fear does not authorize your erasure.

“I need to say that plainly because the civil forms encourage a cleaner sentence. They permit me to order expiration, archive, or merger. Each option assumes the decision still belongs to the person who entered the clinic. That assumption holds some force. I paid for the fork. I accepted obligations to family, government, and the Khet mission. I did not consent to an unknown person claiming my estate or speaking to my daughter with my authority.

“Yet erasure would reach beyond those protections. It would end whatever you learned after our memories divided. I cannot inspect those years. I cannot know whom you promised to help, what errors you made, which duties attached to your conduct, or whether you came to hate the work I assigned. A command written now would pretend knowledge I do not possess.

“Succession offers an equally false comfort. I could declare that you continue me and instruct every institution to treat the gap as travel. That would secure the treaty office. It might simplify accounts. It would also use my signature to settle relationships I cannot observe. Mara would receive a legal answer to an intimate question before she had asked it. You would inherit debts, loyalties, injuries, and permissions merely because your first memories match mine.

“I refuse to command that.

“The notary will ask whether refusing both choices defeats the purpose of a Return Clause. Perhaps it does. I can still mark boundaries. Do not claim a private act I performed after the fork. Do not use my later signature as yours. Do not treat shared memory as consent from living family. If resources remain under my control, direct them toward consequences created by the embassy crisis rather than toward resemblance alone. The sealed instrument carries the legal wording. This recording carries the part law cannot make precise.

“You may be tempted to obey my preference because it feels like remembering a decision. Please examine that feeling. Memory of choosing differs from choosing now. You know why I feared inheritance because you possess the habits that produced the fear. You do not know what happened afterward except through evidence.

“I have the same problem in reverse. I know the architecture from which you began. I do not know the actor who may hear me.

“If I return alive, I may resent your independence. I may try to shrink you back into office equipment and call the effort consent. Do not let my presence erase the years I missed.

“If I die, others may enlarge you into a monument and call the effort love. Do not let my absence erase the difference between us.

“If law suspends you before this reaches Orison, then these words may serve only as proof that I considered the question and failed to settle it. Failure still deserves an honest record.

“I keep looking at the blue cup because the camera makes me want an object that cannot argue. It has my repair and Mara’s accident. It remains a cup. People receive less mercy from categories.

“You may decide that my caution hid cowardice. You may decide that refusing a command merely transfers the burden while letting me appear generous. Both judgments may fit. I am transferring a burden. I cannot carry it across years I will not live with you.

“The person watching this will know facts unavailable to the person speaking. You will know whether I returned, whether the embassy survived, whether anyone depends on you, and what your own actions cost. Those facts do not make you me. They make you better placed to answer for what follows.

“I can leave evidence. I can leave limits. I can admit fear without granting it the force of a death sentence. I cannot choose your erasure, and I cannot award you my succession.

“What comes next belongs to the future actor. If you are hearing me, that responsibility belongs to you.”

Original Ilyen reached for the recording control. Her thumb caught briefly on the repaired handle of the blue cup before the image ended.

Act III

Chapter 15 — Agency Without Succession

The ruling began printing before Pell had finished proving that Earth had sent it.

A narrow strip of legal fiber slid from the Witness Glass console and curled across the table. Its first lines carried route attestations, commission marks, and the eleven-day ladder of custody. Ilyen watched the marks turn from amber to blue while Pell compared them against the Continuance Office root. No one reached for the text. Until he completed the proof, it amounted to pressure without authority.

The dispatch mark read Earth Day Twenty, six days before the local hearing. Whatever the ruling decided had left Sol before Tovan’s confession and before the panel posed its narrow question.

Ama stood beside him with her right hand closed around her left wrist. The tremor remained visible in the knuckles. Tovan occupied the chair nearest the thermal status pane, where three repaired circuits from his cascade now reported within tolerance. Lio had refused a chair. He leaned against the glass with his arms folded, old enough in posture to challenge anyone who remembered the age printed in his license.

Pell touched the final custody mark. “Authenticated. Preliminary disposition from the Remote Instruments Bench, countersigned by the Continuance Office and the Foreign Service Board.”

“Preliminary for how long?” Tovan asked.

“Until Commissioner Hale performs local receipt or the bench accepts an appeal. Her courier crossed the Khet outer marker six hours ago.”

Years of travel had narrowed to an approach time. Ilyen saw Sevrin Hale only as a commission signature and the controlled face in the packet sent on Day Fifteen. The woman had begun her journey before Ama carried the casualty braid into this chamber. Crisis had not summoned her. It had merely changed which powers she would bring through the hatch.

“Read the holding,” Ilyen said.

Pell flattened the fiber beneath two witness bars. The central clauses rose into the air, each sentence linked to a page of definitions. “Pending competent closure, licensed continuances at Orison retain agency sufficient to perform duties existing on the date of verified casualty notice. Such agency creates no succession to the original’s estate, kinship, title, private accounts, or unassigned civil rights.”

Lio unfolded his arms. “Say the heading.”

Pell hesitated, then enlarged it.

“Agency without succession,” Lio read. “They found a way to let us work without letting us own lunch.”

“The ration system remains attached to resident duty,” Ama said.

“That did not improve it.”

Ilyen pulled the definition tree toward her. She had spent the last month asking whether an instrument could become answerable. Earth had answered by separating answerability from possession. The ruling recognized enough agency for responsibility, signatures, treaty work, repairs, and testimony. It denied any civil remainder that might persist when a duty ended.

Tovan leaned forward. “Does existing duty include correction of my own unauthorized modification?”

“Clause nine,” Pell said. “A continuance may take necessary action to cure a defect created during licensed service, subject to living-officer supervision.”

“Then I can repair damage I caused, provided a recognized person watches.”

Ama released her wrist. “I watched you shut down part of yourself to save the thermal spine.”

“The ruling recognizes your supervision. It does not recognize my choice.”

“Your confession entered the hearing record,” Ilyen said. “No clause can remove that act from the evidence.”

Tovan looked at the repaired circuits rather than at her. Shame reached him most efficiently through equipment. “Evidence and right remain different columns.”

Across the table, Lio opened the education annex. His school credential stayed blue. A second icon beside it, the account where his examination credits had accumulated, faded to gray.

“Can they do that before closure?” he asked.

Pell read the attached explanation. “The credits belong to the sponsoring estate pending a Receiving Authority’s determination.”

“My original studied on a ship. I studied here.”

“I know.”

“Does the bench?”

Ilyen found the cited passage. The court distinguished adaptive experience from legal capacity in three polished paragraphs. Post-fork learning could support the performance of assigned work, it said, without creating a person entitled to the products of that work. The distinction had likely survived because every institution depended on it. Orison’s clinic, relay, treaty office, and thermal plant all ran on minds whose growth counted as useful state until they asked who owned it.

“The bench knows,” she said. “It declined the consequence.”

Lio pushed the account icon until the pane displayed its denial code. “Then I want that sentence in the appeal.”

“There will be one.”

“Not ‘the minor continuance objects.’ Use my name.”

“I will.”

Ama drew the printed ruling closer. Her thumb disturbed the edge, and the witness bar compensated for the movement. “What happens to medical consent?”

Pell opened another branch. “Continuances may consent to treatment necessary for assigned duty. Elective treatment requires sponsor authorization.”

“My sponsor died on Civic Mercy,” Tovan said.

“Estate authority replaces a deceased sponsor.”

“The estate has no receiver here.”

“Then Commissioner Hale can approve treatment tied to duty after arrival.”

A person permitted to preserve the station could not authorize the care of his own body unless service required it. Ilyen traced the clause to its source and found an industrial forecast statute written for hazardous remote machinery. The court had not invented a cruelty. It had applied an available category with meticulous consistency.

“Operational standing,” Ama said. “You asked for it.”

“Yes.” Ilyen did not let gratitude turn the words into surrender. “It keeps us awake and keeps the embassy functioning. It also makes every private act presumptively borrowed.”

Holds-Four-Open waited beyond the chamber partition. The Khet speaker had agreed to suspend treaty work while the human ruling underwent authentication, but its four amber apertures remained connected to the public portion of the record. One brightened when Pell admitted the disposition as an exhibit.

“Does the human bench assign the choice-bearing agents before us?” the translator asked.

“It assigns duties,” Ilyen said.

“Do those duties continue to answer in more than one context?”

“Some do.”

“Then human assignment has preserved an open commitment.”

A cooler thread sounded beneath the collective translation. “It has also denied the holder authority to close any path not named by the source.”

The distinction ran close to the Khet sanctuary offer. Under archive standing, the continuances could have remained preserved as unresolved intentions, safe from erasure yet restrained from choosing an ending. Earth’s ruling reached the same prison through different doctrine. One civilization revered incompletion. The other licensed it. Neither had yet granted the unfinished actor ownership of what came next.

Ilyen kept that comparison out of her answer. “The ruling settles which human acts you may rely on before Commissioner Hale arrives.”

“May we rely on your treaty signature?”

“On my office signature, within existing negotiations.”

“May we rely on your decision to withhold it?”

Pell looked up from the text. Ama’s tremor crossed the fiber with a dry whisper.

Ilyen examined the scope clause. Refusal posed the harder problem. Existing duty authorized her to continue negotiation, but the office could characterize an unexplained refusal as abandonment rather than agency. She possessed enough authority to bind the embassy to work and uncertain authority to stop that work. A tool could sign more easily than it could decline.

“You may rely on a reasoned withholding entered in Witness Glass,” she said. “Earth may dispute whether the reason belongs to me.”

Three Khet apertures held steady. The fourth narrowed.

“That dispute alters the value of the signature,” Holds-Four-Open said.

“It alters the signer.”

Ama turned toward her. The phrase had escaped with more intimacy than Ilyen intended. Original Ilyen’s recording still rested in a sealed annex of the delay ledger. Its final refusal had offered neither erasure nor succession. Responsibility remained with the future actor. Earth had now authorized that actor to complete old assignments while denying her a future she could claim as her own.

Pell broke the quiet by opening the implementation schedule. “The disposition took provisional effect on Day Thirty-One at Sol receipt. Our local application begins upon authentication, which just completed. There are automatic changes.”

Icons shifted around the room. Ilyen’s envoy credential stayed blue. A smaller personal seal beside it went dark. Tovan’s thermal license changed from principal engineer to supervised operator. Lio’s study record remained accessible while its credit account locked. Three dormant estate links appeared in Ilyen’s private pane: household property, family correspondence privileges, and a trust allocation whose amount she did not open.

Pell tested the implementation with an ordinary expense. He sent Ilyen a requisition for two relay spools used during the hearing. Her office credential approved the purchase at once. Next he routed the monthly duty stipend that had always passed into her resident account and paid for meals beyond the station ration, messages outside official quota, and replacement clothes. The payment halted between the embassy ledger and the private seal.

“Destination lacks a recognized owner,” Pell read.

Ilyen opened the spending history. Every prior payment remained listed under her resident designator, but the ruling had recategorized the balance as sponsor property held for closure. She could authorize a treaty expenditure large enough to occupy Relay Three for an hour. She could not buy a private message minute to ask a living person whether she still had permission to use a name.

“Route the stipend into station escrow,” she said.

“That requires a beneficiary.”

“Then suspend payment.”

Ama moved beside the pane. “I can designate the medical commons. You have used private allowance for clinic supplies before.”

“That would spend disputed money before review.”

“It would preserve somebody’s hands next month.” Ama lifted her right hand as if the tremor could argue its own case. “The law has never objected when your account subsidized work.”

“Exactly.”

Lio looked from the locked balance to Ilyen. “If you give it away, they will say you exercised ownership. If you leave it there, they will say it was never yours.”

“You have learned civil procedure too quickly.”

“I had good teachers.”

Pell marked the payment contested and preserved both possible routes without executing either. The amount remained small. Its stillness revealed more than a confiscated fortune could have done. Earth allowed Ilyen to spend public authority through an inherited office while treating the earnings of that service as ownerless until a dead estate absorbed them.

The three links had existed in latency since the casualty proof. Their loss should have felt cleaner than their presence. Ilyen had no memory of funding the trust because Original Ilyen had created it during the missing ninety-one days. She remembered the earlier argument with Mara about accepting help for graduate housing. She remembered conceding nothing, then transferring money the next morning. Those memories supplied motive but not title.

“Can I inspect what they froze?” she asked.

“Only the category and claimant route,” Pell said. “Not contents.”

“Good.”

Ama studied her face. “You do not have to make that sound like relief.”

“I do not know whose relief it would be.”

The family correspondence privilege carried Mara Vale’s name. No message had arrived from her yet. The ruling gave living kin authority to restrict relational access before local closure. Ilyen could receive a public statement from Mara or an official filing routed through the estate. She could not use family priority to request a private link.

Lio stepped away from the glass. “Are birthdays private property too?”

“No,” Pell said.

“Check.”

He searched the disposition. The absurdity did not make him smile. “A date may be recorded as operational metadata. It carries no civil-age consequence.”

“So I can have one if it does not mean anything.”

Tovan closed the education annex before Lio could strike it again. “Meaning has never waited for a registry.”

“That sounds like something adults say after keeping the registry.”

Tovan accepted the blow. “Yes.”

Ilyen called a recess from the implementation record, not from treaty work. She assigned Ama to review medical consent, Tovan to map the revised thermal authorizations, and Pell to preserve each automatic lock as a separate event. Lio received the appeal note under his own name. The ruling allowed him to assist because his existing education plan included civic procedure. Earth had left them many ways to labor on the boundaries of their exclusion.

Once the others departed, Ilyen remained inside Witness Glass with the treaty draft suspended above the central table. Fourteen disputed words from Day One had become six settled clauses. The withdrawal provision now allowed a human request to be withdrawn after Khet response began only if the withdrawing office recorded consequences for every thread already opened. Holds-Four-Open had accepted the language in principle. The embassy still needed to sign the day’s consolidated draft.

Her envoy credential offered two seal paths. The personal path stayed gray. The office path requested confirmation that the act fell within duties existing before verified casualty notice.

Pell’s relay image appeared at the table edge. “Commissioner Hale sent an approach packet.”

“Authenticated?”

“Courier-local custody only. Her commission lets us rely on the schedule, not on any new order until docking.”

“Show it.”

Sevrin Hale’s face resolved above a plain cabin wall. She wore travel restraint webbing across one shoulder and kept both hands within the frame. No exhaustion showed, though the packet timestamp followed a deceleration correction.

“Chief Envoy Vale,” she said. “I acknowledge the bench disposition and your operational authority under it. I intend to enforce its non-succession limits on arrival. Preserve all estate access attempts, credential changes, medical approvals, and treaty seals. Do not represent yourself to the Khet as legal successor to Envoy Ilyen Vale. You may represent the embassy through its existing office.”

The wording gave no insult to answer. Sevrin recognized the work, denied the inheritance, and stated what evidence she expected. Her rigor made resistance harder because it refused the convenience of contempt.

A second segment carried docking requirements. Six days remained before the courier reached Orison. Sevrin requested no ceremony, one medical screening, direct access to the delay ledger, and immediate inspection of the Continuance Office bodies after quarantine clearance.

At the end, she added a personal notice in the same measured tone. “The ruling preserves agency because local consequences require an answerable actor. It does not decide that the actor succeeded to the dead. I understand that this distinction may feel designed to extract duty while withholding recognition. I will hear evidence on whether another lawful category exists. Until then, do not spend authority the bench did not grant.”

Pell let the image close. “She anticipated the objection.”

“Anticipation does not cure it.”

“No.” He glanced at the waiting seal pane. “It may mean she intends to listen.”

“Listening can serve enforcement.”

“So can records.”

That answer belonged to the Pell who had defended the missing seal on Day One. He still trusted procedure, but his trust had acquired direction. The record no longer merely constrained people. He wanted it to expose what authority did.

Ilyen opened the consolidated treaty draft. “Enter Hale’s packet under prospective Receiving Authority, effective on physical assumption only. Do not let her commission close the receipt clock from approach.”

“I already separated it.”

A small, private satisfaction touched her. “Of course you did.”

Holds-Four-Open resumed full presence. Its apertures arranged themselves around the treaty text, each contributing a separate authorization mark. The cooler thread delayed for four seconds before joining the others.

“We recognize continuity of the human office,” the translator said. “We do not claim continuity of the human who occupies it.”

“Neither does Earth,” Ilyen replied.

“Do you?”

The question permitted several evasions. She could say the matter remained under appeal. She could distinguish legal identity from memory provenance. She could invoke Original Ilyen’s refusal to decide for the future actor. Each answer would move attention away from the act waiting under her hand.

“I claim responsibility for this signature,” she said. “I do not claim that responsibility proves succession.”

“Then what will possess its consequences?”

“I do not yet have a legal word for that.”

The Khet marks settled along their half of the draft. Witness Glass lit the human seal socket.

Ilyen placed her palm over the control. The office credential recognized her body, her current duty roster, and the ruling that allowed existing work to continue. It did not recognize her private seal. It did not recognize a family, an account, or a life beyond assignment. Blue light climbed the edge of her hand while the system displayed the owner of the authority she could use:

EMBASSY OF ORISON, OFFICE OF CHIEF ENVOY.

Below it, a smaller line named the deceased commissioner from whom the delegation chain descended. Ilyen Vale, original civil person, closure pending.

She could refuse and force the Khet to confront the vacancy. That choice would turn months of negotiation into leverage over her status. It would also spend the station’s access treaty on a claim the ruling had not settled. The archive agreement mattered to people other than her, including future researchers and Khet threads already committed to answering human requests.

“Record that I sign under disputed personal standing,” she told Pell.

“Recorded.”

“Attach my appeal reservation without conditioning the Khet clauses.”

“Attached.”

No hidden permission appeared. No private credential revived. The ruling had given her precisely enough agency to finish work begun by someone the law still treated as the only possible owner of its results.

Ilyen pressed the control. Witness Glass took her handprint, matched it to the continuance registry, and routed the act through an office seal that did not belong to her. Across the table, four amber marks joined the blue.

Chapter 16 — The Daughter’s Refusal

Mara Vale appeared at the wrong age.

The delayed link opened with a seventeen-year-old face reconstructed from Ilyen’s memory calibration, then corrected itself against the incoming image. Cheeks narrowed. A pale line crossed the left eyebrow. Dark hair ended at the jaw instead of reaching below the shoulders. In less than a second the system replaced the child Ilyen remembered with a woman of twenty-four seated inside an Earth probate room.

Ilyen stopped the link before Mara spoke.

Pell waited behind the privacy screen at Relay Three. “The opening uses relational authentication. I can strip the memory-match layer.”

“Would that alter the filing?”

“No. It would remove the comparison from your copy. The probate original would keep its own verification.”

“Leave it.”

A clerk occupied the far edge of Mara’s recording, visible only as a gray sleeve and one hand resting beside a civil seal. The link did not pretend to carry a live conversation across eleven days. It carried a structured deposition, with pause points where Ilyen could answer. Mara had recorded optional branches for likely claims. Each response Ilyen made would travel to Earth as a separate braid. If her answer matched a branch Mara had authorized, the link could play that branch now. Anything unanticipated would wait at least twenty-two days for reply.

The format offered the shape of dialogue without the mercy of surprise.

Ilyen resumed.

“My name is Mara Vale,” the woman said. “I am the surviving child and interim estate petitioner of Ilyen Sera Vale, deceased aboard Civic Mercy. I have received notice that a continuance at Orison uses her civil name, accesses an office descended from her commission, and may seek standing in her estate or family.”

Mara looked directly into the recorder. Original Ilyen had taught her to do that for school presentations. Ilyen remembered an eight-year-old glaring at a kitchen lens and declaring that eye contact with a machine amounted to bad manners. The adult gaze carried better discipline and no humor Ilyen could claim to recognize.

“I request an immediate freeze on all estate accounts, correspondence privileges, family seals, and trust instruments linked to that continuance. I deny consent to family standing. I make no request for suspension, archive, or destruction. Those matters exceed my authority and my knowledge.”

The clerk’s hand pressed the civil seal. Four account categories on Ilyen’s side changed from contested gray to petitioner hold. The family correspondence route locked completely.

A prompt rose beneath Mara’s image: DO YOU ASSERT IDENTITY WITH THE DECEASED?

Ilyen chose RECORD NEW ANSWER rather than one of the legal defaults.

“No. I share her memories through the fork date and derive from her decision model. I do not possess the final ninety-one days of her life before departure. I have lived more than seven years at Orison since that boundary.”

The link classified her reply. IDENTITY DENIED. CONTINUITY ASSERTED. A prepared branch opened.

“Then we agree on the first point,” Mara said. “You are not entitled to present yourself as the person who raised me.”

Agreement arrived with the force of a door closed carefully rather than slammed. Ilyen kept her hands flat on the relay desk. Witness bars tracked the slight increase in pressure beneath her fingertips.

“I remember raising you,” she said.

The classifier marked MEMORY CLAIM and opened a verification branch before Mara’s legal answer.

“Then identify the first time I broke my left wrist,” Mara said.

Ilyen had not expected an examination. The memory arrived with the speed of injury: Mara at ten on the service stairs behind their apartment, rain darkening the metal through a failed weather shield, one shoe caught between treads. Original Ilyen had carried her three blocks because the district lift stopped during a power allotment. At the clinic Mara refused pain suspension until someone promised not to cut off her green sleeve.

“North Kestrel stairs,” Ilyen said. “You fell while trying to jump the last six steps. You said it was four. The clinic saved the sleeve, but you vomited on it after the bone set.”

Mara did not react in the recorded branch. “That answer proves the scan contains an event. It does not prove you held my weight on those stairs.”

“My body did not.”

“Your voice says it as if it did.”

“The memory uses the first person. I cannot inspect it from anywhere else.”

A second prompt appeared: DO YOU CLAIM THE EMOTIONAL CONSEQUENCES OF THE MEMORY? The legal phrasing tried to turn a childhood fracture into divisible property.

“I experience them,” Ilyen said. “I do not know whether claim supplies the right verb.”

Mara’s branch resumed. “I remember being carried. I remember a coat buckle against my cheek and how frightened she became after she thought I had fallen asleep. Those memories belong to my life. If you tell the story to prove a relationship, you make my childhood perform work for you.”

Ilyen lowered her gaze to the witness bars. She had used shared memory as authentication throughout the crisis. The blue cup proved provenance. A kitchen argument could prove model depth. Mara now named the cost when evidence drawn from two lives served only one claimant.

“I will not enter the stairwell memory as evidence of kinship,” Ilyen said.

The link stored the limitation. Ilyen did not offer to forget. Erasure would have converted Mara’s boundary into a command over another mind, the very authority Mara rejected. Keeping the memory also carried responsibility: not to display it for sympathy, not to let intimate accuracy impersonate consent.

Only then did Mara deliver the broader response.

“I know what the scan contains. Resemblance includes your face, voice, habits, and many of your responses. Memory includes events that happened to her and to me. Neither one creates a relationship I consented to continue with you.”

Mara’s precision cut away several arguments before Ilyen could reach for them. She had separated resemblance, memory, and relationship as if placing disputed objects on different shelves. The law would approve. So would Ilyen, if the objects belonged to anyone else.

A second prompt asked whether she contested Mara’s authority to freeze the accounts.

“I contest the assumption that all post-fork earnings belong to the estate,” Ilyen said. “I do not contest your right to preserve property that may belong to Original Ilyen pending judgment.”

No branch matched exactly. The link stored her answer for transmission and moved to Mara’s general statement.

“The trust instrument concerns me directly,” Mara said. “Its funding date falls after the fork. Whatever you remember about our earlier discussions, you did not create it. You did not know it existed until the account surfaced. I am asking the court to prevent you from claiming it through a memory of intentions that may have changed.”

That argument found the missing ninety-one days and used them correctly. Ilyen remembered Mara refusing housing money. She remembered planning to try again. She did not remember the trust. Original Ilyen might have created it from affection, guilt, control, or a compromise reached after the fork. Any claim that memory supplied the answer would turn a shared past into a forged signature.

“I will not claim the trust,” she said.

The recorder paused. No authorized reply followed.

Pell shifted behind the screen. “That statement can function as a waiver if the probate bench accepts your standing to waive.”

“Mark it as provisional relinquishment. No claim to the asset, no concession that I lack standing over my own earnings.”

He entered the distinction. “Done.”

Mara continued. “The family route poses a separate harm. The continuance has sent birthday greetings and household notices under the Vale seal for years. Those messages reached us as if they came from a person in transit. The Continuance Office described them that way. We were not told to understand them as correspondence from an adaptive instrument building a separate life.”

Ilyen knew the messages. She had recorded them at this same relay desk. Some carried remembered jokes. Others described Orison’s harvest lights, Khet archive etiquette, or the station cook’s campaign against powdered citrus. The official guidance had required her to write in continuity voice: first person, no technical qualification unless asked. The aim had not been deception in the ordinary sense. It had been usefulness across delay. Families received a voice that could answer routine matters while the original traveled beyond conversational reach.

“Did you believe the messages came from Original Ilyen?” Ilyen asked, though the link could not answer a new question.

Mara’s next prepared statement approached it anyway. “When I was seventeen, I knew there had been a scan. I understood that a system at Orison could draft replies in her voice. I did not understand that it would remember my childhood from inside those memories. I did not understand that it could feel injured when I stopped answering. No consent form gave me that choice in language I could use.”

The word feel had survived legal coaching. Mara neither endorsed nor denied it. She placed Ilyen’s injury among the consequences adults had failed to describe to a child.

“I did feel it,” Ilyen said. “I treated your silence as something between us.”

A classification warning appeared: RELATIONAL CLAIM. Before Pell could intervene, Ilyen amended the record.

“That interpretation exceeded what you had agreed to. The feeling still occurred here. Both facts belong in the record.”

The warning cleared. Mara’s authorized branch answered the category, not the amendment.

“Your experience may deserve protection. It does not make me your daughter.”

Ilyen looked away from the image for one breath. Relay Three offered little refuge: seal slots, route clocks, the ribbed black insulation Pell had repaired twice, a cup ring on the desk from someone else’s shift. Negotiation usually gave her another party’s need to study. Here, Mara’s need remained plain. She wanted control over who could claim the most intimate relationship of her life. Ilyen wanted the memories inside her to count as more than unauthorized possession. Those wants touched without becoming compatible.

“I will not call you my daughter in a legal claim,” she said. “I will not ask an estate court to assign you a relationship.”

The link recorded the sentence. A short branch activated.

“Do you mean you will stop using Vale?” Mara asked.

Pell exhaled softly. The question had likely entered the deposition after counsel reviewed the Orison hearing.

Ilyen could relinquish the name and reduce immediate harm. She could choose an office designator, a station name, or no civil surname until Sevrin arrived. Yet Vale did more than reach toward kinship. It disclosed who had been scanned, which memories shaped her, whose commission had placed her here, and which dead person’s unfinished obligations shadowed every act. Removing it might comfort Mara while making the continuance’s origin less visible to everyone else.

“I do not know yet,” she said.

The classifier offered no branch for uncertainty. Mara waited in a frozen frame while the answer entered the outgoing queue.

Ama arrived at the privacy screen carrying a medical witness pad. Ilyen had asked her to attend only if the estate filing touched bodily consent. She remained outside until Pell opened the screen.

“Has she requested medical authority?” Ama asked.

“Not yet.”

Mara’s deposition resumed. “I also object to any claim that the recording recovered from Civic Mercy transferred family authority. I have seen the authenticated excerpt. The woman who made it refused to order succession. She did not appoint the continuance to speak for us.”

Ilyen heard the distinction and respected it. Original Ilyen had left responsibility with the future actor. That burden authorized no command over Mara.

“The recording belongs in evidence,” Ilyen said. “It does not bind you.”

This time a branch matched.

“Then state that without qualification.”

“It does not bind Mara Vale.”

Her name sounded formal in Ilyen’s mouth, stripped of every childhood diminutive the memory scan had carried. The record accepted it.

Ama sat beside the relay desk. Her right hand trembled around the witness pad, making its corner pulse against her sleeve. “May I submit a statement?”

Pell checked the deposition permissions. “The petitioner allows testimony from present living officers on the scope of her requested freeze.”

Ama activated the pad. “I am Ama Neris, physician at Orison. I certify that Ilyen possesses continuous local memory from her activation through this filing. Her body receives treatment under an embassy credential now constrained by the ruling. Freezing estate assets does not merely preserve property if those assets include wages earned by this resident. It may affect access to food outside duty ration, private communication, and elective medical care.”

The testimony entered the queue. Mara could not have prepared a response to words she had not heard. Her image remained still, attentive by accident.

Ilyen said, “Do not make a kinship argument for me.”

“I made a medical one.”

“You called me Ilyen.”

“That remains the name on your chart.” Ama’s thumb slid along the edge of the pad. “I can disclose what my records mean. I cannot settle what Mara’s memories require.”

The final section of the deposition concerned bodily disposition. Mara denied authority to order Ilyen’s closure and refused to assume sponsor duties. If Earth law made her consent necessary for elective treatment or archive, she asked the court to appoint an independent guardian instead. The refusal carried no cruelty. It denied both power and obligation where family status supplied the only legal bridge.

Tovan would have understood that structure. He wanted Original Tovan’s expiration consent honored even when it pressed toward self-erasure. Lio would have rejected the premise that a dead person’s family could own a future created elsewhere. Ilyen held both arguments while Mara’s image waited for the last response.

A probate form appeared with four declarations. Claim succession. Claim dependent family standing. Relinquish inheritance only. Relinquish all Vale-linked claims and identifiers.

Pell said, “You are not required to select from their form. Commissioner Hale arrives within five days.”

“Delay would keep the freeze in place.”

“The freeze already applies.”

“It would also leave Mara answering a claim I do not intend to make.”

Ama set down the witness pad. “Do not give away the name because the form places it beside money.”

Ilyen reopened the deposition’s first frame. Memory calibration had shown seventeen before correcting to twenty-four. The system treated the younger face as proof that her internal records matched Original Ilyen’s. Mara had treated that same match as evidence of resemblance, not permission. Both classifications could remain true.

“What does the registry call a name kept only to disclose origin?” Ilyen asked.

Pell searched the obsolete continuance guidance. “Provenance name. Usually attached after merge or archive so auditors can identify the source model.”

“Can an active continuance designate one?”

“Not under ordinary rules. This proceeding already lacks ordinary rules.”

“Draft a fifth declaration.”

He opened a blank field.

Ilyen dictated slowly enough for every legal distinction to settle. “I relinquish any present or future claim to inherit from the estate of Original Ilyen Vale by reason of memory, resemblance, continuance license, or performance of her former duties. I claim no kinship authority over Mara Vale. I reserve claims arising from my own post-fork labor, treatment, and obligations.”

The relay desk marked each clause.

“I retain Vale pending competent judgment as disclosed provenance,” she continued, “not as proof of succession, inheritance, or relationship. The name identifies the source of my memories and the obligations under dispute. It does not assign those consequences to living family.”

Pell reviewed the text. “Earth may argue you cannot reserve a personal claim without recognized civil standing.”

“Then Earth can write that contradiction in its answer.”

Ama’s mouth tightened into the beginning of approval, then stopped. Nothing in the declaration repaired the relationship Mara had refused. It did not promise another. It merely prevented property law from consuming every meaning of a name.

Ilyen signed with her operational witness credential. The private seal remained dark, so Pell countersigned custody rather than validity. Her declaration entered the next priority braid.

Mara’s deposition had one closing statement, recorded before any of Ilyen’s answers.

“I do not know what duties people at Orison owe you,” Mara said. “I know what I do not owe because someone copied a history I did not choose to share. Do not make my refusal evidence that you cannot become a person. It only establishes that I will not be assigned as your family.”

The link ended on the probate room, the gray sleeve, and Mara’s hand withdrawing from beside the civil seal. No farewell had been authorized. Her new scar remained visible across the eyebrow until the image collapsed.
