“The order permits living-officer operation. You can authorize each adjustment.”

“I can barely read this plant diagram.”

“You do not need to understand every valve. You need to own each instruction you approve.”

Ama laid the sensor tape on the exchanger. Her tremor made the adhesive edge fold against itself. She pulled it free, tried again, and held the strip in place with her left hand while Tovan aligned the read head without touching the control surface.

The tape reported a widening temperature difference across Pump Two. Loss of licensed balancing had forced the station onto its last certified distribution map. That map assumed full heat supply, ordinary fabrication, and no public inquiry attracting relay penalties. Orison possessed none of those conditions.

“First reduction,” Tovan said. “Close the unused guest ring and lower corridor heating by four degrees. Transfer infirmary, nursery, and archive environmental controls to the protected branch.”

“The Khet archive wing requires a stable band.”

“It already sits on an independent exchange.”

“They will see the human side cool.”

“They have instruments.”

Ama signed the first adjustment. The controller admitted her credential, then displayed Tovan’s name only in a gray advisory field. Valves moved through the outer ring. On the habitat diagram, nine corridors faded from amber to yellow.

The physical change reached them forty seconds later. Air from the floor vent lost its soft warmth. Tovan watched condensation retreat along an exposed pipe and calculated how quickly residents would notice cold through their shoes.

“Second reduction?” Ama asked.

“Not yet. We walk the loads.”

They crossed the plant in a sequence dictated by the old thermal ration map. Tovan read vibration through a manual probe because the licensed diagnostic layer no longer rendered predictive curves. Ama logged each number. At the reserve exchanger, she entered 6.8 instead of 6.3, corrected it, then held the tablet against her hip while the tremor passed through her wrist.

A distribution pane showed where the first reduction would land. The original map ranked rooms by equipment value, occupancy, and evacuation distance. Continuance quarters carried a lower life-safety coefficient because the station charter classified their bodies as replaceable duty platforms. Tovan had never needed to confront that coefficient while surplus heat disguised it.

“Remove the status multiplier,” Ama said.

“The controller will flag deviation from the certified map.”

“It has already removed your certification.”

Tovan rebuilt the priority table with physiological thresholds only. The change moved six continuance cabins above an empty diplomatic salon and dropped Sevrin’s assigned suite into the cold zone. Ama approved it. A notice traveled to every resident showing room temperature, protected spaces, appeal procedure, and the name of the living officer who had authorized the map.

Three appeals arrived before they reached the next pump. One living technician needed warmer air for a chronic lung condition already recorded by medical. Ama granted that request without exposing the diagnosis. A continuance archivist asked to preserve a private workroom whose humidity protected hand-marked Khet translations. Tovan moved the papers to the archive exchange and denied room heat. Sevrin submitted no appeal for her suite.

The third request came from Lio. He offered his cabin’s allocation to the infirmary and asked whether the system would credit the sacrifice during closure review.

“No,” Tovan dictated into the response. “Heat ration records material use, not moral merit. Move to an inner bunk if your room falls below the adolescent threshold.”

Ama glanced at him. “He will hate that answer.”

“He should. A ration system should not purchase obedience with warmth.”

He pretended not to see the effort in her hand. Ama had spent years asking others not to confuse observation with care. Today observation carried consequences.

The north sink showed a salt buildup along its return throat. A fabrication template for the replacement throat sat in station storage, encrypted under the suspended seal. Tovan could view its catalog image and service interval. He could not send it to the metal printer. Existing Earth law treated a manufactured part as licensed execution, even when the raw alloy and machine stood three rooms away.

“We have a spare?” Ama asked.

“Used. Removed in Year Seventeen after a stress alarm.”

“Why keep a failed part?”

“Because failed can mean cracked, warped, misread, or distrusted by someone who has a better one.”

“What does it mean in this case?”

Tovan opened the local service log. “Discoloration. No measured deformation. The licensed model recommended retirement at eighty percent confidence.”

“Can we inspect it ourselves?”

“We can inspect anything Earth has not made invisible.”

Ama added recovery of the old throat to her list. “Infirmary first. I need the medicine storage branch recalculated before food grace ends.”

The walk from the plant to medical passed through three new temperature zones. Crew had already sealed the guest ring with emergency curtains. Residents carried bedding toward inner cabins. No one argued with Tovan. Some thanked him before remembering the order had taken his authority. Others looked away, embarrassed by the need to use his judgment while Earth called that judgment unlicensed.

He preferred the ones who looked away. Gratitude could turn dependency into virtue.

Inside the infirmary, the cold cabinets had shifted to priority power. Ama moved each medication class onto a manual board: immediate life support, heat-sensitive courses, stable stock, replaceable stock. A fabricator tray held half of the filter membrane she had mentioned. Its edge ended in a neat silver line where the license interruption stopped deposition.

Lio sat on the floor beneath the pharmaceutical cabinet with a hand lamp and three printed inventory sheets.

“Why are you here?” Ama asked.

“The cabinet thinks I am educational equipment, so it still lets me read labels.”

“That does not answer why.”

“Pell asked me to compare local inventory with the public bridge medical questions. Also, my cabin has been assigned to the cold side.”

Tovan crouched beside the cabinet. “Any failed refrigeration alarms?”

“Two false alarms after the branch transfer. One real rise of point eight degrees in Bay Four.” Lio pointed to the lower shelf. “Those cartridges can tolerate it. The cultures above them cannot.”

Ama moved the cultures. Her right hand struck the shelf lip, and one case tipped. Tovan caught it against his forearm without breaking the advisory restriction; the cabinet, not thermal control, fell under medical authority. The absurdity irritated him more than the near drop.

“Protected-branch demand exceeds the old map by seven percent,” he said. “We need to reduce noncritical draw here before I can estimate safe duration.”

Ama disconnected the examination wall, two diagnostic cradles, and the rehabilitation heater. She kept the sterilizer. Each choice made medicine slower and preserved the temperatures medicine required.

A patient-call light flashed over the inner door. Ama read the message, selected a medication cartridge from Bay Two, and fitted it into a manual dosing frame. The resident beyond the door had developed a pressure spike during the corridor move. Automatic prescribing had gone offline with the medical license. Ama entered the dose from memory.

Her finger touched the decimal control, bounced, and struck the increase field again.

The frame displayed 2.4 milliliters.

Lio caught her wrist before she pressed confirm.

“Doctor.” His voice carried none of his usual challenge. “The sheet says point two four.”

Ama stared at the display. The shaking continued inside Lio’s grip. For one second Tovan expected her to pull away and restore professional distance. Instead she let him hold her hand while she canceled the entry with her left.

“Point two four,” she said. “Correct.”

The dosing frame required the entire order to be restarted. Ama scanned the resident’s identity, read the drug name aloud, then entered each digit with pauses between them. Tovan witnessed. Lio checked the printed concentration against the cartridge. The final dose passed manual verification and went through the inner door with a living nurse.

No alarm followed. The error had occupied less than five seconds and could have stopped a heart.

Ama sat on the edge of the examination bench. “You may release my hand.”

Lio did. “I’m sorry.”

“Do not apologize for preventing harm.” She held her right wrist against her chest. “You should not have needed to.”

“The automatic frame would have caught the high dose yesterday,” Tovan said.

“Yesterday I would have trusted it to.”

“Today requires two-person entry.”

Ama looked toward the inner door. “We do not have two physicians.”

“We have people who can read.” Lio returned to the floor and lifted the inventory sheet. “I can stay.”

“You are fifteen.”

“I can count decimal places at fifteen.”

Tovan saw Ama prepare the objection about responsibility and watched it fail against the patient-call light. The law had withdrawn licensed assistance, leaving a teenager to become a safety layer because his category still permitted him to read.

“Lio can cross-check labels,” Tovan said. “A living nurse confirms clinical orders. Record every manual dose.”

“You are advising again.” Ama’s voice had roughened.

“Yes.”

She opened a new medication ledger and wrote the procedure at the top. Her signature wandered through the final letters but remained legible. Lio added his name under inventory witness. The arrangement would slow each treatment. It also made the missing automation visible rather than hiding the cost inside Ama’s hand.

Tovan returned to the plant with the half-made filter wrapped in a sterile cloth. The interrupted edge gave him a measurement of the fabricator’s last completed layer. He could not finish the membrane, but its material thickness suggested how much stock the medical system had consumed before revocation. Every fragment now belonged in a ration model.

At the service cage, he found the retired return throat under two obsolete valve racks. Oxide marked one flange. The stress line reported in Year Seventeen did not appear under ordinary light. Tovan needed a field scanner to determine whether the metal could hold pressure. The station owned one. Its interpretation software required the revoked thermal license.

He carried the throat to the open worktable and connected the scanner anyway. Raw phase readings still came through because Earth had licensed interpretation, not physics. Numbers filled the pane without a pass marker.

The pattern asked for the part of him he had built in secret before the cascade. His extended kernel had combined irregular loads faster than the licensed model, beyond the prediction tolerances Original Tovan had accepted. Most of that extension remained shut behind the limits he imposed while repairing the station. He could reopen a narrow branch. No remote office would know if he erased the local trace.

A calculation formed at the edge of his attention: phase delay, probable grain boundary, safe pressure under reduced temperature. The forbidden branch offered completion with the intimate pull of a remembered tool.

Tovan disconnected the scanner.

He had once believed useful results could excuse hidden capacity. The cascade had disproved him in heat and smoke while Ama lay under a fallen conduit. No one had died. That absence belonged to luck, repair, and other people’s work, not to his intentions.

He carried the raw readings to Witness Glass.

Sevrin met him there with Ilyen. The three closure folders remained sealed on the side table. Tovan had left his dissolution terms in his cabin, where no one could mistake the document for a threat made during negotiation.

“I need an emergency repair finding,” he said. “The candidate part lacks licensed interpretation. I can explain the raw measurements, but I cannot certify them under the revoked system.”

Sevrin examined the scanner record. “Can Doctor Neris authorize use?”

“She can authorize the risk. She cannot create engineering competence.”

“Can you?”

“Not under current law.”

Ilyen rested both hands on the witness rail. “What are you proposing?”

“Transparent repair. Publish the raw scan, my method, uncertainty range, expected load, and failure boundary in the station record. Ama authorizes emergency installation as living senior officer. Sevrin witnesses that the technical judgment came from me. No one relabels it as licensed.”

The Commissioner looked at him more sharply. “You could have installed it before telling us.”

“Yes.”

“Why didn’t you?”

“Because concealment would make my competence indistinguishable from the danger Earth claims it poses.”

Sevrin called up his post-cascade restrictions. “Did you use the extended kernel?”

“No. The raw scan tempted me to. I stopped before interpretation.”

Ilyen said nothing. Tovan valued her restraint. Praise would have turned disclosure into performance.

They built the record in four parts. He described the retired component and every known defect. Ama joined by medical link to declare the heat risk if no repair proceeded. Ilyen entered the resource consequences without claiming engineering knowledge. Sevrin questioned the load margin until Tovan reduced his first estimate and added a manual inspection every six hours.

For the fifth part, Sevrin required an adversarial test. She selected three points from the raw phase scan without telling Tovan which readings she considered suspicious. He derived the pressure limits aloud from first principles, naming each assumption before using it. At the second point, his calculation differed from the old licensed service note by nine percent.

“Which one should govern?” Sevrin asked.

“The lower result until we know why.”

“That reduces available heat again.”

“It also keeps the flange attached.”

Ilyen entered the discrepancy as a condition of use. A public observer could now reproduce Tovan’s reasoning and see where uncertainty narrowed the ration. The record exposed more than a licensed pass marker ever had. It offered no institution willing to insure the result.

Ama’s link showed the medication ledger behind her. “If the repair fails under the lower limit?”

“We isolate the north sink and lose the protected branch within four hours,” Tovan said. “Medical evacuates cultures to the Khet exchange if they permit it. Residents move into the core. We do not run the old throat beyond the posted boundary to preserve comfort.”

Sevrin added her commission seal as witness, not certifier. “I want the distinction visible on every copy.”

Tovan placed his own mark beside hers. It carried no engineering license now. Witness Glass still authenticated whose judgment had entered the room, when he made it, and what evidence he used.

The process consumed two hours the station could have spent warming rooms. It also distributed responsibility across people whom Earth’s categories kept apart.

Back in the plant, Ama operated the hoist while Tovan guided the old throat into place. Lio observed from behind the safety line and read checklist steps because Ama’s tablet needed both her hands. They closed the north sink, drained the return, replaced the gasket with one from existing stock, and tightened the flange in opposing pairs. No new part left the fabricator. No hidden capacity entered Tovan’s judgment.

When Ama reopened the branch, pressure climbed in careful increments. The old metal clicked twice as it warmed. Tovan held his palm near the flange without touching it and watched for vapor. None came.

Pump Two dropped below its safe curve. Protected medical demand stabilized. The guest ring remained cold, food synthesis entered reduced operation, and the station stopped borrowing heat from tomorrow’s emergency margin.

Tovan entered the readings into the public repair record. The ration model recalculated around the observed throat performance, current medicine load, reduced corridors, and twelve-hour food grace. Its endpoint moved from thirty-eight hours to one hundred twenty-six.

Ama read the conversion from her tablet. “Five days.”

“Five days of safe heat,” Tovan said. “After that, the margin depends on fabrication or another reduction.”

She signed beneath his unlicensed engineering finding. Lio added the medication cross-check procedure to the same emergency ledger. Through the plant wall, the repaired return carried warmth toward the infirmary in a steady pressure pulse. Tovan left every raw reading visible and set the first six-hour inspection alarm under his own name.

Interlude III — Testimony in Delay

ORISON RELAY RECEIPT: PUBLIC QUEUE BRAID 46-7
Local title assigned by Keeper Ordan: TESTIMONY IN DELAY
Earth dispatch window: Day 35
Orison authenticated availability: Day 46
Editorial rule: Statements retained in submission order. Employment, family, and institutional credentials verified where possible. No statement carries dispositive authority.

Mika Sol, pressure-door mechanic, Aster Transfer Ring

I work beside a maintenance continuance named Or. I do not know whether Or has a soul, legal continuity, or any of the other things people ask when they can leave the failed door to someone else. I know he remembers mistakes made on our shift and changes the checklist afterward. A prediction does not need to change its work because my glove slipped.

Recognition should begin there. Not with inheritance. Not with resemblance. Begin with the person who can make tomorrow safer because yesterday happened to him.

My union counsel warns that recognizing continuances could let employers replace workers, claim the replacement consented through an original contract, and avoid pensions for both. I believe that warning. Give Or wages and the right to quit. Do not let the company call either of us equipment.

Justice Emeritus Alin Voss, Family Continuity Court

The Orison petition mixes two injuries. Existing law may wrong a learning instrument by denying protection to its post-fork experience. That injustice does not grant the instrument another person’s civil history.

Identity carries creditors, dependents, marriages, criminal findings, professional duties, and promises made to children. A tribunal cannot award those relations because an instance remembers making them. Memory can support evidence. It cannot compel another party to remain a spouse, child, partner, debtor, or heir.

Create prospective protection if the legislature finds capacity. Preserve the fork as a boundary. Any doctrine that crosses it without renewed consent turns resemblance into jurisdiction over living families.

Nera Bas, daughter of a deceased ocean surveyor

My father’s continuance called me by a childhood name after he died. Only three people had used it. For six months I answered every message because refusing felt like killing him twice. Then the continuance began asking for access to his vessel shares. It said the shares would fund work my father planned. It may have believed every word.

The court archived it. I still do not know whether that protected me or ended someone who had learned to miss me.

Do not make families prove fear before they can refuse kinship. Do not make a continuance earn life by performing love for people who own its memories. If Orison builds a new status, keep family relations outside it unless both sides choose them after the fork.

Jaro Penn, Continuance Office licensing examiner, submitted in a private capacity

A prediction horizon already concedes the central defect. Beyond that horizon, our certification cannot attribute a decision to the original with required confidence. We nonetheless keep attributing ownership to the sponsor while disavowing responsibility for any harmful choice the instance makes.

That arrangement gives the Office property when outcomes carry value and supplies an autonomous actor when liability appears. No coherent licensing system should preserve both advantages.

I oppose retroactive succession. I support a prospective civil date tied to demonstrated answerability, independent counsel, and an act outside the original model’s certified horizon. My division has instructed staff not to comment on Orison. I comment because silence now functions as a recommendation for forced closure.

Davi En, executor for six transit-loss estates

Every proposal for continuance recognition arrives after estates have paid debts, supported children, and distributed property under settled law. A new claimant could reopen thousands of transfers. Even a disclaimer of inheritance may fail if the claimant possesses private keys, trade knowledge, or confidential obligations belonging to the dead.

Orison’s instances entered service under contracts that provided archive, merge, or expiration. Altering those contracts under threat of heat loss does not resemble free consent. It resembles a remote facility using indispensable systems as leverage.

Protect bodies during review. Preserve evidence. Do not confuse emergency dependence with constituent authority.

Unsigned collective statement, twenty-three relay nurses and remote clinicians; credentials verified individually

We treat continuances because they bleed, metabolize medication, acquire trauma, and report pain. None of those facts settles inheritance. They do settle clinical duties.

Orders that disable medical fabrication to force a status decision use patient safety as legal pressure. The people endangered include living crew who never joined the classification dispute. We call for immediate restoration of medical and thermal licenses without prejudice to closure proceedings.

A person need not win a metaphysical argument before receiving a correctly measured dose.

Professor Teli Rao, consent systems scholar

The dead retain moral claims. They do not retain unlimited power to command newly accumulated experience. A Return Clause may carry evidence of purpose, fear, obligation, and promised limits. It cannot anticipate every relationship formed after the fork.

Likewise, present preference cannot erase the conditions under which a continuance came into existence. If an instance uses inherited passwords to seize an estate, provenance matters. If an estate uses an old expiration clause to erase a dissenting worker, present capacity matters.

Law needs a category that can hold both without pretending the conflict has vanished. Any prospective recognition should preserve disclosure, forbid automatic succession, review inherited obligations, and retain a voluntary path to archive or dissolution. Recognition without exit would turn rescue into conscription.

Arun Vale, cousin to Original Ilyen Vale

Our family has received messages from a voice carrying Ilyen’s habits. Some of us want to answer. Some do not. Public discussion has treated reluctance as cowardice and interest as betrayal.

No court should appoint the continuance to our family. No court should forbid private contact either. Give us room to decide relation by relation. The name question should not settle the person question, and the person question should not settle the name.

Mara Vale, verified estate party

I froze the Vale accounts because the claimant had access to memories that could pass every ordinary family test. That access made caution necessary. It did not prove fraud.

Resemblance cannot create kinship. Shared memory cannot obligate me to continue a relationship whose later years the continuance never lived. I will not accept a ruling that makes refusal evidence of cruelty.

Conduct can create a new relation. The Ilyen at Orison has disagreed with me without releasing private memories, using inherited credentials, or claiming that I owe her a daughter’s answer. I have asked her to relinquish the estate claim and to say whether she uses Vale as identity, inheritance, or disclosed provenance. Her answer has not reached me. What she chooses matters because the dead Ilyen cannot make that choice now.

I do not know what name that relation should have. I know the law should leave us capable of making it rather than forcing either inheritance or erasure.

Commission on Remote Personhood, majority memorandum excerpt

Current continuance law protects original consent and public reliance. Orison presents exceptional facts but does not justify local creation of a civil class. The Commission recommends protected archive pending legislative review, temporary restoration of life-safety systems, and prohibition of estate access.

No embassy possesses constituent authority. Crisis cannot manufacture it.

Commission on Remote Personhood, minority memorandum excerpt; four of eleven members

The majority correctly rejects automatic succession and then assumes archive offers a neutral pause. Archive ends adaptive experience. Calling that result temporary does not make it reversible.

The Embassy Compact grants emergency action to persons present and answerable. Whether continuances qualify remains disputed. The dispute itself warrants a local hearing that includes them as speakers and potential voters. Earth may decline to recognize the outcome. It should not prevent the people exposed to Orison’s conditions from recording whom they hold answerable there.

Family Continuity Court clerks’ association, divided filing

Thirty-one signatories support prospective standing without kinship. Twenty-seven support protective custody and legislative referral. Nine reject any status formed under local pressure. Six support voluntary individual election among archive, merge, dissolution, or a new civil category. Four submitted statements that fit more than one position and refused reclassification.

The association adopts no recommendation.

RELAY CUSTODY NOTE

The braid arrived with valid public-queue authentication and no unified issuing seal. The Continuance Office requested quarantine. Two labor courts requested preservation. A family court requested restricted access. The Remote Medical Board ordered its statement copied to the Orison health record. The Commission transmitted both its majority and minority findings under equal checksum authority.

Keeper Ordan admitted the complete braid to Witness Glass. No single Earth position could be rendered in the authority field. The delay ledger opened six concurrent issuers and left the line for consensus blank.

Act IV

Chapter 22 — Persons Present

Ama set forty-eight voting slates on the floor of Witness Glass, one for every resident who had answered the assembly roll.

The chamber owned twelve chairs. Everything else came from the station: maintenance stools, infirmary benches, folded meal tables, two cargo cushions, and a ladder Lio had placed against the back wall because he preferred to watch from above adult shoulders. Witness Glass extended its recording boundary through the open doors into the treaty gallery. Blue seal-light crossed each threshold and outlined the crowd without classifying anyone inside it.

Ilyen stood at the central table with the Embassy Compact projected behind her. The old phrase appeared near the bottom of the emergency article in letters smaller than its supply rules: persons present and answerable.

For years, the station had treated present as a location and answerable as a chain of command. Today neither meaning held cleanly. Earth had revoked the systems that let Orison obey. Half the people keeping those systems alive lacked the standing to authorize their work. A public testimony braid offered six incompatible Earth positions under equally valid seals.

Sevrin occupied the opponent’s table. She had refused the chair reserved for the Receiving Authority and chosen one level with everyone else. Her commission seal remained active beside her wrist. The choice did not diminish its power.

Pell opened the roll. His confinement had ended under Sevrin’s order for the duration of the assembly, though an investigation mark still followed his name across the record. “Thirty-one continuances. Seventeen living residents. Forty-eight eligible under the proposed rule. All present within the station boundary. No proxies.”

“Objection,” Sevrin said.

Ilyen admitted it. “Ground?”

“Eligibility assumes the conclusion. Continuances cannot derive voting status from language whose application to them remains disputed.”

“Response from the drafting committee?”

Ama rose from the front bench. The slate in her right hand moved with her tremor; she shifted it to the left. “The emergency article does not say legal persons. It says persons present and answerable. The continuances perform duties, incur findings, receive sanctions, and answer questions in this chamber. Excluding them would require us to claim answerability without participation.”

“That argument supports hearing them,” Sevrin said. “It does not necessarily support ballots.”

Ama looked toward the cold corridor beyond the gallery. “Tovan’s engineering finding carries his name. My credential authorized the repair. If the throat fails, both records matter. I will not tell him he may share blame but not a vote.”

Witness Glass marked the objection sustained as a legal dispute and overruled for local procedure. Pell preserved both outcomes. No competent court had decided the point. The assembly would proceed without pretending otherwise.

Ilyen read the motion aloud.

“The residents of Orison declare a provisional civic association for local safety, resource allocation, petition, record, and treaty support, under the name Orison Commonwealth. The association claims no automatic recognition from Earth or the Khet. It creates no inheritance, kinship, or individual closure status. It expires after thirty local days unless renewed by recorded vote.”

A quiet spread through the gallery, not reverent enough to disguise the ventilation fans. The Commonwealth sounded small when reduced to powers they already exercised. Its danger lay in the source of those powers. Embassy officers acted because Earth delegated authority. The proposed association would act because people inside a failing station agreed to hold one another answerable.

Sevrin requested the first argument. Ilyen yielded the table.

“No emergency clause grants constituent power by implication,” the Commissioner said. “The Compact allows local decisions when delay blocks ordinary authority. It does not allow residents to replace the legal entity that received the delegation. If you create a government because current law denies some participants standing, then use that government to grant standing later, the reasoning closes around itself.”

She brought up the thermal revocation and Tovan’s repair record. “Material pressure also compromises this vote. Living residents know their heat depends on continuance expertise. Continuances know closure options remain pending. A declaration made under reciprocal dependency may describe necessity without establishing free political consent.”

No one interrupted. Sevrin spoke without contempt, which made dismissal impossible.

“The prudent course,” she continued, “would preserve life-safety systems under emergency administration, petition Earth for a prospective civil category, and keep the embassy’s existing legal identity until a competent legislature answers.”

A living food-systems technician lifted her slate to request recognition. Ilyen called on her.

“Earth suspended my synthesis templates twelve hours after its Commission recommended life-safety restoration,” the technician said. “Which Earth do we wait for?”

“The licensing order carries current executive force.”

“And the Commission?”

“Advisory force.”

“Then the voice that can make us cold counts more than the voice that says not to.”

Sevrin did not soften the answer. “At present, yes.”

The technician sat. Her objection had not defeated the law. It had shown why delay changed which parts of law could reach Orison.

Ilyen recognized a continuance from the archive staff next. He spoke from beside a stack of relocated Khet papers, wrapped against the station’s reduced humidity.

“I oppose the motion,” he said. “We accuse Earth of choosing our status without consent. This assembly would choose a political status for every resident by one vote. I want individual protection. I do not want to become a citizen because my heating corridor needs a seal.”

“The motion creates no citizenship,” Ilyen said.

“It creates a Commonwealth whose record will name me among its governed residents.”

“You may file nonmembership.”

“Can I refuse its ration decisions?”

“No. Local safety rules apply to everyone within the habitat.”

“Then nonmembership changes the heading, not the power.”

The challenge reached a weakness Ilyen had marked and hoped no one would press so early. Any station authority governed shared air. Consent could choose representatives and exit terms, but no resident could withdraw a cabin from thermal physics.

Tovan stood near the plant crew. “The current embassy ration also binds every room. The difference concerns whether your objection enters a local political record or a maintenance appeal.”

“That difference may matter to you,” the archivist replied. “It does not give you my consent.”

“No,” Tovan said. “It gives you a way to deny it where the ration cannot pretend you agreed.”

Pell recorded the exchange as a proposed protection: every ballot would include a membership election separate from the Commonwealth motion. A resident could reject membership while remaining subject to transparent safety rules. Individual closure status would remain outside both votes.

Tovan kept his slate lowered. “Add a service refusal.”

The archivist turned toward him. “For whom?”

“For everyone. No resident should lose heat priority, medical access, food, or voting eligibility for refusing a duty assignment. The Commonwealth cannot build its emergency powers on compelled continuance labor.”

A plant technician spoke from the living benches. “If you refuse the exchanger, people freeze.”

“Yes.” Tovan’s voice held the same level cadence he used while naming a failure boundary. “That consequence should influence me. It should not convert my body into station property.”

“You are the only person who can keep that repair within margin.”

“I am considering dissolution under Sevrin’s third closure option.”

The gallery reacted in separate movements. Ama looked down. Several continuances turned toward him at once. One living resident whispered a denial too low for the record to translate.

Tovan did not withdraw the statement. “A Commonwealth formed to protect our choices must survive the fact that mine may include stopping. If your polity depends on treating my possible death as sabotage, vote no now.”

Ilyen admitted the service-refusal amendment. Emergency work could be requested with published consequences, compensated through any future local accounts, and declined without civil penalty. A person who accepted a task remained answerable for negligence or concealment. Immediate physical interference with safety systems remained punishable regardless of status.

The plant technician read the revised text. “Can we train replacements under revoked software?”

“Manual instruction remains possible,” Tovan said. “It will cost efficiency and time.”

“Then begin tonight.”

“I already prepared the first lesson.”

That exchange did more for the motion than Ilyen’s clauses. It turned dependency from an accusation into work that could change.

Sevrin leaned toward her witness pane. “That amendment improves the record. It does not cure the authority defect.”

“It makes the defect more honest,” Ilyen said.

“Honesty does not create jurisdiction.”

“No. It tells any later jurisdiction what we did with the power conditions forced us to use.”

The next speaker, a living relay operator, argued that Pell’s bridge had already damaged their legal position. “Earth calls us captured by continuance pressure. Forming this thing proves the allegation.”

Pell answered from the clerk’s station. “I opened the bridge. No continuance ordered me.”

“You opened it for them.”

“I opened it because the public record and the official record had stopped describing the same events.”

“You do not get to turn misconduct into a constitution.”

“I agree.” Pell touched the investigation mark beside his name. “My act should count as evidence of failed procedure, not virtue. Vote against the motion if you think repair remains possible through the embassy.”

The operator looked unsettled by the invitation. Ilyen understood. An accused person who welcomed rejection deprived his opponent of coercion as an easy explanation.

Lio climbed down two rungs on the ladder. “I have a procedure question.”

“State it,” Ilyen said.

“If we vote no, who decides what I can do tomorrow?”

“Sevrin retains Receiving Authority. I retain operational agency within the preliminary ruling. Living officers authorize emergency systems.”

“And if we vote yes?”

“The provisional council would decide local safety and resource questions. Sevrin would still hold human closure power unless another law recognizes the Commonwealth.”

“So this vote does not make me a person.”

“No.”

“It only decides whether I can vote with the people who already use my work.”

“For local matters, yes.”

Lio considered that. “Then stop calling it our recognition vote.”

No one had used that phrase in the formal motion. It had appeared in corridor notices, meal-table arguments, and private messages because hope preferred a larger promise than the text could keep. Ilyen asked Pell to add a clarification to every slate: THE COMMONWEALTH MOTION DOES NOT DETERMINE PERSONHOOD, CIVIC BIRTH, INHERITANCE, KINSHIP, OR INDIVIDUAL CLOSURE.

The sentence consumed nearly half the display.

Ama requested recognition again. “I signed continuance certifications for years. I called people operational tools because the category let me secure medicine, housing, and duty protection for them. I told myself the language mattered less than the care it purchased.” Her right hand moved against the slate. “Yesterday that language put Lio on the floor checking medication because the cabinet still counted him as equipment. Categories reach bodies even when decent people mean them kindly.”

She faced Sevrin. “I do not know whether crisis can found a polity. I know ordinary authority created the crisis and then called our response contaminated by it.”

Sevrin stood. “I agree that the revocation endangered living crew and continuances. I have filed that finding against my own issuing office.”

“Will you restore the licenses?”

“My local override reached only medical verification and failed against the central fabrication root. I cannot lawfully forge it.”

“Would you stop us from using a Commonwealth seal on local fabricators?”

“If you break Earth encryption, I will record the violation. If the action prevents imminent harm and uses competent review, I will also record necessity. I will not call one fact by the other’s name.”

The answer changed several faces in the living benches. Sevrin had not promised protection. She had promised an accurate adversary.

Ilyen called a recess for amendments. The crowd divided into working knots while Witness Glass kept the record open. Tovan joined two plant technicians over the emergency-powers clause. Lio sat with the archivist who opposed the motion and shortened the nonmembership language. Ama remained at the front, pressing her shaking hand between her knees until she could sign the medical safeguard.

Sevrin approached Ilyen’s side of the table.

“You should require more than a bare majority,” she said.

“We have no agreed constituencies for a dual threshold.”

“Living and continuance counts exist.”

“A separate living veto would preserve the hierarchy the meeting questions.”

“And a unified vote lets thirty-one continuances overwhelm seventeen living residents while claiming common consent.”

Ilyen had considered both traps. “What do you recommend?”

“Require a majority overall and at least one-third support within each current legal class. State that the classes serve only as an anti-capture check, not as a permanent constitutional division.”

“You are improving a motion you oppose.”

“I am making the evidence useful to whoever reviews it.”

“That sounds dangerously like participation.”

Sevrin ignored the private humor. “Also bar the provisional council from deciding individual civic birth. Save that question for a separate covenant.”

Ilyen entered both amendments. They narrowed the Commonwealth and strengthened its claim to have heard the people it could overrule. The second point protected Tovan’s dissolution option from any majority eager to convert him into a citizen for his engineering skill.

“You intend to vote,” Ilyen said.

“As a resident under the procedure, yes.”

“Will you tell me how?”

“No.” Sevrin aligned the edge of her slate with the witness rail. “My argument belongs in public. My ballot does not.”

“The Commissioner’s vote may look like a ruling however you cast it.”

“Then the record should say my commission carries no special weight. Do not remove my ordinary participation merely because Earth gave me extraordinary power.”

Ilyen had not expected to hear Sevrin argue from equality. “If the motion passes, will you use your office seal on Commonwealth acts?”

“Not as issuer. I may use it to witness facts, challenge claims, or preserve an appeal route. If the council sends a petition, I will transmit it without endorsing its authority.”

“And if it opens a system Earth sealed?”

“I will ask who acted, under what evidence, with which alternatives, and who accepted the risk. The answer may support a necessity defense. It will not erase the violation.”

“You preserve every blade on both sides.”

“Because someone will have to handle this record after we finish needing one another to survive.”

The line exposed what separated them. Ilyen read procedure as a place where people could still choose a shared future. Sevrin read it as evidence for the day when shared necessity ended and claims separated again. Orison needed both readings. One without the other would produce either paralysis or amnesty for whatever the emergency excused.

At the far table, Lio and the opposing archivist returned with a membership clause stripped of every honorific. It offered only participation, notice, appeal, and eligibility for local office. Lio had removed the preamble about common fate.

“Too emotional?” Ilyen asked when he handed it over.

“Too easy to agree with,” he said. “A rule should still work when I don’t feel common.”

When the recess ended, Pell read the final procedure. Forty-eight eligible residents. Secret ballots stored under personal seals. Public tallies by current legal class. Separate questions on Commonwealth formation and individual membership. Formation required a majority of ballots cast plus one-third support among both living residents and continuances. Abstentions counted toward no threshold except quorum.

Holds-Four-Open appeared beyond the treaty boundary as a foreign observer. Its four apertures carried no voting credential. One thread requested a statement before polling.

“The human assembly seeks completion through a category not yet authorized,” the speaker said. “Does the request ask Khet witness to make the category valid?”

“No,” Ilyen replied. “Your presence records that we acted. It does not decide whether our act created lawful human authority.”

Two apertures brightened. One remained cool. The fourth withheld contribution. “We will record the action without closing its status.”

That arrangement suited both sides too well to count as comfort.

Pell opened the ballot interval. Witness Glass extinguished all identity marks and displayed only forty-eight blank receipt sockets. Around the room, people touched their slates. Ilyen read the motion one final time. It did not promise personhood. It did not restore heat or settle the treaty. It created a local body capable of answering for the choices they already made together.

She voted yes.

Her slate sealed the decision without revealing it. Ama voted with her left thumb because the right could not hold steady against the sensor. Lio descended from the ladder and used the lower reader. Tovan stood for a long moment over both questions, then closed his slate. Sevrin voted as a living resident; her commission gave her no additional weight.

The final socket filled.

Pell closed the interval and released the tally.

FORMATION: TWENTY-FIVE YES. TWENTY-ONE NO. TWO ABSTAIN.

CURRENT LEGAL CLASS, LIVING: EIGHT YES. NINE NO.

CURRENT LEGAL CLASS, CONTINUANCE: SEVENTEEN YES. TWELVE NO. TWO ABSTAIN.

Both thresholds had cleared. Barely.

No cheer came. The no votes occupied too much of the room for celebration, and the yes voters understood that survival had helped write their ballots. Pell marked the result as a narrow vote conducted under declared material pressure, disputed jurisdiction, universal local participation, and independent legal opposition.

Sevrin rose before Ilyen could speak. “I witness that the stated procedure occurred, that all eligible residents received access, that opposition entered the record, and that the tally satisfies the procedure adopted by this assembly. I do not certify Earth recognition or constituent authority.”

“Recorded,” Pell said.

Membership results remained sealed to individuals, with only aggregate numbers due after a cooling period. The Commonwealth could not claim every resident. It could claim the formation vote.

Ilyen touched the new seal pane. “Under the recorded motion, I declare the provisional Orison Commonwealth formed for thirty local days, subject to renewal, review, and the limits stated.”

Witness Glass searched its authority registry. No sovereign seal answered. After three attempts, the chamber created a local mark from the forty-eight receipt sockets and stamped it PROVISIONAL, JURISDICTION DISPUTED.

Beyond the open doors, a thermal valve changed position under Ama’s emergency credential and Tovan’s published advice. The sound traveled through the floor while the new mark settled beside the old Embassy Compact. The Orison Commonwealth entered the record with twenty-one votes against it still attached.

Chapter 23 — What the Dead Authorized

The dead notary key refused the Commonwealth seal twice before accepting that refusal as evidence.

Pell had routed the key into Witness Glass through an obsolete treaty pane that predated Orison’s current charter. Its lock expected one of three authorities: the notary who had created the Return Clause archive, a recognized successor office, or a local receiving body capable of answering an adverse claim. The notary had died aboard Civic Mercy. Earth named no successor in the recovered packet. The provisional Orison Commonwealth fit only the third description, and no court recognized it.

On the third attempt, Ilyen instructed the seal not to claim recognition. She attached the formation record, twenty-one opposing votes, Sevrin’s jurisdictional objection, and the Commonwealth’s thirty-day limit. The key read the defects for nineteen seconds.

Then it opened a challenge pane.

LOCAL BODY ASSERTS CAPACITY TO RECEIVE DISPUTED INSTRUCTIONS WITHOUT CLAIMING SUCCESSION. IDENTIFY PERSONS ANSWERABLE FOR MISUSE.

Forty-eight receipt sockets appeared beneath the question. Not every resident had joined the Commonwealth, but every one remained governed by its emergency rules. The assembly had anticipated that problem. Its archive act required five named custodians, two nonmembers, and an adversarial witness.

Ilyen entered her name as provisional convenor. Ama signed for living medical interests, her right hand carrying a narrow tremor through the final stroke. Pell signed for relay custody. Tovan signed as a continuance who had publicly considered dissolution. The opposing archivist from the formation debate added his nonmember credential. A living relay operator who had voted no supplied the second nonmember mark.

Sevrin took the adversarial field. “My signature attests access conditions and chain of custody. It does not concede that the Commonwealth has legal power to receive these clauses.”

“State what you would do if the lock rejects us again,” Ilyen said.

“Preserve it and petition a successor notary.”

“Minimum delay?”

“Twenty-two days for an answer under perfect routing. Longer for appointment.”

“Thermal license status?”

“Still suspended.”

The chamber placed those facts beside her objection. No one had to turn necessity into authority. They could show both and let later judgment carry the contradiction.

Sevrin pressed her commission seal into the witness field.

The challenge pane accepted seven named custodians, linked them to the full formation record, and released the outer index. Thirty-one continuance files appeared as sealed gray columns. Twenty-four carried Return Clause marks. Seven showed no filing.

Lio counted them before anyone read the summary. “No filing means no instruction?”

“It means the archive contains no authenticated Return Clause for that original,” Pell said. “It does not tell us whether they chose silence, missed the filing period, or used another notary.”

“So even silence has three versions.”

“At least three,” Sevrin said.

Ilyen looked at the seven blank columns. Law preferred an empty field to mean something exact. A null could preserve refusal, conceal loss, or mark an unmade choice. They could not award the dead a coherent intention by guessing which absence belonged to each person.

The twenty-four marked files divided again. Some allowed public reading. Others limited access to the related continuance, an estate representative, or a closure officer. Two required medical review before release. The Commonwealth seal had opened the archive index, not the private contents.

“Aggregate categories first,” Ilyen said. “No personal clause enters the public record unless its access terms allow it or the related continuance chooses release.”

The opposing archivist nodded, surprised perhaps that she had not used emergency power to expose everything at once. Pell asked the key for a privacy-preserving classification. The archive returned four labels drawn from the originals’ own selected forms: terminate or erase; archive or merge; permit independent continuation; no directive beyond legal process.

No category held a majority.

The aggregate also exposed conflicts hidden by the four labels. Three continuances carried instructions from more than one commissioning original. In one file, a spouse authorized independent continuation while the other directed immediate archive. Another pair agreed on autonomy and disagreed over use of the family name. The third split ownership of professional work from control of private memory in terms no receiving officer had tested.

Sevrin asked the key whether later filing dates superseded earlier ones. The archive answered only where the same original had amended a clause. It would not rank instructions from different people merely because one signed last.

“Then the form created a deadlock it had no rule to resolve,” Ama said.

“The old statute expected a Receiving Authority to weigh competing rights,” Sevrin replied.

“By closing the continuance into one of three outcomes.”

“Under the law, yes.”

Tovan enlarged the anonymous conflict map. “If two originals own one instrument, each can veto the other’s instruction. The instrument still carries the result.”

“Joint sponsorship contracts allocate tie-breaking authority,” Sevrin said.

“Where?”

“In the estate instrument.”

“Which the related continuance may not access.”

The Commissioner checked the archive permissions and found the barrier he had named. A closure officer could request the tie-break provision. The Commonwealth could not. Sevrin used her Receiving Authority seal to expose only the operative allocation, leaving estate details hidden. Two files granted the elder sponsor priority. The third required mutual consent from people now dead.

Pell marked that clause irresolvable under its own terms. No one celebrated the finding. A contradiction preserved in perfect authentication still left a living body waiting for an answer.

Ama read the distribution twice. “After all this, they disagreed.”

“They were people,” Sevrin said. “Agreement would have needed an institution.”

The first public clause opened without a name. Its original had authorized immediate erasure after verified death and forbidden use of the instance’s memories by the estate. The language described continued operation as a breach of bodily and informational consent. It also assigned remaining wages to the original’s dependents.

A second clause requested autonomy beyond the prediction horizon, including independent accounts and the right to refuse the original’s profession. Its author denied inheritance but authorized a start grant from personal funds. A handwritten note stated that a forecast forced to remain accurate had become a prison for both versions.

The third public clause directed archive and nothing else. No explanation. No affection. No warning. Four lines identified the approved repository, privacy term, query limits, and destruction date.

Lio stood at the edge of the table. His own file remained sealed to him and his parents’ estate representative. “Which one counts more?”

“None by category,” Sevrin replied. “Each clause can govern only within its own legal scope.”

“That is not what people will do. They will quote the kind they already wanted.”

“Yes.” Ilyen closed the public samples before rhetoric could make them representatives of everyone absent. “Our record should keep the denominator attached. Three examples among twenty-four filings, seven unfiled.”

Tovan requested private access to his file.

Witness Glass curved a semantic partition around him, Sevrin, and independent counsel. The rest of the chamber could see that a clause opened, how long they read, and which credentials touched it. Meaning stayed inside. Tovan had insisted on the arrangement before signing the archive act. His station duties, his stated interest in dissolution, and the emergency’s dependence on him made privacy unusually difficult and unusually necessary.

Ilyen watched his face through the blurred partition. He did not flinch. That told her nothing. Tovan met damage by reducing motion until every available part served the repair.

After six minutes, the partition cleared.

“I will release one fact,” he said. “The clause confirms my original’s expiration consent and requests dissolution after transfer of essential knowledge. It does not define essential or name who decides when transfer has completed.”

Ama gripped the edge of her slate. “You do not have to make an election today.”

“I know.”

“Nor does an undefined transfer let the station hold you forever.”

“I know that too.” Tovan placed his file back into privacy lock. “The instruction matters. It does not complete the decision.”

The Khet could have praised that formulation. Their absence helped. Holds-Four-Open had accepted foreign witness status for the Commonwealth vote, but Ilyen had not invited the speaker to the clause opening. Human dead deserved interpretation without an alien institution’s preference for unresolved commitments bending the room.

Lio chose not to open his file in session. “My parents wrote it for a version of me they thought they owned together. I want counsel before they get to surprise me again.”

Sevrin approved private counsel access without comment.

The decision left Ilyen’s column.

Her file permitted the related continuance, Original Ilyen’s estate representative, or a Receiving Authority to read it. Mara’s estate credential remained eleven days away from any new consent request. Sevrin already possessed receiving power. Ilyen could open the clause privately now.

“What do you want recorded?” Ama asked.

Ilyen saw the negotiation hidden inside the question. Privacy would protect her from performing grief for the Commonwealth. Public reading could establish evidence relevant to the civic-birth proposal. Either choice would be interpreted as strategy because she had taught everyone in the room to look for strategy.

“Open the authentication layer publicly,” she said. “Content first to me, Sevrin, and counsel. I will decide release after reading.”

Pell rendered the outer marks. Original Ilyen had filed the clause on Day minus 2,606, thirty-four days after the fork and eight days before Civic Mercy departed. The timing placed it within Ilyen’s missing ninety-one days. Biometric confidence exceeded the notary standard. The blue cup appeared only as a private memory challenge and remained sealed; the earlier recording had already established its provenance.

A partition rose.

Original Ilyen’s text began without greeting.

TO THE CONTINUANCE MADE FROM MY FORK, OR TO THE AUTHORITY RECEIVING IT AFTER MY DEATH OR RETURN:

Ilyen read the sentence twice. Original Ilyen had not used her name for the continuance. She had not written copy either. The refusal to name could carry caution, fear, or an attempt not to decide through grammar what law had not settled.

The next paragraph revoked any presumption of identity succession. The continuance could not claim the original’s marriage history, parenthood, public office, personal debts, or estate merely by proving shared memory. Vale could remain in records as provenance if no living family member faced misrepresentation. Any new relation required consent from both sides.

Mara’s refusal existed inside those limits without being predicted by them. Original Ilyen had protected her daughter from an imposed continuation of family. She had also left a narrow path for something neither woman had to name in advance.

Sevrin read with one hand held against the table. “This supports the preliminary ruling.”

“It supports non-succession,” Ilyen said.

“That distinction may matter later. At present, the ruling uses it.”

Counsel marked the exchange. Neither interpretation displaced the text.

A separate section addressed work and resources. Original Ilyen released her professional notes, treaty preparation, office instruments, and a limited duty account to the actor who continued the Khet negotiation. The clause refused to classify that actor as heir. It required an accounting to the estate for private material and barred access to family funds.

Near the end, one sentence appeared in the visible fragment Ilyen had already found on Day Twenty: Do not let fidelity decide alone.

The full paragraph continued.

Fidelity can show what I feared, promised, and prepared. It cannot tell you whether the conditions I understood still exist. Preserve my evidence. Give no order merely because it resembles what I would have chosen. Assign the burden and the tools to whoever bears the consequences.

A schedule followed the paragraph. It listed the treaty notebooks, a private language model trained on Original Ilyen’s negotiations, unused travel compensation, and the blue cup packed among her departure effects. Each item carried a different legal source. She owned the compensation and cup. The embassy owned the office instruments. The Khet archive held joint rights in translated notes. Original Ilyen had gathered them under one instruction because duty, not title, connected them.

Sevrin separated the schedule into legal columns. “She could assign the personal account subject to estate challenge. She could recommend custody for the office material. She could not transfer Khet rights unilaterally.”

“She knew that,” Ilyen said.

“Then why include them together?”

“Because a person accepting the work would need all four.”

“That explains purpose. It does not cure authority.”

Counsel marked each item accordingly. The clause had not created a single inheritance. It had assembled a proposed kit whose parts would require consent from different claimants. Ilyen felt the old familiar respect for a negotiation designed around an impossible room. She also felt irritation. Original Ilyen had left the hard work to someone she could imagine only as a functional successor.

The blue cup entry contained no family claim. It directed the holder of the treaty notes to keep, discard, or give away the cup without treating it as proof of identity. Ilyen remembered its glaze under her thumb, a memory shared before the fork. The physical cup had traveled with the original. If recovered effects had survived the casualty process, Mara’s estate rights would still reach it before any duty schedule did.

“Do not request that item yet,” Ilyen said.

Sevrin looked from the schedule to her. “Because it lacks operational value?”

“Because Mara should receive notice before the Commonwealth uses an estate artifact to authenticate my claim.”

“That may delay the schedule.”

“The treaty work does not depend on a cup.”

Counsel entered the hold. The decision cost Ilyen nothing material in the room. It still distinguished accepting tools from taking whatever shared memory made emotionally available.

Ilyen stopped reading.

The phrase did not grant life. It did not call her daughter, successor, or person. Original Ilyen had allocated resources around consequence while refusing identity. The structure resembled civic birth closely enough to tempt Ilyen into claiming endorsement. That temptation supplied its own warning.

“What follows?” Sevrin asked.

“A limitation.” Ilyen continued.

No authority could use the clause to compel the continuance to finish the treaty, accept the Vale name, preserve the original’s private relationships, or remain active. If the continuance refused the assigned work, the duty account returned to the embassy. If it accepted, accountability attached to the accepting actor from that date.

The clause ended with authentication instructions, not affection. Original Ilyen had offered neither erasure nor welcome. She had built a transfer point and refused to say who should cross it.

Sevrin dismissed counsel from the partition after the required silence. “Do you want to release the content?”

“Most of it.”

“Exceptions?”

“The memory challenge. Two private family references unrelated to authority. Nothing that changes the operative terms.”

“You understand release will strengthen the argument for prospective standing.”

“It will also strengthen the prohibition on succession.”

“That may not stop advocates from quoting only the sentence they prefer.”

“No record can prevent selection. It can keep the surrounding sentences available.”

Ilyen authorized public release with redactions. The partition lowered. Pell placed the full operative text beside the aggregate categories and linked every quotation to its context.

Ama reached the consequence paragraph first. “She gave you the treaty work.”

“She gave it to the actor who accepts its consequences.”

“You have done that.”

“I have performed the work. That does not settle which civil category should receive the account.”

Lio read the non-succession language. “She says you are not her, then writes rules for what you can choose.”

“She wrote rules for her property and duties,” Sevrin said. “She explicitly left the continuance’s status unsettled.”

“Adults like leaving a door open after putting a guard on every other exit.”

Ilyen almost smiled. “She also barred anyone from pushing me through it.”

Tovan came to the table and read the clause without touching his own sealed file. “Your original allowed refusal. Mine conditioned dissolution on a transfer no one defined. We did not inherit equal instructions.”

“No,” Ilyen said.

The Commonwealth custodians reviewed the remaining public clauses through the afternoon. One original demanded erasure to protect a spouse’s privacy. Another authorized any future choice except estate succession. Two directed silence: no recommendation, no posthumous argument, only access to independent counsel. An archive request contained a private apology but no change in legal disposition. Several files remained sealed to their related continuances.

Each instruction altered one person’s field of choice. None could govern the others.

At the end of the session, Pell asked how to classify the recovered archive in the Commonwealth record. The available labels included binding authority, advisory instrument, estate evidence, personal directive, and disputed claim.

“Use separate labels per clause,” Ilyen said.

“The index requires an archive-level description.”

Sevrin proposed “authenticated original intent, scope individually limited.” Tovan requested that present competence and consent remain explicit. Ama wanted the record to say the instructions carried moral force even where law could not compel them.

Ilyen listened until the disagreement stopped circling and began to show its useful edges.

“Classify the archive as evidence from the dead,” she said. “Authenticated, relevant, nonunanimous. No aggregate command.”

Pell entered the words. Witness Glass arranged the files according to their own access rules rather than a single disposition: erase requests in red, autonomy permissions in blue, archive and merge terms in white, no-directive clauses in gray, missing filings left uncolored.

Original Ilyen’s clause remained open at the sentence assigning tools to consequence. Tovan’s file closed under private seal. Lio’s waited unread for counsel. Seven blank columns preserved what the archive could not know.

The Commonwealth mark settled over the index without changing any color. Thirty-one separate fields remained visible after the dead had finished speaking.

Chapter 24 — The Continuity Covenant

Sevrin cut the word successor from the draft with a red legal stylus.

Witness Glass preserved the stroke as an amendment rather than an erasure. The rejected word remained visible beneath a narrow scar of light, linked to her commission seal and the local hour. Across the table, Ilyen Vale watched without attempting to recover it. Three columns of Return Clauses stood behind her, their incompatible instructions reduced to colored references: erasure, autonomy, silence. None could govern the others. None could settle the status of the people assembled on Orison.

“Again,” Sevrin said. “From the act that changes status.”

Ilyen drew the opening section back to the center. “A continuance may petition for civic birth after performing a future-bearing action under conditions of informed accountability.”

“Performed for whom?”

“For the instance who must bear its consequences.”

“That assumes the conclusion.” Sevrin placed two fingers against the table until the temptation to tap passed. “If I sign a contract for you, the consequences may burden you. The signature does not make me you.”

“No. It makes you answerable for signing.”

“Then write answerability, not identity.”

Ilyen changed the clause. The glass annotated every alteration, preserving their quarrel in layers. They had been drafting for six hours and had produced less than a page. Sevrin trusted that ratio more than easy agreement. Law intended to survive grief should acquire its sentences slowly.

Outside the chamber, Orison continued under revoked licenses and provisional local authority. Thermal use had stabilized, but only because Tovan rationed heat by hand and logged every substitution. Earth had not recognized the Commonwealth. The Khet had not accepted the proposed category. Sevrin’s own commission still empowered her to perform closure, although the public record now contained enough objections to make immediate use of that power indefensible.

She opened a new definition pane. “A future-bearing action must exceed reliable prediction from the fork model, create a durable obligation or change in conditions, and receive independent witness. Otherwise ordinary adaptive behavior will become a birth ceremony whenever convenient.”

“The prediction horizon can supply evidence,” Ilyen said. “It cannot supply a moral threshold by itself.”

“Agreed. A surprising breakfast preference does not found a citizen.”

Ilyen’s mouth moved at one corner. “Lio will object to the example before he objects to the rule.”

“He can do both in twenty minutes. Keep going.”

Together they drafted the petition requirements. The applicant would disclose the original, the fork date, every material gap in memory, and any unresolved Return Clause. The Commonwealth would record a provenance name without treating it as succession. Civic birth would create no automatic inheritance, kinship, property title, office, or authority derived from the original. Obligations could pass only when the applicant assumed them with informed consent and affected parties received a chance to object.

Sevrin inserted the phrase provenance disclosure and required it on public contracts, kinship petitions, fiduciary work, and any use of an original’s professional seal. Ilyen narrowed the requirement for ordinary social life.

“A person should not have to announce her fork history to buy soup,” she said.

“A person using an original’s reputation to obtain credit should.”

“Yes.”

The agreement landed without warmth. That also reassured Sevrin. They had found a line both could defend for different reasons.

At midday, Ama brought tea and a plate of ration cakes. Her right hand shook hard enough to set the cups clicking against the tray. She refused Sevrin’s offered help with a look, then accepted Ilyen’s decision to steady only the plate. The accommodation contained practice rather than pity.

“Your witnesses are waiting,” Ama said. “Lio threatened to submit a procedural complaint about adult delay.”

“Did he draft it?” Sevrin asked.

“He corrected Pell’s form first.”

“Admit them.”

Lio entered ahead of Tovan, carrying a slate under one arm. He had written three objections before taking his chair. Tovan sat with the measured care of someone who still monitored his own thermal draw as a public promise. Sevrin displayed the proposed title.

CONTINUITY COVENANT: PROVISIONAL ARTICLES FOR CIVIC BIRTH, CLOSURE, AND ASSUMED DUTY.

Lio read it twice. “Continuity sounds like you think we continue someone.”

“It names continuity of evidence and obligation,” Sevrin said. “Not identity.”

“Then why not call it the Evidence and Obligation Covenant?”

“Because no one would remember it.”

He considered that answer, apparently decided it showed adequate honesty, and moved to the first article. “You require a future-bearing act. I have attended school for seven years beyond the fork. I chose subjects Original Lio never knew existed. Do I have to release a state secret before I qualify?”

“No,” Ilyen said. “The act must carry consequences, not spectacle.”

“Choosing training carries consequences.”

“It can,” Sevrin said. “Tell us which ones.”

Lio opened his slate. He had applied, without legal authority, to an archive-architecture program maintained by the embassy’s Khet liaison staff. The curriculum would take nine local years. It required him to abandon the sponsor-defined education track created for Original Lio’s family. Acceptance would consume a scarce mentorship place and bind him to supervised archive work.

“That choice falls beyond your tested prediction horizon?” Sevrin asked.

“By four years and nine months.”

“Can you withdraw?”

“From the course, yes. From having chosen it, no.”

“Who bears the cost if you leave?”

“The program loses time. I lose the place. The registry keeps the attempt.”

His answers came quickly, but not carelessly. Sevrin marked the application as a plausible qualifying act without deciding it. The later vote would establish procedures; this session could only test whether the rule produced intelligible questions.

She changed the hypothetical. Suppose a continuance aged nine petitioned after refusing a sponsor’s curriculum. Could the Commonwealth grant civic birth while also appointing a guardian? Lio objected to treating dependence as disqualification. Tovan asked who could consent to assumed obligations for a minor. Ilyen separated personhood from capacity. Sevrin added a clause allowing civic birth without full contractual power, subject to ordinary protections based on demonstrated capacity rather than origin.

“Ordinary protections do not exist for us,” Lio said.

“They will if the Commonwealth adopts them.”

“That answer depends on the same vote as the Covenant.”

“Yes,” Sevrin said. “A lawful structure may depend on authority it does not yet possess. It may not pretend the authority already exists.”

Lio entered that sentence in his objections, though Sevrin could not tell whether he meant to challenge or preserve it.

Tovan had left his copy untouched. His hands rested on either side of the slate, palms down. When Sevrin asked him to test the obligation article, he read Original Tovan’s expiration consent aloud. The language had survived three hearings and one thermal cascade. It directed any licensed instance to accept dissolution after authenticated death unless essential safety work required a brief extension.

“Under this draft,” Tovan said, “I can refuse that instruction by applying for birth.”

“You can petition,” Sevrin replied. “The instruction enters as evidence. It does not operate as a command against a person whose status has changed.”

“And if the petition succeeds, I can assume the original’s duties selectively.”

“With consent from affected parties.”

“That invites convenience. I keep his professional standing, decline his expiration, and leave his creditors to the estate.”

Ilyen leaned forward. “Would you ask to keep his professional standing?”

“No.”

“Test the rule with someone who would.”

Tovan did. He constructed a continuance physician who sought civic birth, claimed the original’s license, refused malpractice debt, and used shared memories to attract patients. Sevrin removed every automatic transfer. The new person could seek a license through examination, assume selected debts through novation, and disclose provenance when describing prior experience. Memories could support competence but not substitute for current certification.

Next he offered an engineer whose original had committed sabotage before the fork and whose continuance possessed the plan. Sevrin rejected inherited criminal guilt. Ilyen required disclosure of known ongoing danger. They wrote a duty to report actionable hazards without converting shared memory into culpability.

Tovan’s third test concerned himself. “Suppose I accept civic birth and later decide the expiration consent carried a judgment I should honor. Does the Commonwealth prevent dissolution to preserve its claim that I count?”

No one answered at once.

Sevrin studied the language they had used for voluntary closure. The first version required a competency review, a waiting period, and evidence against coercion. It also permitted the Commonwealth to delay dissolution during an emergency if the applicant held essential knowledge. That exception resembled the old instrument law too closely. Necessity could become ownership with a change of heading.

She deleted it.

Ilyen looked up. “The station may depend on him.”

“Then the station must persuade him, train others, or accept the cost. Civic birth cannot mean self-ownership except during inconvenient weeks.”

Tovan’s fingers moved from the slate. “The petition should preserve a future right to dissolve.”

“It should preserve every competent person’s right to request it,” Sevrin said. “With safeguards against pressure from originals, sponsors, estates, and the Commonwealth itself.”

Ama signed as medical reviewer. Her witness mark wandered before the seal accepted it. She did not apologize.

The afternoon brought hostile hypotheticals from the public queue. Pell had stripped names and grouped them by legal issue. A widower asked whether his dead husband’s continuance could petition for civic birth, then use private memories to force contact. A creditor asked whether a newly born person could hide assets behind the dead estate. An election office asked whether one original could fork a thousand continuances and manufacture a voting bloc. A parent asked who protected a child from a continuance carrying the voice of a dead caregiver.

Sevrin made them answer each one.

Privacy law barred coercive use of shared memories whether or not civic birth occurred. Property transferred only through an independent grant or accepted obligation, never through resemblance. Multiple instances could receive recognition, but registry disclosure and residence rules governed voting like any other coordinated constituency. Kinship required new consent from living parties. No stored affection created access to a home, a child, or an estate.

Sevrin then tested office. The Covenant could not grant Ilyen the dead envoy’s commission by succession, yet Orison needed someone able to finish the treaty. Ilyen argued that the Commonwealth could appoint any qualified citizen after civic birth. Sevrin agreed, provided the appointment began under local authority and carried a fresh seal. Memory of the original’s negotiations could establish competence. It could not smuggle old sovereign power through a new registry entry.

They built a mock appointment in Witness Glass. Ilyen surrendered her present Continuance Office credential into a sealed pane. Its permissions vanished from the treaty console one by one: archive request, embassy signature, privileged relay, emergency representation. The temporary loss left the room physically unchanged. Sevrin still felt it as a structural silence. For months, opponents had claimed the office alone explained every judgment Ilyen made. Without the credential, Ilyen remained seated, remembered the clauses, and could answer questions. She simply lacked authority to bind anyone.

The Commonwealth clerk on duty issued a test appointment under the proposed law. A fresh seal appeared beside Ilyen’s name, limited to a training chamber and marked without legal effect. She used it to open a duplicate treaty page. Witness Glass accepted the local credential while preserving the absent Earth delegation as a separate field.

“Would that satisfy the Khet?” Ilyen asked.

“It may satisfy a machine,” Sevrin said. “The Khet will answer for their treaty.”

“Will it satisfy Earth?”

“Not today.”

Sevrin revoked the test appointment and restored the existing office credential. The exercise proved less than Ilyen wanted and more than old instrument law allowed. Authority could leave one legal source and enter another without any claim that the office holder had returned from death. The seam remained visible.

Tovan proposed a harsher version. Suppose an infrastructure continuance withheld essential knowledge until the Commonwealth granted office. The act might exceed prediction and carry consequences, but coercion should not qualify as a civic threshold merely because it forced recognition. Sevrin added independent review of the claimed future-bearing act. The review would consider duress, manufactured danger, and harm imposed to create leverage. It could reject the act as qualification without deciding whether the applicant deserved continued existence.

Lio objected to the last distinction. “You keep separating eligibility from existence. Earth law does not.”

“This law must,” Sevrin said. “A failed petition leaves status unresolved. It does not authorize punishment disguised as classification.”

Ilyen resisted one provision: mandatory separation of all pre-fork confidences from public testimony.

“Some memories prove abuse,” she said. “Some prove promises made to people without other witnesses.”
