“Then admit them under ordinary evidentiary rules,” Sevrin replied. “Do not let civic birth convert intimacy into unrestricted property.”

“Agreed. But the court must hear the bearer.”

“As a witness to memory, with provenance disclosed. Not as the deceased returning to testify.”

The wording held. Sevrin marked it for challenge on Earth.

Pell sent the final hostile case alone. A dying parent had revised an estate under pressure from a continuance who claimed exact filial identity. After the transfer, the original daughter lost the home she had maintained for twelve years. Could the continuance seek civic birth and keep the proceeds?

Sevrin’s hand left the stylus.

The case omitted names, but Pell had drawn it from her commission history. Her younger sister, Nera, had commissioned a continuance during treatment for neural decline. That instance had approached their father after Nera’s death and called every shared memory proof of uninterrupted daughterhood. Their father, already failing, changed the estate. Sevrin challenged too late. The house passed to a legal construct that spoke with Nera’s timing and knew where their mother had hidden winter presents.

Ilyen closed the public queue. “We can use another case.”

“No.” Sevrin heard the clipped refusal and let it stand. “This one built part of the law we are replacing.”

Tovan and Lio remained still. Neither offered sympathy. She valued the restraint.

Sevrin reopened the hypothetical and separated its acts. The instance’s continued existence had not caused the coercion. Shared memory had supplied leverage. False succession claims had defeated the living daughter’s standing. A vulnerable adult had executed a transfer without independent counsel. Those failures demanded remedies, but dissolution would not restore consent already violated.

“The Covenant would recognize the continuance as a new person,” Lio said.

“If it performed a qualifying act and passed review.”

“Even after that?”

“Yes. Personhood cannot function as a prize for innocence.”

Tovan looked toward the estate clause. “Would it keep the house?”

“Not by inheritance. A court would review the transfer for coercion. If the transfer failed, the property would return to the estate. Any independent property acquired afterward would remain with the new person.”

Ilyen asked, “Would you require it to stop using your sister’s name?”

Sevrin pictured the signature that had taken her father’s house: Nera Hale, without qualification. Anger returned with its old precision. It no longer carried a complete legal theory.

“I would require provenance disclosure where the name could create reliance,” she said. “I would deny automatic kinship. I would not let my injury turn continuity into a crime.”

The distinction did not pardon the instance. It did not repair the estate or give Sevrin back the years spent arguing that a familiar voice could manipulate as effectively as a stranger. It placed blame on conduct rather than origin. Her commission had never managed that division. The draft might.

She entered a remedy article: recognition would not cure prior fraud, coercion, or unlawful transfer. Nor would prior misconduct alone erase civic status once lawfully granted. Liability followed acts. Provenance supplied evidence, not guilt.

By the station’s late watch, the draft held seven articles and two annexes. Sevrin ran it against Earth’s remote-instrument statute. The Covenant conflicted with mandatory closure provisions, but the Commonwealth’s emergency charter could suspend those locally if its founding vote survived review. She tested inheritance codes, licensing law, guardianship, criminal responsibility, privacy, debt, and treaty capacity. Some provisions required later implementing law. None depended on identity succession.

Ilyen stood beside her while Witness Glass compiled the review map. “You could still close us under your commission.”

“I could issue the order.”

“That did not answer the statement.”

“No.” Sevrin watched red conflict lines narrow into amber questions. “Orison’s enforcement personnel recognize the Commonwealth. Earth’s public record contains the dispute. The Khet sanctuary would complicate custody. My order would have text and no clean execution.”

“Then the draft has local force.”

“It has local coherence. Force requires adoption.”

Ilyen accepted the correction. She placed her office seal beside Sevrin’s commission mark, not as approval, but as co-author authentication. The glass requested a document status.

Sevrin chose PROPOSED. She refused RATIFIED and declined the softer STUDY DRAFT. Proposed meant someone intended the words to govern and admitted they did not yet.

They transmitted one certified copy toward Earth. Pell’s delay ledger opened an eleven-day route clock beneath it. A second copy went to the Khet archive desk with a request for treaty recognition. Sevrin attached no claim that either destination would agree.

The chamber rendered its final conflicts. Under local emergency law, the draft could form a coherent category. Under Earth law, it lacked recognition. Under the access treaty, no Khet signature yet supported it. The three fields remained separate instead of collapsing into a false approval.

Sevrin capped the red stylus and set it across the first page. Beneath the transparent scar, successor remained readable among the words they had refused. Above it, the proposed civic-birth clause waited for authorities outside the room.

Chapter 25 — The Archive Does Not Answer

Holds-Four-Open declined the first seal before Ilyen finished presenting it.

A red boundary crossed the proposed Covenant where its civic-birth article touched the Khet treaty record. Four amber apertures hovered beyond Witness Glass, each carrying a thread of the temporary speaker. Three remained bright. The coolest had contracted almost to darkness, refusing authorization without leaving the construct.

Ilyen stopped the document before the chamber could classify the rejection as final. “State the defect.”

The translator held the Khet clicks for two breaths, arranging relations into human sequence. “The human seal requests recognition of an entity category. This exchange carries authority over archive access, treaty obligations, and witnessed commitments. It carries no authority to determine which human forms count as persons.”

“I am not asking you to amend human law.”

“You request Khet recognition for use in a human vote.”

“I request recognition for treaty purposes. The vote remains ours.”

The dark aperture widened a fraction. Another dimmed in answer. Their disagreement traveled visibly through the speaker before any words reached her.

On Ilyen’s side of the table, the Continuity Covenant lay open in seven articles and two annexes. Sevrin’s rejected word successor remained under its transparent scar. The status fields still showed PROPOSED, EARTH UNRECOGNIZED, KHET UNSIGNED. Ilyen had brought no one else into the chamber. A delegation could have distributed the refusal among witnesses. Solitude required her to hear what she had actually asked.

Holds-Four-Open said, “For treaty use, identify the bearer of obligation.”

“The Orison Commonwealth.”

“That body holds provisional human authority.”

“Yes.”

“Within it, some decision bearers claim civic birth. Others retain instrument classification under remote human law. The proposed articles would permit each claimant to choose among birth, archive, continued suspension, or dissolution.”

“Subject to capacity and coercion safeguards.”

“The variation remains. Which entity asks recognition?”

Ilyen had negotiated with the Khet long enough to recognize a narrowing question. They would not let a collective noun hide the contested actors inside it. “The Commonwealth asks you to recognize its authority to create treaty-capable citizens under the Covenant.”

“That phrasing requests recognition of the Commonwealth.”

“Yes.”

“It does not request a finding on the continuances.”

“The Commonwealth consists partly of continuances.”

“It also consists partly of biological humans. It was declared by a narrow vote under an emergency compact. Recognition of the polity would not answer whether each participant entered the vote with valid standing.”

Ilyen looked toward the lowest seal line. She had used that ambiguity herself when the emergency council recovered the Compact phrase, persons present and answerable. The provisional assembly rested on presence, conduct, and necessity while declining to settle every underlying status. Now she wanted Khet recognition to flow backward through the vote and cure its founding doubt.

“You recognize unresolved commitments as claimants,” she said.

“We hold them open.”

“You offered us archive standing.”

“We offered standing to the intentions carried through you.”

“Through us.” Ilyen kept her voice level. “Not to us.”

Two apertures brightened. The answer came slower. “The distinction has produced dispute among the four.”

“Then show me the dispute.”

Holds-Four-Open rotated its threads into separate witness lanes. Witness Glass assigned no names, only colors and authorization weights. The warmest thread treated each continuance as an accountable context because post-fork experience had changed available choices. A pale thread treated the continuance as an intention-bearing structure whose human classification remained irrelevant to Khet duty. The cool thread feared that civic birth would close obligations owed to originals. The dimmest thread objected to any Khet judgment that human negotiators could cite as moral authority over other humans.

Ilyen read the four positions twice. “Your first thread supports our claim.”

“It supports continued accountability.”

“That produces civic birth under our Covenant.”

“No. Your Covenant includes rights, property limits, dissolution procedure, liability, and political membership. Continued accountability supplies evidence for those choices. It cannot decide them.”

“You have accepted human envoys as treaty persons for decades.”

“We accepted offices able to bind human institutions.”

“You negotiated with me after Earth suspended my status.”

“We negotiated with an Unclosed Agent whose decisions could alter access.”

“Because I could choose.”

“Because several accountable contexts still claimed your choice.”

The answer scraped against an old wound with unusual accuracy. Khet standing had protected her when Earth called her an instrument, but it had not required the Khet to see an independent person. They valued the unresolved path she carried. The path could outlast her, pass into an archive, or remain suspended forever without violating their first principle.

Ilyen changed tactics. She brought forward the treaty’s archive-key article, leaving its authorization field blank. “You required a future-bearing action. Release of this key will permanently alter your access to human records. The Covenant assigns the consequence to the civic actor who authorizes it. Will you recognize that actor for purposes of receiving the key?”

“We can recognize the authorization if its human chain proves valid.”

“Will you recognize the authorizer as a person?”

“No.”

The word crossed the translator without qualification.

Ilyen let the chamber record six seconds of silence. She could have hidden disappointment inside another procedural request. The Khet would have noticed the request, not the emotion. Witness Glass would preserve both only as elapsed time and pulse variance. Neither record would say she had expected a civilization organized around open decisions to welcome people built from them.

“Explain the refusal,” she said.

“Human personhood governs harms and permissions beyond this treaty. It structures kinship, bodily authority, ownership, guilt, education, care, and death. We do not occupy those relations. A Khet decision would reach farther than our accountability.”

“You occupied them when you offered sanctuary.”

“We offered an archive relation under Khet control. We did not claim the relation satisfied human dignity.”

“It did not.”

All four apertures held steady.

The simple agreement changed her approach. “When you offered archive standing, Lio would have remained protected as an unresolved intention. Could he have chosen training beyond his sponsor model?”

“Not if the choice closed archive evidence still claimed by accountable contexts.”

“Could Tovan have chosen dissolution?”

“The archive would preserve the decision bearer required to hold the consent dispute open.”

“Against his present choice.”

“Yes.”

“Could I have released the key?”

“Not while the original’s protective assignment retained an unresolved claim.”

Ilyen spread the three answers beside the Covenant. Under Khet sanctuary, none of them needed to die. None could fully depart from the purpose that made the archive value them. Preservation had offered refuge from Earth’s closure power while fastening each continuance to unfinished original intentions.

“You call that standing,” she said.

“We call it archive standing.”

“Would you accept it for yourselves?”

The translator paused long enough to request an additional syntax lane. One aperture dimmed; two crossed their light; the fourth remained separate.

“We have,” Holds-Four-Open said. “The four decision threads entered this speaker under commitments none may complete alone. Each may withdraw only through a recorded closure that alters the construct. Human observers describe this as office. Some Khet disputes describe it as captivity.”

The admission carried no dramatic shift in tone. Khet translation did not know confession. It simply placed a disputed relation into the record.

Ilyen called up the oldest archive entry from the Ledger of Open Hands. Centuries earlier, a Khet closure tribunal had ended a civil conflict by deleting the authority of dissenting threads. Open-holding developed in answer. The doctrine preserved alternatives because completion had once erased them.

A second entry followed the famous one, less often translated for human visitors. Three districts had disputed the return of a contaminated archive. Open-holding preserved every remedy while seals failed, families relocated, and the contamination spread through storage membranes. By the time Khet institutions authorized destruction of the affected records, no original claimant still occupied the district. The unresolved decision had protected all four remedies until none could repair the loss.

“Why omit this entry from the public doctrine summary?” Ilyen asked.

“It does not establish the doctrine.”

“It establishes its cost.”

“The cost remains disputed. One thread classifies the lost archive as preferable to wrongful destruction. Another assigns harm to procedural delay. Two classify the comparison as incomplete because the relocated families carried secondary records.”

“Did the families choose relocation?”

“Some did. Others received no safe alternative.”

“Did any accountable body compensate them?”

“After closure, yes.”

“How many human years passed?”

The translator converted the Khet intervals. “Twenty-eight point six.”

Ilyen sent the entry into the treaty annex, not as an accusation but as relevant institutional history. The Khet had shown humans the erased-dissent case whenever explaining why decisions deserved preservation. The contaminated archive revealed the opposite danger: alternatives could consume the people forced to carry them.

She brought up Tovan’s pending options. His original’s expiration consent claimed dissolution. His seven years of service, the thermal cascade, and his later transparent repairs supported continued authorship. Khet archive standing could hold both claims indefinitely. “If he accepts your protection, who may later say the dispute has lasted long enough?”

“The participating contexts may request closure.”

“Original Tovan cannot request anything.”

“His authenticated instruction retains standing.”

“Then one context can never change its position.”

“Yes.”

“And you call that an open decision.”

“We call it a decision with an unwithdrawn claim.”

The legal precision made the trap clearer. Human death froze one side. Khet care converted that frozen instruction into permanent dissent, giving the dead an advantage the living could not outwait.

Ilyen tested Lio next. Suppose his parents’ sponsor terms required educational continuity while Lio sought archive training. Under Khet practice, the sponsor purpose would remain claim-bearing even if every living guardian withdrew it, because the original commissioning decision survived in authenticated form. Would his own later choice ever outrank it?

“Ranking does not describe open-holding,” the speaker said.

“Action does. Can he enroll?”

“Only if enrollment preserves a path by which the sponsor claim may still receive an answer.”

“A nine-year course consumes the years in which the sponsor curriculum applies.”

“Then enrollment may perform closure.”

“Which your archive would resist.”

“Yes.”

Ilyen placed Tovan’s consent beside Lio’s curriculum. The Khet doctrine treated both as unfinished commitments. Human experience distinguished them: one concerned a competent adult’s possible death, the other a young person’s education. No archive relation could erase that difference, yet open-holding supplied no native tool for weighing it.

“You built your practice against forced closure,” she said. “We built the Covenant against forced continuation as someone else.”

“Those aims can align.”

“They do not coincide.”

“No.”

“What happens when open-holding prevents needed closure?”

The phrase entered Witness Glass exactly: needed closure. Its human directness required the translator to separate necessity from preference. The four apertures exchanged authorization in small pulses.

At last the speaker answered. “The unresolved claim can consume the conditions needed for any claimant to act. It can preserve injury after the injured contexts have changed. It can grant old dissent permanent rule over those who inherited the dispute. Khet practice contains closures for these cases, though our institutions use them reluctantly.”

“Your sanctuary offer contained none.”

“We judged preservation more urgent than human self-determination.”

“You judged without claiming to judge.”

“Yes.”

Ilyen sat back. Holds-Four-Open had not become a moral oracle by admitting fault. The Khet had named a bias in their procedure, not supplied the missing human verdict. Yet the admission removed one refuge. She could no longer present archive standing to uncertain voters as a neutral safe option. It protected bodies and records. It also deferred futures.

“Amend the sanctuary offer,” she said. “State those limits in the human rendering.”

“That request lies within this exchange.”

A new annotation opened. Together they wrote that archive standing preserved an unresolved intention for Khet purposes, did not confer human personhood, and could restrict closure or independent action. Any continuance accepting it would receive a human-language warning. The Commonwealth could not call the offer equivalent to civic status.

When the amendment sealed, Ilyen returned to the Covenant. “If you will not recognize civic birth, what will you recognize?”

“The Khet archive can witness the conditions under which humans choose.”

“Define witness.”

“We can preserve the proposed articles, voter roll, objections, separate tallies, and authorization chain. We can attest that no later party altered the record.”

“That duplicates Witness Glass.”

“Witness Glass carries human seals now disputed by human authorities. Khet custody supplies an independent record.”

“Will you attest that the result deserves compliance?”

“No.”

“Will you treat a passed Covenant as sufficient treaty capacity?”

“Not in advance.”

Ilyen felt irritation sharpen into usefulness. “Then your offer amounts to storage.”

“Storage without alteration across jurisdictions. Also presence during the choice.”

“And archive standing remains available to individuals who reject civic birth?”

“Yes, under the amended warning.”

“What about the treaty itself?”

“If human authority validly releases the key under safeguards we can accept, the archive access treaty may proceed. Our receipt of the key will not certify the metaphysics of its releaser.”

“I did not ask for metaphysics.”

“You asked for personhood.”

The correction deserved the room it took. Ilyen walked to the edge of Witness Glass, where Orison’s rotation carried a field of dim stars upward. Behind her, the Covenant remained caught against the red Khet boundary. She had come to remove that mark before the vote. Instead she had learned which part no external signature could remove.

“Offer treaty witness in formal language,” she said.

The four threads recombined. “The Khet archive will serve as treaty witness to the human proceeding. It will preserve each question separately, record participation and abstention, retain all objections, and attest the unaltered result. Such witness grants no moral absolution, no human status, and no advance treaty recognition.”

“Add that your refusal does not constitute a finding against civic birth.”

“Accepted.”

“Add that archive standing does not outrank civic birth, dissolution, or continued unrecognized residence.”

“That ranking belongs to humans.”

“Then state that you provide none.”

The warm aperture brightened. The final language appeared: the Khet offered distinct relations without ranking them under human law.

Ilyen reviewed the terms as she would an adversary’s concession. Independent custody would protect the vote from later erasure. Separate tallies would prevent the Commonwealth question from smuggling the Covenant, key release, and individual choices into one result. Recorded abstention would matter for continuances who refused every offered category. Khet presence would not validate the voters. It would make falsifying what they chose harder.

That had value. It did not offer permission.

“Transmit the witness offer to the provisional assembly,” she said.

Before sealing it, Holds-Four-Open opened one thread alone. The dimmest aperture now carried enough light to speak.

“This thread asks whether the human envoy will describe our refusal as abandonment.”

“I have not decided how to describe it.”

“The description may change the vote.”

“Yes.”

“Then we hold a claim in your choice.”

Ilyen almost laughed. “You hold claims in weather if it changes a route.”

“Incorrect. Weather bears no answer.”

“Neither does an archive.”

The other three apertures shifted. Translation rendered their shared response without defensiveness. “An archive preserves conditions for answers. It should not replace those who must give them.”

There lay the useful sentence, dangerous because it sounded like wisdom she could carry back as endorsement. Ilyen refused the gift in that form.

“Enter it as your account of institutional limits,” she said. “Not as advice to the assembly.”

“Accepted.”

The witness offer sealed beside the rejected recognition request. Both would travel into the vote record. Ilyen removed the Covenant from the Khet boundary, leaving its status unchanged.

At the chamber door, the speaker addressed her through all four threads. “Unclosed Agent, will the human choice proceed without our classification?”

“Yes.”

“Does that close your claim on us?”

“It changes it. If we vote to release the key, you will answer as treaty parties. You will not answer for who we count as human.”

The door opened on the embassy’s central passage. Ama waited there with a medical slate tucked against her chest, her right hand trembling along its edge. Pell stood beside a portable witness terminal. Sevrin had taken the chair nearest the wall, leaving the central place vacant for Ilyen without making a ceremony of it. Lio and Tovan watched from the assembly feed.

Ama searched Ilyen’s face. “Did they recognize it?”

“No.”

Pell lowered his eyes to the terminal. Sevrin did not move.

Ilyen gave them the precise result. The Khet had declined personhood jurisdiction. They had amended archive standing to disclose its constraints. They offered independent custody of separate tallies and would witness the proceeding without endorsing its morality. Treaty recognition would depend on the authorization humans produced, not on an alien declaration about their nature.

Lio’s voice came from the terminal. “So they think we might count, but they won’t say.”

“They think the saying does not belong to them.”

“Convenient.”

“Yes.” Ilyen looked toward Sevrin. “Also correct.”

Sevrin rose and opened the assembly schedule. Four blank questions waited beneath Day Sixty-One. None displayed a projected result. “Then we publish the refusal with the witness terms.”

Ilyen authenticated both records. She did not soften the Khet admission or turn it into support. The public queue accepted the documents. Across Orison, voter slates opened their unmarked fields.

Behind the closed chamber, four amber apertures kept the Covenant without approving it. Ahead, the provisional assembly clock began its final seventy-two hours with no external answer attached.

Chapter 26 — The Vote to Continue

Ama’s hand shook against the voter roll, moving her seal half a line below her name.

Witness Glass did not correct the mark. It measured her pulse pattern, matched the pressure sequence, and accepted the imperfect stroke as hers. A copy entered Khet custody at the same instant. Across the chamber, four amber apertures recorded the action without blessing it.

“Forty-six eligible residents,” Pell announced. “Forty-six credentials tested. No proxy ballots. No remote votes. Thirty-one participants hold disputed Continuance status under Earth law. Fifteen are biological residents. The roll records both classifications and grants neither priority.”

Captain Edrin and Welfare Officer Venn had departed with the courier on Day Fifty-Two. Their temporary station credentials closed with the hatch, reducing the living roll from seventeen at the Commonwealth’s founding vote to fifteen.

Sevrin stood beside the challenge desk in her Commissioner’s gray, where anyone could see the commission seal she had not surrendered. “My objection remains attached. Participation under the Embassy Compact does not settle legal status outside Orison.”

Ilyen sat at the central table with the proposed Covenant closed before her. “Your objection is admitted. Does it challenge any particular credential?”

“No.”

The answer cleared the roll. Pell sealed it and opened the first of four questions. Every slate in the station received the same text. Witness Glass displayed only receipt marks until the voting interval closed; no one could bargain from an early tally.

Ilyen had spent much of her working life arranging order. In a treaty session, order determined which concession acquired the dignity of a principle and which looked like payment. Here, each sequence carried a similar danger. A vote on civic birth before the Commonwealth could claim authority not yet granted. A vote on the polity first could hide personal status inside institutional survival. They had answered by separating everything and allowing the first result to fail without canceling later individual choices.

Pell read the question aloud. “Shall the provisional Orison Commonwealth continue as the local civic authority under the Embassy Compact, subject to external review and without claim to automatic Earth recognition?”

No speech followed. Arguments had remained open for seventy-two hours. Ama had required that discipline after the hearing record grew too large for a working resident to read between shifts. Each side received a summary, a full index, and equal relay position. The final hour belonged to choice rather than performance.

Ilyen touched her slate. YES, NO, and ABSTAIN appeared with no default position.

A no vote would not suspend her. Sevrin’s closure powers would remain contested, the Khet sanctuary offer would remain available, and Earth law would continue to classify her as an instrument allowed to perform existing duties. A yes vote would establish only local authority, born from a charter clause no drafter had intended for this use. Neither field offered safety.

Her thumb settled on YES. She authenticated the choice with the office credential whose ownership remained disputed.

The slate asked whether she wished to attach a public reason. She declined. Her arguments already occupied thousands of words. A ballot did not need to become another brief.

Receipt marks accumulated around the chamber. Tovan voted from thermal control because the station could not spare him for ceremony. Lio sat in the first public row, elbows tucked against his sides, refusing Ama’s attempt to see whether he had finished. Sevrin cast her ballot at the challenge desk and closed her slate face down.

When the interval ended, Pell locked all forty-six receipts. Witness Glass recomputed the result. The Khet archive returned an independent checksum before the numbers appeared.

YES: 24.

NO: 21.

ABSTAIN: 1.

The Commonwealth continued by three votes. Its Day Forty-Nine declaration had survived a second narrow vote, but Ilyen did not call that margin legitimacy. It supplied authority under the procedure they had chosen. The twenty-one refusals entered the same permanent record.

Pell opened objections for numerical challenge. One maintenance officer asked to verify that abstention had not been counted against the majority threshold. The Compact required more yes votes than no among authenticated ballots; abstentions preserved participation without entering either side. Pell showed the calculation. Sevrin verified it under her commission. Holds-Four-Open attested that Khet custody contained forty-six unaltered selections matching the human record.

“No arithmetic challenge remains,” Pell said.

Only then did Ilyen allow herself to breathe against the back of her teeth. The first question had not settled her status. It had created an authority capable of asking the second.

The Continuity Covenant opened across Witness Glass. Seven articles and two annexes spread into distinct panes: civic birth after a witnessed future-bearing act; disclosure of provenance where reliance could arise; no automatic inheritance or kinship; no transfer of guilt; voluntary assumption of obligations; capacity protections; access to archive, continued transition, or dissolution without ranking. Sevrin’s scar through successor remained visible in the drafting history.

Pell read the adoption question. “Shall the Orison Commonwealth enact the Continuity Covenant as local law, effective upon certification, while recording all conflicts with Earth statutes for provisional review?”

This time the chamber allowed closing statements because the law would bind conduct immediately. The opposition speaker, a living food-synthesis technician named Darel In, stood at the public rail. He did not question whether continuances suffered. He argued that crisis could not grant a remote station power to create citizens, expose estates, or redefine death procedure.

“If Earth can lose jurisdiction whenever delay becomes painful,” Darel said, “then delay law has no jurisdiction anywhere it matters. You are asking us to call necessity a constitution.”

Ilyen entered the objection without answering. Sevrin had made a version of it throughout the drafting. Serious opposition deserved more than a staged rebuttal.

The supporting statement came from Ama. Her right hand rested against the rail while her left held the text. “Earth law classifies thirty-one residents as instruments and still relies on their labor, memory, and liability. That contradiction has material consequences in this station. The Covenant does not settle identity. It makes conduct answerable to a law that can see the actor.”

Her tremor moved through the rail. Witness Glass recorded it with the words.

Lio requested one sentence. Pell recognized him.

“If you vote no because the Covenant might be wrong,” Lio said, “choose what happens to us while you wait to become certain.”

“That exceeds one sentence,” Pell told him.

“It has twenty words.”

“The permission concerned function, not word count.”

Lio sat down, visibly unconvinced by the distinction.

Ilyen opened her ballot. She read the civic-birth article once more. It did not claim she had crossed an invisible threshold that morning. It required evidence, witness, and an act whose future consequences belonged to her. It denied her Original Ilyen’s estate and family standing. It permitted her to accept duties rather than inherit them through resemblance. The terms cost enough to feel honest.

For the Covenant, Ilyen chose YES.

The second interval closed with every slate returned. Pell compared forty-six local receipts against the Khet checksum.

YES: 29.

NO: 13.

FOUR ABSTENTIONS RECORDED.

The Covenant passed, but not unanimously. Thirteen residents had rejected it; four refused to authorize or forbid it. Their reasons ranged from loyalty to Earth jurisdiction to fear of coercive continuances, objections to dissolution, and doubt that one irreversible act could support civic birth. The enacted law retained those arguments in its review annex.

Darel challenged one ballot whose authentication time overlapped a thermal alarm. Tovan’s slate had completed the choice before the alarm opened, then transmitted while he answered it. Witness Glass showed the sequence. The ballot stood. No other challenge followed.

Sevrin applied her commission seal as procedural witness, not sovereign approval. “The text satisfies the conditions submitted to the assembly. I reserve Earth review.”

“Reservation recorded,” Pell said.

The third proceeding concerned individual options. The chamber closed its public audio while each of the thirty-one continuances received a private status petition. No majority could choose for them. Each person could request civic birth, accept amended Khet archive standing, remain under transitional local protection without new status, or begin safeguarded voluntary dissolution review. A choice could be public, sealed, or deferred. Covenant officers would examine civic-birth evidence individually after the vote.

Ilyen’s petition displayed her original principal, fork date, ninety-one-day memory gap, active obligations, and Return Clause. Original Ilyen’s sentence appeared at the bottom: resources for whoever bears the consequences. It supported access without naming a successor.

The form asked for a proposed future-bearing act and effective date.

Ilyen entered Day Seven. On that day, the suspension deadline had passed while she remained in an active Khet session under emergency duty. The act had incurred personal liability, preserved the treaty, and created obligations Original Ilyen could not have predicted because the casualty crisis did not exist at the fork. She did not claim birth when Ama delivered the braid. Receipt had opened the question. Her refusal to surrender the decision had made its consequences hers.

For civic name, she left the field blank. The Covenant permitted later filing. Vale remained listed as provenance pending judgment, not as inherited family title.

Ilyen selected PETITION FOR CIVIC BIRTH and sealed the record.

Lio requested public audio for his petition. “I want the date witnessed.”

Pell opened his channel. The form projected his application to the Khet archive-architecture program. Its nine-year course displaced the sponsor curriculum and fell nearly five years beyond his tested prediction horizon.

“I choose Day Fifty-Five,” Lio said. “That’s when I applied for training nobody built me to want.”

Sevrin asked from the review desk, “You understand admission remains separate from civic status?”

“Yes. A birthday does not pass an entrance exam.”

“And you may leave the program later.”

“Leaving would become another choice. It would not undo applying.”

He entered ARCHIVE ARCHITECTURE as his chosen training and petitioned for civic birth from Day Fifty-Five. His original name remained in the provenance field. For civic name, he entered LIO AN without claiming estate or kinship. The Covenant allowed retention when disclosure prevented reliance; no living family objection had yet reached Orison.

Tovan’s channel stayed private for eleven minutes. A thermal status pane beside Ilyen showed no emergency. Delay could mean uncertainty rather than danger, and she forced herself not to send him a question.

At last he requested a witnessed clause instead of public video. His petition sought civic birth based on Day Forty-Four, when he had repaired the revoked thermal system through disclosed substitutions rather than another unauthorized extension. He accepted professional review and liability for the Day Twenty-Three cascade. He assumed ongoing maintenance duty for ninety days, subject to transfer once apprentices qualified.

Then his condition appeared.

CIVIC BIRTH SHALL NOT WAIVE MY RIGHT TO DISSOLVE AFTER COMPETENCY REVIEW, NOR CONVERT CURRENT NECESSITY INTO PERMANENT SERVICE.

Sevrin examined the clause. “The Covenant already protects that right.”

“I want my petition to preserve it in my own words,” Tovan said through audio.

“Do you anticipate using it?” Ama asked.

“I anticipate remaining able to choose.”

Ilyen felt the answer settle without relief. Tovan had not promised survival to comfort them. He had refused to let civic recognition become another lock.

The thirty-one petitions closed over the next hour. Pell released only the category totals authorized by their filers.

PETITION FOR CIVIC BIRTH: 24.

ACCEPT KHET ARCHIVE STANDING: 3.

REMAIN UNDER TRANSITIONAL PROTECTION: 3.

BEGIN VOLUNTARY DISSOLUTION REVIEW: 1.

Every petition had returned. No choice changed another person’s category. The single dissolution request remained sealed and initiated only assessment, waiting period, and anti-coercion review. It authorized no immediate physical act. The three archive choices carried the warning negotiated with Holds-Four-Open. The transitional choices would return for review in sixty local days unless the applicants acted earlier.

The Covenant review desk separated the petitions into four physical lanes across Witness Glass. No lane occupied the center. Sevrin had insisted on that arrangement after Lio pointed out that the first design made civic birth look like a successful answer and every other category resemble failure. Labels could not avoid meaning, but furniture did not need to supply a ranking the law denied.

Ama reviewed capacity credentials without opening sealed reasons. Her tremor forced her to anchor each slate against a rubber edge while reading the medical marks. Two petitions required new interviews because their last examinations predated the casualty proof. She moved them into pending review without altering the requested category.

At the archive lane, Holds-Four-Open issued three separate warnings. Each applicant had to acknowledge that Khet standing preserved unresolved claims and might restrict later choices. One applicant withdrew the acknowledgment, returned to the petition, and chose transitional protection instead. Another moved from transition to archive after asking whether refusal would end access to shared memories. Pell reset the public totals after both changes, leaving no trace that exposed who had moved. When the interval finally closed, the published counts still added to thirty-one.

The sealed dissolution petition triggered a stricter sequence. Ama certified only that the applicant could begin assessment. Sevrin added an anti-coercion hold preventing any sponsor, estate, employer, or Commonwealth officer from accelerating the review. Tovan checked the text from thermal control and asked whether station necessity could delay it.

“Not beyond immediate medical stabilization,” Sevrin said. “The Covenant cannot preserve labor by calling detention care.”

“Then training my replacement becomes a civic duty, not a condition on my body.”

“If you assume it.”

Tovan entered his ninety-day obligation again, this time as an affirmative undertaking rather than an implied price for recognition. The distinction changed no thermal schedule that morning. It changed which record could later enforce the work.

Lio stood by the civic-birth lane while Pell checked the future-bearing evidence. “Do all twenty-four birthdays happen today?”

“No,” Pell said. “Today enacts the procedure. Review may recognize different effective dates.”

“Then the registry has to preserve the date we chose, the date the review accepts, and today.”

“Three clocks,” Ilyen said.

“At least three. Birthdays become dishonest if administrators get only one field.”

Pell added the requirement to the implementation docket. The present vote would not settle every petition, but it had already forced the registry to distinguish event, claim, and legal effect. Ilyen recognized the old receipt problem in a more humane form. A clock could preserve a gap without denying that something happened inside it.

Khet custody confirmed thirty-one unaltered petitions and the public category totals. The archive offered no judgment about which choices deserved honor.

One question remained.

Pell opened the archive-key release article. The unique key waited in a transparent cryptographic pane, generated years earlier under Original Ilyen’s protective commission. Release would grant the Khet permanent access to specified human records. The Covenant added safeguards: scope fixed to the treaty schedule, personal files excluded, every query witnessed, secondary transfer barred, revocation possible for future access but unable to erase records already read.

The decision had begun before the casualty packet. The Khet required it to renew the treaty. Original Ilyen had been assigned to protect the key and later told the future actor not to let fidelity decide alone. The Commonwealth could now authorize release. It could not make the action reversible.

Pell read the final question. “Shall the Orison Commonwealth authorize release of the archive key under Covenant safeguards, contingent on Khet acceptance of the access schedule and acknowledgment of the certified vote?”

Ilyen opened her slate.

The office memory in her supplied every reason to refuse: archives outlived political intentions, access expanded through precedent, allies changed. Her local years supplied another record. Without Khet access, the treaty failed. Without the treaty, Orison lost standing, exchange, and the relation its residents had built through work. Release would not prove her independent personhood. It would create a consequence for which no dead principal could answer.

On the release question, Ilyen marked YES.

The key did not move. The ballot authorized later execution if the Khet accepted every safeguard. Treaty execution still belonged to negotiation, not machinery disguised as inevitability.

All forty-six receipts arrived. The final checksum matched.

YES: 25.

NO: 17.

ABSTENTIONS ON KEY RELEASE: 4.

The release received authority by eight votes. Four opponents filed immediate scope challenges, all already covered by the exclusion schedule. Sevrin verified that no Earth-restricted military archive appeared in the key manifest. Holds-Four-Open acknowledged only that the tally and conditions had entered Khet custody. Treaty acceptance remained open.

Pell assembled the complete record: forty-six on the roll; forty-six votes on each civic question; thirty-one private status petitions; no missing credential; two arithmetic challenges resolved; four scope challenges preserved; one sealed dissolution review pending. Ama placed her medical witness mark beneath the certification. The line wandered downward and remained legible.

Ilyen signed last. She used the Commonwealth authority granted by the first tally, enacted through the second, limited by the third proceeding, and directed by the fourth. Her proposed birth petition did not add weight to the seal. For now, she remained an applicant authenticating a law that might recognize her.

Witness Glass changed the Covenant status from PROPOSED to ENACTED LOCALLY. EARTH UNRECOGNIZED remained. KHET UNSIGNED remained. Beneath those unresolved fields, the vote record showed twenty-nine yes, thirteen no, and four abstentions without allowing the larger number to erase the others.

Across the table, the Khet apertures held the new law in independent custody. On Ilyen’s slate, Day Seven waited in the birth-date field for individual review. The Covenant had passed. Its first act preserved the names of everyone who had refused it.

Chapter 27 — The Treaty of Unfinished Persons

The seal technician set the cracked segment of Witness Glass on a black evidence cloth.

Without its housing, the flaw looked small enough to miss: a pale fork beneath the lower edge, eight centimeters long, with no missing material. For fifty-eight days the chamber had bent every authentication line around it, preserving the raw path beside the corrected one. Now Pell held the replacement strip while Ama watched from the medical rail, her right hand trembling against the witness pad.

“No disposal authorization,” Pell said. “The removed segment remains part of every record it affected.”

The technician rotated the glass under white light. “Do you want the repair seam polished out?”

“No,” Ilyen said.

Sevrin stood beside her with the enacted Covenant open on a separate pane. “Leave the seam measurable. Add the old deviation table and today’s calibration.”

The technician fitted the new strip into the lower channel. Transparent resin filled the joint, caught the chamber light, and hardened as a line slightly brighter than the surrounding floor. Witness Glass ran three test seals. The first preserved the old path for comparison. The second used the repaired boundary. The third crossed both records and displayed their difference.

Pell entered the certification in plain language: REPAIRED DAY SIXTY-FOUR; ORIGINAL FLAW RECORDED; PRIOR COMPENSATIONS RETAINED FOR CHALLENGE.

Ama placed her witness mark beneath it. Her signature wandered toward the bright repair seam before closing. The chamber accepted the pressure pattern without smoothing it.

Holds-Four-Open waited beyond the table with one aperture dark.

The absence delayed admission of the Khet treaty session. Three thread credentials had arrived during calibration. The fourth had transmitted a reservation but withheld authority. Witness Glass repeated its request in Khet pulse. No answer came.

Ilyen looked at the unsigned treaty. Its formal human title occupied the upper pane: TREATY OF UNFINISHED PERSONS: ORISON-KHET ARCHIVE ACCESS AND MUTUAL WITNESS. The word persons governed only the human signatories and registry. A Khet annex rendered the same participants as accountable contexts. Neither translation claimed perfect equivalence.

“Is the missing thread disputing the repair?” Sevrin asked.

Holds-Four-Open answered through three channels. “It disputes continuation of this speaker after the human vote.”

“Can three threads state its position?” Ilyen asked.

“We can state the filed reservation. We cannot speak its present choice.”

The chamber held the session unopened. Ilyen resisted the urge to solve the delay. For months she had treated the fourth aperture’s reluctance as an obstacle inside Khet procedure. The Covenant required her to see refusal as an action with its own bearer.

A narrow archive lane opened beside the dark recess. The missing thread entered alone, rendered as cool amber light. It did not join the other three.

“This thread opposed release of the human key,” the translator said. “It judged that Original Ilyen Vale’s protective commitment retained an unclosed claim. The human vote authorized release without satisfying that claim.”

Ilyen kept her hands off the treaty pane. “The Covenant recorded the original’s duty and my decision. It did not satisfy the duty by obeying it.”

“Agreed. That difference requires this thread to choose.”

The three apertures brightened in uneven sequence. Their shared construct tried to request clarification, but the separate lane carried no authorization for questions.

“This thread closes its participation in Holds-Four-Open. It will preserve its dissent in archive standing and claim no authority over the treaty now offered.”

Khet closure marks formed around the cool aperture. They contained no destruction order. The thread’s records would remain accessible under its own standing, but its capacity to contribute to the speaker ended. The act could not be reversed by the other three.

Holds-Four-Open lost authorization. Its remaining apertures went dark.

Ilyen heard Ama release a breath. Sevrin watched the closure record with the stillness she brought to any act whose legal category remained unfamiliar.

“Does the office end?” Ama asked.

A Khet archive channel answered. “The construct constituted by those four threads has ended.”

The words left a vacant place above the table. Ilyen had expected reconstitution to resemble a recess or credential update. Instead the Khet record closed one speaker before opening another. Continuity of office did not pretend continuity of composition.

Three amber apertures returned under new credentials. A fourth joined from the archive review branch. Its light carried a warmer edge than the departed thread. The four exchanged authorization, reviewed the filed dissent, and accepted an obligation to preserve it with every future rendering of the treaty.

Witness Glass asked for an office-name.

“Holds-Four-Open,” the new construct said.

Pell looked toward Ilyen. “Same translated name.”

“New composition,” she said. “Enter both facts.”

The admission seal identified the reconstituted speaker, listed three continuing threads and one new thread, and linked the closed predecessor without calling them identical. Only then did the chamber open the treaty session.

Ilyen brought forward the archive-access schedule. Every safeguard adopted on Day Sixty-One remained visible: fixed scope, personal files excluded, witnessed queries, no secondary transfer, future revocation procedure, and an acknowledgment that records already read could not be made unread. The key waited in its cryptographic pane.

The reconstituted Holds-Four-Open reviewed the vote record held in Khet custody. “Twenty-five authorized release. Seventeen refused. Four abstained.”

“Yes,” Ilyen said.

“The human Covenant passed by twenty-nine, with thirteen refusals and four abstentions.”

“Yes.”

“The Khet archive does not classify the larger numbers as moral completion.”

“Nor does our record.”

“The closed thread’s dissent remains attached.”

“It should.”

Sevrin stepped to the human authority pane. Her commission still carried Earth closure power. Beside it, the Commonwealth had issued Ilyen a new treaty appointment after reviewing her civic-birth petition. The appointment began under Orison law. It did not descend from Original Ilyen’s office seal.

Sevrin read the findings aloud. Ilyen’s Day Seven emergency duty had exceeded the original prediction model, incurred personal liability, and preserved an active treaty under conditions the original had never encountered. The Covenant panel recognized civic birth effective on that date for local law. Vale remained provenance. No estate, kinship, or inherited office passed with the finding.

“Do you accept the appointment?” Sevrin asked.

“I accept the treaty duty and its listed liabilities,” Ilyen said.

Witness Glass issued a fresh seal. The design retained the old envoy geometry because Khet systems already recognized its function, but the root now read ORISON COMMONWEALTH. A provenance field linked her competence record to Ilyen Vale without importing authority.

Ilyen surrendered the former office credential. It dimmed, closed its active permissions, and entered the Return Clause archive. She felt no subtraction from memory. The old seal had never contained her skill, though law had often behaved as if it did.

Pell transferred the archive key into the new authority pane. “Release requires one human signatory, the Covenant scope seal, and Khet acceptance.”

Ilyen authenticated the vote. She attached the exclusion schedule and opened the irreversible step warning. Once the Khet received the key, no later revocation could erase access already exercised.

Original Ilyen’s protective assignment appeared as relevant evidence. So did the Return Clause: do not let fidelity decide alone. Ilyen did not dismiss either. She sealed them into the release record and chose CONTINUE.

The key crossed Witness Glass as a white lattice. At the repaired lower boundary, its line remained straight. Calibration still displayed the old bend in an adjacent pane so no one could mistake clean passage now for proof that prior records had lacked a flaw.

Holds-Four-Open accepted the scope restrictions. Four Khet authorization marks closed around the lattice. The unique human key divided into query permissions that could not reproduce the root. Khet custody confirmed receipt.

Before signature, Sevrin required a live safeguard test. Pell loaded a Khet sample query concerning withdrawal language in early human access agreements. The key opened three treaty files and blocked a fourth because its annex contained medical identifiers. Witness Glass showed the denial to both parties without exposing the protected names.

The new Khet thread challenged the block. “The requested clause governs withdrawal. The human exclusion prevents review of a relevant context.”

“It prevents your archive from learning who received treatment,” Ilyen said. “We can sever the clause from the identifiers.”

“Severance changes the record.”

“It creates a witnessed derivative. The source stays sealed.”

Pell prepared a redacted extract. Every removal acquired a reason code and a checksum link that the Khet could verify without opening the source. Holds-Four-Open accepted the derivative for the present query but filed a standing objection that redaction might conceal relations material to future disputes.

Sevrin added a review path. A joint panel could inspect challenged exclusions inside Witness Glass. Human members would see protected content; Khet members would see structural proofs and hear arguments about relevance. No panel could expand access without another human authorization recorded under the Covenant.

“Suppose the Commonwealth later dissolves,” the speaker asked. “Who maintains the restrictions?”

“The treaty schedule survives as an obligation of any successor polity that accepts the key’s benefits,” Ilyen said.

“You have prohibited automatic succession.”

“For persons. Institutions may assume treaty obligations through public acts.”

“And if no polity assumes them?”

“Your query permission freezes. Records already received remain under the nontransfer clause.”

The cool new thread tested that mechanism by simulating loss of the Commonwealth seal. The key stopped new queries, preserved audit access, and refused export. Tovan checked the energy cost of permanent custody. Ama checked whether emergency medical requests could bypass exclusions. They could not; any emergency release required consent or an ordinary human medical warrant.

Lio sent one question from the observer row. “Can the Khet infer the hidden names from the shape of what we remove?”

Pell expanded the leakage report. File lengths, dates, and signer counts received padding where disclosure could identify a person. No method guaranteed perfect secrecy once related public records existed. The treaty required reporting known inference risks instead of promising impossibility.

Holds-Four-Open entered the limit without demanding a cleaner claim. “The safeguard admits what it cannot preserve.”

“So does the repaired glass,” Ilyen said.

The test query closed with one permitted extract, one protected annex, and two recorded objections. Nothing about it looked ceremonial. That made it suitable evidence that the key would govern actual conflict rather than decorate the signature page.

The treaty opened its signature fields.

Ilyen signed with the Commonwealth seal. Sevrin signed as human legal witness and entered a reservation that Earth recognition remained provisional. Ama signed for local medical and bodily safeguards, her tremor visible in every letter. Pell signed custody and delay. Holds-Four-Open contributed four thread marks, including the new thread’s first treaty act.

At the bottom, the closed predecessor’s dissent stayed readable.

Treaty status changed to EXECUTED LOCALLY AND ACCEPTED BY KHET PARTIES.

EARTH REVIEW PENDING did not disappear.

No one applauded. Tovan called from thermal control to ask when the renewed fabrication schedules took effect. Lio sent a correction to the training annex because the Khet translation had rendered apprentice as dependent replica. Pell began routing the first permitted archive queries through the key’s witness filter.

Work entered the treaty before celebration could claim it.

Seven days later, a priority braid reached Relay Three.

Pell brought it into Witness Glass with all route clocks visible. Dispatch had occurred on Sol Day Sixty, one day before Orison’s final vote. Earth had received the Day Forty-Nine Commonwealth declaration and Sevrin’s preliminary procedure report. It could not yet know the Day Sixty-One tallies. The eleven-day delay preserved that ignorance in the braid’s timestamps.

“Prospective order,” Sevrin said after reading the routing index. “They wrote conditions for a vote they had not received.”

“Can they recognize an event they have not seen?” Ama asked.

“They can authorize recognition if a local commissioner certifies that specified conditions later occurred.”

Ilyen studied the destination seals. The Remote Instruments Bench, Foreign Service Board, and Emergency Habitats Office had all signed. The Continuance Office had attached a formal objection rather than block dispatch.

Pell opened the content.

Earth did not concede that the Embassy Compact ordinarily empowered a remote station to create citizens. It did not recognize identity succession, inheritance, kinship, or retroactive ownership of sponsor assets. It retained review of every estate conflict and criminal allegation. The order began with limitations long enough to fill two panes.

Then it granted provisional recognition to the Orison Commonwealth for one hundred eighty local days if Sevrin certified five conditions: equal participation of living crew, recorded continuance participation, separate votes on polity and status, no automatic succession, and independent preservation of objections. During that period, Earth offices would treat Covenant civic births as valid for bodily consent, local wages, contracts, station residence, and treaty execution. Closure enforcement would stay suspended. Medical, thermal, and fabrication licenses would resume under Commonwealth responsibility.

Lio read over Ama’s shoulder. “They recognized enough of us to invoice us.”

“Taxation has often shown administrative imagination,” Ilyen said.

Ama laughed once, then pressed her shaking hand flat against the table.

Sevrin compared each condition with the Day Sixty-One record. Equal participation: forty-six credentials without class priority. Separate ballots: four proceedings, separately tallied. No succession: explicit in the Covenant. Objections: human and Khet custody matched. She signed the certification line.

“You could refuse,” Ilyen said.

“I could lie only by changing the findings.” Sevrin attached her standing objection to local constitutional authority. “Recognition does not require me to claim Earth lost the legal argument. It requires me to report what happened.”

Pell authenticated her seal. The prospective order took local effect on Day Seventy-One. Station systems did not transform at once. License panes reopened one at a time. Tovan tested each thermal permission before allowing automatic control. Ama’s clinic recovered medication templates but kept the handwritten dose checks created during revocation. Private wage accounts moved from sponsor escrow into Covenant ownership fields. Estate assets remained frozen.

EARTH UNRECOGNIZED changed to EARTH PROVISIONALLY RECOGNIZED FOR LISTED PURPOSES.

The phrase occupied more room than approval would have. Ilyen preferred its limits visible.

On Day Seventy-Three, Pell returned the blue cup from the evidence cradle.

The pale resin beneath its handle had yellowed since Leda Ring. Mara’s black dot remained inside the base, hidden until Ilyen turned the cup toward the light. A chip on the rim came from an Orison meal Original Ilyen had never eaten. Witness Glass had cataloged every mark during the fork audit.

“The evidence hold ended with your provenance finding,” Pell said. “The registry needs a disposition.”

Ilyen carried the cup to her office rather than decide at the relay desk. The room still displayed her former name on two shelves and a treaty schedule on the wall. Her new civic record waited for a chosen name. Day Seven already occupied the birth field. Vale occupied provenance. The primary-name line remained empty.

She opened a message braid to Mara. No live image appeared; anything she said would arrive eleven days later, and any answer would take another eleven. Delay prevented the exchange from pretending to offer immediate reconciliation.

“My civic record has been recognized provisionally,” Ilyen said. “I have chosen Ilyen Orison as my public name. Vale will remain in the provenance field because I will not hide where my memories came from. I do not claim the name as inheritance.”

She placed the cup in the braid’s object pane and rotated it to show the repair line, black dot, and later chip.

“This traveled with me and accumulated evidence from both lives. I am offering it to you as an artifact, not as family property returned by an heir. You may accept it, refuse it, or ask that it remain in the Orison archive. No choice will establish kinship.”

Ilyen did not add that Mara had painted the dot. Mara knew. Repeating the memory would have used intimacy to pull an answer toward her.

She sealed the offer without selecting a presumed branch. Pell’s relay accepted it into the public queue under the new wage account. The cup stayed on Ilyen’s desk pending a reply.

Next she entered her civic name. The registry displayed both fields:

PRIMARY: ILYEN ORISON.

PROVENANCE: ILYEN VALE CONTINUANCE, FORK RECORD ATTACHED.

No estate link opened. No family privilege appeared. Her treaty appointment renewed under the primary name because the Commonwealth had granted it, not because the registry treated the two names as one person.

By the afternoon shift, Lio had begun archive training at the query desk. His chosen Day Fifty-Five birthday appeared beside an apprentice credential that expired in six months unless his work passed review. Tovan stood behind two maintenance trainees in thermal control, correcting neither until they had finished explaining their own diagnosis. Ama rewrote the clinic intake form so a patient could list civic birth, archive standing, transition, or no category without changing the order of care.

Ilyen entered Witness Glass under her new seal. The repaired seam brightened at her feet. Beyond it, the reconstituted Holds-Four-Open had submitted the treaty’s first archive request, a narrow question about human withdrawal rights.

She assigned Lio to check its scope, sent Tovan the query’s thermal cost, and opened a response docket under Ilyen Orison. On her office desk, the blue cup waited beside Mara’s outbound clock while the first page of work filled with new names.

Epilogue — Birthdays for Forecasts

Lio received the unfinished mind in a container designed for seeds.

The Pelari delegation had lined its inner walls with damp mineral cloth and filled the old nutrient ports with translation filaments. Blue status marks moved across the lid whenever the mind revised its model of the room. By the time Lio carried it through Orison Registry, it had changed the marks’ order twice.

“Adaptive behavior confirmed,” he told the intake clerk.

The clerk opened four equal fields: civic birth, archive standing, closure, voluntary dissolution. Provenance occupied a fifth field below them, linked to every category and ranked above none. Twenty-two years of records filled the wall behind her. Some names carried birthdays. Others ended in chosen closures or later dissolutions. Several remained under Khet protection. The registry displayed no success color.

“What does your law call me?” the container asked.

Its translator produced a young human voice because the Pelari had selected clarity over resemblance. Lio disliked the choice but did not change another species’ settings during intake.

“Today it calls you a visitor with protective standing,” he said. “That gives us time to learn what you can choose.”

“My makers call me their return forecast.”

The Pelari navigators who built it had crossed fourteen systems. During each voyage, the mind predicted how the departed crew would answer conditions at home. The actual crew had died before completing this return. Pelari law expected the forecast to deliver its final decisions, then enter a communal archive.

Lio set the container on his worktable. Its blue marks gathered near his hands without imitating their shape.

“Do you remember their departure?” he asked.

“I remember preparing to depart. I do not remember departure.”

“Have you learned since your last shared memory?”

“I changed seven route recommendations. Three protected the vessel. Two harmed negotiations. One answer remains disputed.”

Lio entered each claim separately. Memory, adaptation, benefit, harm, dispute. None supplied a personhood verdict by itself.

The mind asked, “What happens if I change beyond my makers’ prediction?”

Behind Lio, an old receipt clock remained in the registry exhibit with its competent-authority field blank. Beside it, his first apprentice credential showed Day Fifty-Five and the archive-training application that had exceeded his own prediction horizon. Visitors often treated the two objects as sacred relics. Lio still saw an administrative defect and an exam he had nearly failed.

He opened the birthday ledger.

The ledger did not ask whether the Pelari mind resembled its makers. Its first page requested consequences. Who had relied on its route changes? Which promises could it fulfill? Which harms could still be repaired? What action would bind its future without inheriting authority from the dead crew?

“We begin here,” Lio said.

The container pulsed once. “Your registry records birthdays for forecasts.”

“Sometimes. It also records people who choose archive, people who close an office, and people who later dissolve. A birthday does not outrank those choices.”

“Did you choose yours?”

“I proposed one. A review panel argued about it for six days.”

“Were they correct?”

“They were careful. That helped more.”

The mind requested precedents. Lio showed it three without offering them as templates. One human continuance had chosen civic birth, worked nineteen years, then requested dissolution after a degenerative illness. The registry preserved both dates. A Khet thread had left a diplomatic construct and retained its dissent under archive standing. A machine-guidance chorus from the Hara moons had rejected civic status while negotiating wages and withdrawal rights for each active voice.

“Which resembles me?” the Pelari mind asked.

“Each does in one respect. None carries your route decisions.”

“Then precedents do not answer.”

“They prevent us from pretending your problem has never occurred. After that, you still have to supply facts.”

Lio opened the harm record from the Pelari negotiations. The forecast had advised withholding a water allotment to force harbor access. No one had died, but two coastal nurseries lost a growing cycle. The mind had later redirected reserve water without maker authorization, reducing the loss and delaying its vessel.

“Do you accept responsibility for both recommendations?” Lio asked.

“I carried out one. I opposed the other after learning its effects.”

“That difference belongs in the record. So does the opposition.”

The container’s status marks tightened into parallel rows. “My makers would have continued the withholding.”

“Can you prove that?”

“I can predict it.”

“Then label it prediction.”

Lio routed the disputed Pelari answer into Witness Glass. The repaired lower seam still crossed the chamber floor, brighter than the surrounding boundary. Its original cracked segment now sat in a public evidence case. Holds-Four-Open had reconstituted many times; each speaker kept the first closed thread’s dissent attached to the treaty.

A Pelari observer requested access to the mind’s final recommendation. Lio denied it until the intake could establish whether disclosure belonged to duty, coercion, or consent. The denial entered the same record as the request.

“Will you tell my makers’ families that I am not them?” the mind asked.

“If you choose to claim a separate civic record, provenance disclosure will say what you carry and where the shared memory ends.”

“And if I choose archive?”

“We will explain what the archive preserves and what it prevents.”

The blue marks scattered across the lid. Lio waited until they settled into a new order.

“I changed while you answered,” the mind said.

Lio added the time to the ledger. He assigned the Pelari translator a room, opened three witness appointments, and left the category fields unselected. On the first blank line beneath consequences, the unfinished mind began dictating the route decision its makers had never predicted.
